THE NOTARY PUBLIC Chapter 1. 

DEFINITION 

Q. What is a notary public?

A. (1) A commonly accepted definition is in these words: "The notary public is a public officer whose duty it is to attest the genuineness of any deeds or writings in order to render them available as evidence of the facts therein stated." (2) The office is known to the law of nations, and the notary's official acts receive credence not only in his own country but in all others in which they are used as evidence. The office is one indispensable to the law merchant. (3) The office of notary public is a public office, civil in its nature, not military. (4) Public office is a public agency, or trust. One, therefore, who holds a public office is an agent, or trustee, of the public. Public offices are created for the purpose of effecting the end for which government has been instituted, which is the common good, and not for the mere profit, honor or private interest of any one man, family, or class of men. The term "public office" embraces the idea of these things, namely, tenure, duration, fees, emoluments, powers and duties. All these taken together constitute public office. The word "officer" is one inseparably connected with an office. It follows that one and only one-who holds a public office is a public officer.

ORIGIN AND HISTORY 

Q. When and where did the office of notary originate?

A. (1) The office was probably of Roman origin. In any country whose jurisprudence is founded on the Roman law the office of notary public is very important. In countries whose basic law is the common law of England, the duties are somewhat limited, but the importance of the office is everywhere recognized. In our 

States, perhaps the laws of Louisiana delegate to the

Notary Public duties and powers of wider scope and greater importance than those of any other State or Territory. (2) In earlier centuries the notary was a scrivener, or public clerk, or writer, and was not classed as an officer of the law. He sat in the market place and read and wrote letters for persons seeking that service. It is said of 

Charlemagne that in the year 805 A. D. he required every bishop, abbot and count of his court or jurisdiction to have a notary attached to his staff, and that he invested the said notary's acts with public authority. (3) As early as the seventeenth century we find authoritative expressions in English legal literature indicating that in those days the notary had come to be recognized as a public officer. In all American references the notary is treated as a public officer, so recognized by constitutions, statutes, court decisions and executive rulings. 

ELECTIVE OR APPOINTIVE 

Q. Is the office of notary public an elective one, or appointive?

A. The office is apparently in all cases filled by appointment. 

The extensive research necessary to compilation of this volume has not brought to light any instance where the office of notary public is originally elective. In many of the States, one who holds an elective office may be ex-officio notary public, that is to say, notary public by virtue of office, best known example of this arrangement being the office of justice of the peace. In a sense the notary public office is thereby made elective, but the election is for the other office and not for that of notary public.

NATURE OF Duties 

Q. What is the nature of a notary's official duties?

A. (1) The official duties of a notary public are said to be of ministerial or clerical nature; that is to say, that they do not involve the element of judicial discretion. (2) It is true that there are certain features of a notary's work which have more than once been reviewed and discussed under the issue of judicial discretion. In those states where part of a notary's official duty is to take and certify the acknowledgment of a married woman,

separate and apart from her husband, it is sometimes contended that his act of taking and certifying such acknowledgment is one judicial in its nature. Whether or not this is a correct view is not important for our purposes here. The appellate courts have not agreed about it, and it is a problem for the lawyers, not the notaries. But it does seem not improper to point out that the factor of judicial discretion in such a notarial certificate is not obvious, hardly apparent. He, the notary, has no discretion in the performance of such a duty.. 

There are certain questions which he is required to ask the acknowledger and which she is expected to answer. 

If she answers one way, he should certify; if she answers another way, or if she refuses to answer, he should not certify. Where, in such a situation, has he any judicial discretion? Again, it has happened more than once that a notary when taking a deposition has felt called upon. to inflict punishment for contempt upon a recalcitrant witness. Now the power to punish for contempt is one confided to judicial discretion-to courts-and in some jurisdictions the attempt of a notary to exercise such a power has been challenged. In passing it may be stated that all of the "usual powers" of a notary, as that phrase will be often used in these pages, are of clerical or ministerial nature, not judicial.

IMPORTANCE OF THE OFFICE 

Q. Is the office of notary public an important one?

A. Yes, highly important; in fact, indispensable to the carrying on of modern business. The functions of the Notary's office are called into action throughout the country far more often than those of any other public officer. Clearly enough if we did not have the office of notary public, we'd have to create it or something like it to take its place. 

WHAT KIND OF OFFICER 

Q. What kind of public officer is the notary public?

A. It is correct to state broadly that the notary public is a state officer. But this statement falls short of the accuracy and comprehensiveness essential to correct definition. In some of those jurisdictions which are under direct Federal authority, for example, the District of Columbia, the notary is an officer of the United States. In Alaska, the postmaster-ap-

pointed by the Federal Government-who acts as a Notary, is a Federal officer, though the Alaskan notary-not a postmaster-who holds his commission from the Governor of the Territory, is a territorial officer. In some jurisdictions the notary is appointed by local authority. Thus, in the state of 

Georgia, certain of the circuit judges have authority to appoint to the office of notary. In all but one of the forty-eight 

States there are notaries who are state officers because they hold commission direct from the state government, Vermont being the exception.

NOT AN ATTORNEY AT LAW 

Q. Does the fact of being a notary make one an attorney at law?

A. (1) Definitely it does not. True, one who is a lawyer may also be a notary but the appointment of a non-lawyer to the office of notary public does not vest such appointee with legal knowledge, or with the right to practice law. (2) In nearly all of the states laws have been enacted defining "practice of law," and declaring who may follow that honored calling. Careful research has not revealed to this writer any instance in which the practice of notarial duty is defined by law practice, or in which appointment to the office of notary public has clothed the appointee with right or license to engage in law practice or to charge for legal services. If the appointee has such right or license it grows out of other qualifications than the notary public appointment. (3) The practice of law is not in any way a part of a notary public's official duties. See the chapters on FEES 

and EARNINGS, and WRITING PAPERS. 

NOTARY'S PRINCIPAL DUTY 

Q. What is a notary's principal official duty?

A. The notary's principal official duty is to "authenticate" a written instrument by attaching thereto his official certificate. 

As will presently appear in detail there are duties and acts which he performs in addition to attaching his official certificate, but every act done by him in his official capacity, and every step taken, becomes effective only if and when it shall have culminated in his official certificate. 

AUTHENTICATION 

Q. What is meant by authentication of a written instrument?

A. Elsewhere in these pages will be found further discussion of the subject of authentication-authentication of papers and authentication of the notary himself. For the present it may suffice to state that to authenticate a written instrument is to properly prove and establish it, upon the basis of competent legal authority, as being the very paper which it purports to be. 

PURPOSE OF AUTHENTICATION 

Q. Why should a paper be authenticated?

A. The purpose of authentication is to render the instrument available as evidence of its own contents, without further proof of its execution, and/or to entitle it to public record. 

VERIFICATION AND IDENTIFICATION 

Q. How does the notary's authentication of a paper achieve such purposes?

A. (1) It verifies the signature attached to the paper, and identifies the signer. (2) As often stated, all notarial work is directed toward accomplishment of the authentication of a written instrument. (3) Any analysis of authentication, or of a notary's official duties, must therefore finally get around to the task of proving that some person or persons did, or did not, a certain thing, such, for example, as selling a tract of land, or mortgaging a herd of cattle, or contracting to build a house, etc. In the course of time, dating back to the beginning of human contracts, no doubt men began to sell land and trade horses and otherwise transfer property rights. At first this would be done by word of mouth. Thus, A sold B a farm. Sooner or later there would be a dispute whether such sale was made or not. Either A or B, or both, would call a witness or witnesses, because it was for some reason believed that if two or more persons testified to a thing it was more probably true than if only one so testified. 

NOTARY, A BONDED WITNESS 

(4) Then men learned to write, and it was found that cold letters remain after the fragile structures of memory have failed. So transfers began to be made in writing.

But it would inevitably happen that A or B or C would sign a paper and thereafter say he did not sign it; and that D or E or F would learn to forge another's name.

So that, notwithstanding it had been at first thought that a written transfer would forever settle all disputes, it was found that a writing was only helpful, not always conclusive. So someone hit upon the idea of having the signature witnessed. From this it was but another step to having as such witness an officer under bond. The notary is that officer, that witness, and his authentication certificate means that he guarantees upon his oath as an officer, and subject to suit upon his bond, that the paper authenticated is indeed the very paper that it purports to be, insofar as the signer and the signature are concerned. (5) It would also happen that in course of time dishonest persons would try to practice fraud and deceit for their own profit by falsely pretending to be notary, and by giving false certificates. So it was found necessary to provide for authentication of the notary himself, of which more anon.

USUAL POWERS 

Q. As means of accomplishing authentication of written instruments, what are the usual powers of a notary public? 

A. (1) By the expression "usual powers" of a notary public, we mean the power and duty to perform those certain acts most commonly entrusted to him in the different jurisdictions. These are: a. To take and certify the acknowledgments of the signers of deeds, contracts, mortgages and other like instruments; b. To administer an oath and certify it as an affidavit; c. To take the depositions of witnesses; d. To protest commercial paper; e. To make, certify and furnish copies of his own official records. 

OTHER POWERS AND DUTIES 

(2) In addition to the foregoing usual powers, the legislature of a state may impose additional duties and grant additional powers. Thus in Louisiana a notary may hold family meetings, under the Napoleonic Code; in 

Florida he may solemnize marriages; in Virginia he is a conservator of the peace, etc.

DIFFERENT KINDS OF NOTARIES 

Q. Are there, and how many, different kinds of notaries?

A. The notary's official duties are so nearly the same in all jurisdictions that it may not be amiss to state that all notaries are of one class. This is not quite true, however, as regards the source of appointment, the term of office, the official bond, the jurisdiction and some other things. Most notaries are appointed by some state officer, as will be noted in the pages following. Many hold the office ex officio. Some are state notaries, meaning that they have statewide jurisdiction, while others function in only one or more counties, or precincts or districts.

DEPUTY NOTARY 

Q. Is there such a thing as a deputy notary public?

A. A notary's duties are personal and the general rule in nearly all the states is that he must perform those duties himself, not delegate their performance to any other person. A state legislature has the power to provide for a deputy notary within its own borders. In England much notarial work is left to deputies. In some American jurisdictions, Louisiana for example, a deputy or clerk may attend to some of the notary's work, and there are state statutes supporting that custom, but the general rule is that only the notary himself may perform his official duties. A notary may act by or through a deputy only in a state or territory where he is by law expressly authorized so to do.

NOTARY PARTNERSHIP 

Q. Is there such a thing as a notarial partnership?

A. Notarial duties being purely personal, it would seem that they must be performed by one individual; that a notarial partnership would therefore be an impossible thing. No instance of a notarial partnership has come to the attention of this writer.

NOTARY CORPORATION 

Q. Is there a notarial corporation?

A. This writer has found no instance of a notarial corporation, does not believe any has been authorized by law.

WHOSE AGENT 

Q. Is a notary public the agent of any one for whom he does notarial work?

A. Officially a notary public is the agent of the public only; he is not and can not be officially, as a notary public, the agent of any individual.

HOLDING OTHER OFFICE 

Q. May a notary public hold any other office while notary?

A. (1) In the case of notary ex officio, obviously yes. In nearly all the states it is expressly provided that a notary may hold and exercise the duties of other offices. In Pennsylvania one may not be a notary and at the same time hold any judicial office. The statute in that state is as follows: "No person being a stockholder, director, cashier, teller, clerk or other officer in any bank or banking institution, or in the employment thereof, or holding or exercising any judicial office in this commonwealth, or any office of trust or profit under the constitution or laws of the United States, shall at the same time hold, exercise or enjoy the office of notary public; and no person shall be appointed to said office in any city or county who shall not have resided therein one year immediately preceding his appointment." 

An Indiana statute upon this topic reads as follows: "No person, being an officer in any bank, corporation or association possessed of any banking powers, shall act as a notary public in the business of such bank, corporation or association. No person holding lucrative office or appointment under the United States or under this state, and prohibited by the constitution of this state from holding more than one (1) such lucrative office shall serve as a notary public, and his acceptance of any such office shall vacate his appointment as such notary; but this provision shall not apply to any person holding any lucrative office or appontment under any city or school or town of this state. No person, being a public official, or a deputy or appointee acting for or serving under the same, shall make any charge for service as a notary public in connection with any official business of such office, or of any other office in the governmental unit in which such persons are serving, 1

unless such charges are specifically authorized by some statute other than the statute fixing generally the fees and charges of notaries public."

EX-OFFICIO NOTARY PUBLIC 

Q. What is a notary public ex-officio?

A. (1) One who is a notary by virtue of holding another office. 

As noted in the preceding paragraph there are lawsin nearly all the States, Territories, Districts-providing that one may be a notary ex-officio, that is to say, by virtue of holding some other office. (2) Attention is directed especially to provisions of Federal laws shown in following pages, clothing certain Government officers and employees with notarial powers, in connection with Army, Navy, Marine, Coast Guard, and civilian administrations. 

DE FACTO NOTARY PUBLIC Q. What is meant by the expression DE FACTO NOTARY 

PUBLIC?

A. An officer "de facto" is one who without actually being qualified to act, nevertheless performs and discharges the duties of a public office. A notary may be a notary de facto, and his official acts may be upheld, when he is actually without legal notarial standing. This topic is more fully examined in the chapter on AUTHENTICATION. 

NUMBER 

Q. How many notaries are there in the United States?

A. Probably about 500,000. Exact figures are not easily obtained. 

Thus, Illinois has about 90,000; Texas about 75,000; New 

York more than 80,000; Iowa more than 15,000; Missouri about 25,000, etc.

MAIN OCCUPATIONS 

Q. The office of notary public being everywhere a “sideline” rather than a principal occupation, what classes of persons are found in the notary's ranks?

A. Practically every honorable profession or calling. Abstracters, bankers, clerks, doctors, engineers, editors, farmers, book-

keepers, funeral directors, merchants, stenographers, lawyers -one can hardly name an occupation that does not have notaries in its lists. Probably more lawyers are notaries than any other class though the bank employees and office secretaries run them close for second place. 

THE NAME of a Woman NOTARY 

Q. If a single woman is appointed notary, how should she sign her official certificates?

A. A good way for anyone to sign is by first name in full, middle initial and surname in full. This rule is as good for a single woman as for anyone else. 

IF MARRIED

Q. If a married woman is appointed notary how should she sign her official certificates?

A. She should see that her commission is issued to her in her right name, that her official bond is signed by her right name and that all of her official certificates are signed in her right name. Her right name is her first maiden name, middle name or initial and her husband's surname. If she is Thelma Davis and marries William J. Butler, she should not sign as Mrs. 

William J. Butler but as Thelma Butler, or Mrs. Thelma 

Butler or as Mrs. Thelma D. Butler-always using her own first name and her husband's surname, but not her husband's first name or initial. 

Q. If a woman while a notary changes her name, what should she do about it, and how should she sign thereafter? 

A. The laws of the states are not all alike upon this point. In some of the states she should continue to sign with the name shown in her commission; in others she is required to report the change. Thus in New Jersey we find the following statute: "After the marriage of a woman notary public, and before she signs her name to any document which she is authorized or required to sign as notary public, she shall make and sign a statement in writing addressed to the governor, of the following tenor and effect: I, Mary

Doe, a notary public of the state of New Jersey, do hereby certify that I did on the............day of………………………….., 194.... intermarry with Richard Roe, my present hus-

band, and this statement is made to the end that I may continue to act as such notary public by the addition of my said husband's surname after a hyphen at the end of the name in and by which I was appointed and commissioned such notary public, according to the statute in such case made and provided, which statement shall be verified by both names and shall be verified by oath of the notary public and by her new name that the facts therein stated are true. The statement must be filed in the office of the governor and in the office of the county clerk."

A woman notary who changes her name while a notary, should consult a competent attorney in her own state as to the course that she should pursue with reference to her notarial certificates and duties.

ELIGIBILITY 

Q. What qualifications or conditions are necessary to render one eligible for appointment as notary public? A. (1) As upon so many other points, the laws of the states vary widely in eligibility requirements. (2) Formerly women were not permitted to act as notaries, but this inhibition seems to have been withdrawn practically everywhere, and it is probably a safe bet now that there are more women than men notaries. (3) In some of the states a person under the age of 21 is eligible. This rule is justified, or sought to be justified, by the fact that notarial duties are ministerial only. In nearly all jurisdictions, though, it is required that one to be a notary must be of legal age, that is to say, 21 or older. (4) In some, but not all, states, or jurisdictions, there are educational requirements, but these are generally vague and of very liberal interpretation. (5) In some jurisdictions, states, no eligibiliy requirement exists as to character; in others "good character" or "good moral character" is essential. (6) Appointment of an alien to be notary public has been upheld as lawful, but in most states only citizens are appointed. (7) A general requirement is that the appointee must be a resident of his jurisdictional area. See the following state-by-state analysis.

DISQUALIFICATIONS 

Q. May a notary lawfully certify a written instrument involving or relating to a transaction or matter in which he is personally interested?

A. (1) The general rule is that he should not officially act on matters in which he has a personal interest. This rule, though is subject to exceptions. (2) The rule is not identical as to affidavits and acknowledgments. (See chapters on those subjects.) (3) Legislatures and courts everywhere have recognized and emphasized that if a certificate of acknowledgment is to have due weight, the officer giving such certificate must be disinterested and impartial. It is therefore well established that a notary public should not certify an acknowledgment where he is, himself, a party to the transaction. Several of the states, for example, Kansas, Maryland, Michigan, New Mexico, and others, have enacted a statute relating to this subject in words and terms substantially as follows: "It shall be lawful for any notary public who is a stockholder, director, officer, or employee of a bank or other corporation to take the acknowledgment of any party to any written instrument executed to or by such corporation, or to administer an oath to any other stockholder, director, officer, employee or agent of such corporation, or to protest for non-acceptance or non-payment, bills of exchange, drafts, checks, notes, and other negotiable instruments which may be owned or held for collection by such corporation; provided, it shall be unlawful for any notary public to take the acknowledgment of an instrument by or to a bank or other corporation of which he is a stockholder, director, officer or employee, where such notary is a party to such instrument either individually or as a representative of such corporation, or to protest a negotiable instrument owned or held for collection by such corporation, where such notary is individually a party to such instrument. 

Study of the statute will reveal that it does not repeal or set aside the general rule, but rather clarifies and strengthens it. 

(4) A notary may not lawfully certify an instrument if he is therein named as grantor or grantee; in other words, if

his name appears in the paper as grantor or grantee, it seems the universal rule that he is thereby disqualified. 

He may not lawfully take and certify his own acknowledgment as grantor; he may not lawfully take and certify the acknowledgment of someone else who is selling or conveying something to him as grantor. (5) A notary may not lawfully certify the acknowledgment of any instrument given or accepted by a partnership of which he is one of the partners. (6) It would seem that a notary may not lawfully take and certify an acknowledgment of either his wife, or of her husband. And without doubt this is the general and well established rule, but exceptions have been sustained. (7) In some jurisdictions, a stockholder, officer, agent or employee of a private corporation is disqualified to certify if the corporation is a party; in others, the disqualification is restricted or eliminated. (8) In some jurisdictions, blood or marriage relationship to a party will disqualify the notary; in other jurisdictions no such ground for disqualification is recognized. If the notary is in doubt, he should consult a competent local attorney before acting. (9) Where fraud or deceit is shown, this fact has the effect of voiding the certificate, whether disqualification for interest exists or not.

Further reference to this topic is found in the chapters on ACKNOWLEDGMENTS, AFFIDAVITS,

DEPOSITIONS, PROTESTS. 

BOND AND OATH 

Q. Why are bond and oath required of a notary public?

A. For the same reasons they are required of any other public officer. See hereinafter the chapter on CIVIL LIABILITY. 

However, the requirements upon these points are not uniform in all the States. See the following state-by-state analysis. 

TERM OF OFFICE 

Q. For what period of time does a notary public serve?

A. For such period as is designated in the statute or law providing for his appointment. In some of the states, appointment is for one period, in another for a longer or shorter; in some the time is set at the pleasure of the appointing power. Or-

dinarily the term is either two or four years. Death of course terminates the appointment. The term of a notary ex-officio is that of the office by virtue of which he becomes a notary. 

RESIGNATION, SUSPENSION 

Q. May a notary public resign his office?

A. Without doubt he may, by tendering his resignation to the appointing power, by depositing his records, if any, with the proper authority, if any provided by law, and by discontinuing his notarial services. 

Q. May a notary public be suspended temporarily?

A. This writer has not encountered any provision of law for temporary suspension of a notary.

IMPEACHMENT, REMOVAL 

Q. May a notary public be removed from office, by impeachment or otherwise, before expiration of his appointive term? 

A. (1) A notary, as any other public officer, may be removed from office for official misbehavior. In the different states different means are provided for his impeachment and removal, the authority to remove being ordinarily vested in the governor. While no authoritative court ruling upon the point has been noted, it would seem logical to suppose that only some such act as the giving of a false and fraudulent certificate or negligence, or other misconduct in office, would justify such removal. (2) It is provided in nearly every state that if a notary changes his place of residence by moving out of his notarial jurisdiction he thereby forfeits and vacates the office.

Q. If one is a notary ex-officio only, for example, a justice of the peace, and duly qualified as justice of the peace, and as notary public, may he be impeached, or deprived of his commission as a notary, but be and remain a justice of the peace? 

A. This writer thinks that as long as the main office is held, the ex-officio status remains, and that the two offices can not be separated by removal from either, without vacating both. No authoritative court decision upon the point has been found.

WHAT IT COSTS TO QUALIFY 

Q. What are the legal fees or charges, to qualify for notary?

A. In each of the states there are fees and expenses incidental to qualifying, but these are not uniform in all the states, either in kind or amount. In some, the only charge is for filing of bond and oath. In others there are one or more of these papers, namely, application for appointment; bond; oath; copy of official signature, each of which must be filed and the filing fee paid, followed by issuance of the official commission, for which there is another charge. In some of the states there is levied an additional flat charge in the nature of a license fee which varies in amount, being in Idaho $3.00; in Delaware, $10.00; in Virginia, $25.00; etc. Cost of qualifying is a local question.

SEAL, FINAL DISPOSITION 

Q. At termination of the notary's commission, either through the notary's death, or otherwise, what disposition is made of his seal of office?

A. In nearly all jurisdictions, the seal is his own property, he being required by law governing his appointment, to provide himself with a seal, and in those jurisdictions, he may make such disposition of the seal as suits him, or, after his death, as suits his heirs, except that in some jurisdictions it is further stipulated by law that the seal be delivered at end of the term to the public custodian, whatever officer that happens to be, usually the county court clerk. If the seal is furnished, and does not belong to him, obviously it is subject to disposal by the authority to which it belongs. 

OFFICIAL RECORDS, FINAL DISPOSITION 

Q. At the end of the notary's term of office, what disposition is made of his records of office, if any? 

A. (1) In some jurisdictions a notary is not by law required to keep official records, and in those jurisdictions it would seem that-if he keeps records-such records are his individual property and he may make of them such disposition as pleases him, or in event of his death, his heirs. (2) In those jurisdictions, though, where he is by law required to keep official records, such records belong not to him, but to the public. They are the property of the

public just as are the court records in the court house, and it is nearly everywhere provided by law that at the end of his term he shall deliver such records to a designated custodian, usually the county court clerk. (3) Where it is by law provided that the records shall be delivered to a custodian at the end of the term, it is also usually provided that upon failure to make such delivery, either the notary himself, or his heirs or legal representatives for failure to make such delivery, become subject to criminal punishment and also to liability for money damages for benefit of any person injured by failure of delivery. 

"MY COMMISSION EXPIRES" 

Q. Under what circumstances is it necessary, if ever, for a notary to show on his official certificate the date of expiration of his commission?

A. This question is answered more specifically in the analysis which follows in this chapter but some explanation may be in order here. A notary whose commission has expired may have no legal right to act as a notary, and persons who favor him with their patronage are entitled to be informed of the date when his commission will expire. Reasons for this are obvious. 

STATE REQUIREMENTS DIFFERENT 

In some of the states all notary commissions expire on a given date and are renewable at that time. Thus, in Texas, the commission of an appointed notary expires on June 1st of the odd year, no matter when issued, and the commission of an ex-officio notary expires in January of the odd year when the biennial term as justice of the peace ends, regardless of the date of his qualification for office. In Texas therefore, it has been thought not necessary to require that a notary's official certificate show the date of expiration of his commission, since such expiration is fixed by law and everyone is presumed to know the law. Maybe so. But this writer opines it would be better for every notarial certificate, given anywhere, to state the date of expiration of the notary's commission. In many states, Missouri for example, notary appointments are made by the governor, at any time, and run for a fixed period of years from the date of such appointment, thus making it essential that the official certificate show definitely the date of expiration of the notary's commission.

SHOWING COMMENDED 

Q. If a notary lives in a state whose law does not require the official certificate to show the date of expiration of the commission, may he or she nevertheless, properly and legally stamp the official certificate so as to show such date?

A. Yes. Whether the law requires this showing or not it may be made without in any way affecting the validity of the certificate or doing any harm in any other way. 

PENALTY FOR OMITTING 

Q. In those states where it is required that a notary's official certificate shall show date of expiration of his commission, what penalty, if any, is provided for his failure to make such showing?

A. The failure to make such showing is a criminal offense punishable in some states by fine, in others by imprisonment. 

Also for any damage or loss which may be occasioned by such failure the injured party has as against the offending notary and his bondsmen a right of action for damages.

FEDERAL, AND STATE-BY-STATE, ANALYSIS 

Q. Are notary public regulations and requirements under Federal and State laws identical? 

A. (1) Definitely not the same, but in no way conflicting, so far as this writer has discovered. And this seems as good a place as any to set forth some of the principal Federal laws relating to notaries acting in Federal service. ARMY (2) "Any officer of any component of the Army of the United States on active duty in Federal service commissioned in or assigned or detached to duty with the Judge 

Advocate General's Department, any staff judge advocate or acting staff judge advocate, the President of a general or special court martial, any summary court martial, the trial judge advocate, or any assistant trial judge advocate of a general or special court martial, the president or the recorder of a court of inquiry or of a military board, any officer designated to take a deposition, any officer detailed to conduct an investigation,

and the adjutant, assistant adjutant or personnel adjutant of any command shall have power to administer oaths for the purposes of the administration of military justice or for other purposes of military administration; and shall also have the general powers of a notary public in the administration of oaths, the execution and acknowledgment of legal instruments, the attestation of documents and all other forms of notarial acts to be executed by persons subject to military law: Provided, That no fee of any character shall be paid to any officer mentioned in this section for the performance of any notarial act herein authorized.”

NAVY, MARINE CORPS, COAST GUARD 

(3) "During the existence of a war in which the United States is engaged or of a national emergency declared by the President, and for six months after the termination of such war or national emergency, such officers of the Navy, Marine Corps, and Coast Guard, as the Secretary of the Navy may designate, shall have the general powers of a notary public in the administration of oaths; the execution, acknowledgment and attestation of instruments and papers; and the performance of all other notarial acts: Provided, That no fee of any character shall be paid to any officer for the performance of any notarial act herein authorized: Provided further, That whenever the Coast Guard shall be under the jurisdiction of the Secretary of the Treasury during a national emergency, the Secretary of the Treasury shall have and may exercise as to the Coast Guard the authority of the Secretary of the Navy under this section: 

And provided further, That the signature without seal of any officer of the Navy, Marine Corps, or Coast Guard acting as such notary shall be prima face evidence of his authority." "In places beyond the continental limits of the United States where the Navy or Marine Corps is serving, such officers of the Navy or Marine Corps as are authorized to administer oaths for the purposes of the administration of naval justice and for other purposes of naval administration shall have the general powers of a notary public, or of a consul of the United States, in the administration of oaths, the execution and acknowledgment of legal instruments, the attestation of documents, and the performance of all other notarial acts."

EXECUTIVE OFFICES, AGENCIES, BUREAUS 

(4) "Chief clerks of the various executive departments, independent establishments, and other government agencies, or of bureaus threof, chiefs of field parties and any officer or employee of any executive department, independent establishment, or other Government agency, in the District of Columbia, or elsewhere, who shall have been designated in writing for such purpose by the head of the department, establishment, or agency concerned, are required, empowered, and authorized, when requested, to administer oaths, required by law or otherwise to accounts for travel or other expense against the United States, with like force and effect as officers having a seal; for such services when so rendered, or when rendered on demand by notaries public, who at the time. are also salaried officers or employees of the United States, no charge shall be made; and no fee or money paid for the services herein described shall be paid or reimbursed by the United States." 

NATIONAL PARKS, RESERVATIONS 

(5) "The superintendent, the acting superintendent, custodian, and principal clerks of the various national parks, and other Government reservations, are required, empowered, and authorized, when requested, to administer oaths, required by law or otherwise, to accounts for travel or other expense against the United States, with like force and effect as officers having a seal; for such services when so rendered, or when rendered on demand by notaries public, who at the time are also salaried officers or employees of the United States, no charge shall be made; and no fee or money paid for the services herein described shall be paid, or reimbursed, by the 

United States."

NOTARIES MAY ADMINISTER OATHS 

(6) "In all cases in which, under the laws of the United States, oaths are authorized or required to be administered, they may be administered by notaries public duly appointed in any State, District, or Territory of the United States, by clerks and prothonotaries of courts of record of any such State, District, or Territory, by the deputies of such clerks and prothonotaries, and by all

magistrates_authorized by the laws of or pertaining to any such State, District, or Territory to administer oaths." Alabama

COUNTY NOTARIES 

The governor may appoint a competent number of notaries for each county in the state to serve for a term of four years. In each election precinct, and in each ward of cities with over 5,000 population the governor may appoint one notary public who, in addition to the powers of a notary, has also the powers of a justice of the peace for such precinct. Where there is no notary public or he is absent or incapable of action any justice of the peace may perform notarial duties but his certificate must recite the facts showing necessity for him to act. Official bond of $1,000, to be approved by probate judge. Women are eligible for appointment but have authority of commercial notaries only. Notaries have the authority usually incident to their office, and jurisdiction is limited to the county for which commissioned. It is required that each notary keep a fair register of all his official acts. Governor may remove notary from office at his own discretion. The statutory requirement for an official seal is as follows: "For the authentication of his official acts each notary must provide a seal of office, which must present, by its impression, his name, office, state and county for which he was appointed."

STATE NOTARIES 

The law further provides as follows: A competent number of notaries public for the state at large shall be appointed by the Governor, who shall hold office for four years from the date of their commission. Such notary publics for the state at large shall perform all the acts and exercise all the authority now performed and exercised by notaries public under the general laws of the state of Alabama; the jurisdiction of such notaries public shall not be limited to the county of their residence but shall extend to any county of the state. **** Bond **** Notaries public appointed under this chapter must give bond, with sureties to be approved by the judge of probate of the county of their residence in the sum of $2000.00, payable to the state of Alabama, and conditioned to faithfully discharge the duties of such office, so long as they may continue therein, or discharge any of the duties thereof; which bond must be executed, approved, filed and recorded in the office of the judge of probate of the county of their residence before

they enter on the duties of such office. Such notaries public for the state at large in the event of any breach of the conditions of their official bonds, may be sued in the county of their residence, or in the county in which the breach was committed, or in the county where the party or parties, who suffered damages from the 

acts, each such notary must provide a seal of office which must present by its impression, his name, office and the state for which he was appointed. Alaska (1) Appointment is by the Governor of the Territory for a four-year term. Appointee must be an adult, and resident of the district for which appointed and must make $1000 bond. Is required to pay $10.00 to the secretary of the Territory for issuance of commission. Has usual powers and duties. Required to "provide and keep an official seal, upon which must be engraved the name of the district and the words Notary Public, with the surname of the notary and at least the initials of his Christian name." 

Certificate should show expiration date of commission. Jurisdiction is Territorial. Upon removal from office or resignation, must deliver all official records kept by him to the clerk of the district court, or upon his death, his legal representative must make such delivery, under penalty of damages in favor of anyone injured by failure to make such delivery. (2) Each postmaster in the Territory is authorized and directed to administer oaths and affirmations and to take acknowledgments, and to make and execute certificates thereof, and to perform all other functions of a notary public within the Territory. A postmaster giving such certificate shall certify as postmaster, using his stamp cancellation as a seal, stating the name of the post office and the date, and for such service is allowed the same fees as are allowed a notary. Arizona

Appointment by the secretary of state for four-year term. Official certificate not valid unless under seal. Seal must bear name of the notary, name of county for which commissioned and the words, "notary public." Certificate must show date of expiration of notary's commission. Jurisdiction limited to the notary's county of residence. Required to keep record of all official acts, showing names of parties, date and character of instrument executed, date of acknowledgment, and description of property dealt with. On removal from county or expiration of term of appointment, re-

cords to be deposited with county recorder. Fee of $2.50 charged for issuance of commission. Bond, $1000. Oath of office. No eligibility requirement other than to be resident of county; women eligible if over twenty-one years of age. Arkansas

No eligibility requirement other than to be a citizen of county for which appointed, man or woman. Appointed by governor, for four-year term. Oath; bond, $1000. Fee for issuance of commission, $5.00. Usual powers and duties of notary public. 

Required to keep official record and give certified copy when requested. Upon removal or termination, records to be deposited with county clerk. Must have official seal, and official certificate must show date of expiration of term. Seal must show "notary public, county of Ark." California

In California women, as well as men, are eligible to serve as notary. To be appointed one must be a citizen of California, a resident of the county for six months or longer and over twentyone years of age. $5000 official bond. Appointment by the governor for a four-year term. Usual powers and duties. Required to keep record of all official acts, names of parties, date and kind of instrument. All official acts must be certified under official seal, which must bear the words, "Notary Public", arms of the

State, and name of county. County jurisdiction only. Not required to certify date of expiration of commission. 

Canal Zone

Governor of Zone appoints, for three-year term. Canal Zone jurisdiction. Seal required, and certificate must show date of expiration of official commission. Usual powers and duties. Required to keep records of official acts, except oaths and affirmations. Colorado

Appointment by Governor for four-year term. Has usual powers and duties. Official seal required, showing notary's name and residence. Official certificate must show date of expiration of commission; failure to show which is punishable by forfeiture of commission. Jurisdiction only in county of residence. $1000.00 bond. Must be citizen of state. Women eligible. Required to keep records and on request furnish copies thereof. Commission fee, $5.

Connecticut

State-wide jurisdiction with five year term under appointment by the governor. Term runs from April 1 of year of appointment. 

Usual notarial duties and powers. Women eligible. Must be citizen. No bond required. Commission fee, $10. Must file application for appointment in own handwriting, if for first appointment, and must be recommended by county clerk, but these requirements not stipulated for reappointments. No seal required. Usual powers. No official record required. Connecticutt notary not liable to any statutory penalties. Delaware

Two year term, except justice of the peace and state revenue collector, who are appointed ex-officio notary. License or commission fee, $10. Oath. No bond. Seal to be engraved with name of notary, date of appointment, official title, anything else he wishes. 

No records required. Usual powers and duties. Not necessary for certificate to show date of expiration of commission. County jurisdiction only. Each financial institution to have a notary of 

Number of additional notaries provided for. Appointment by the governor. 

District of Columbia

Five year term under appointment by the President, and removable at discretion. Not necessary to show commission expiration date. Must be resident of district. Must have official seal and is clothed with usual powers and duties. Bond, $2,000. Jurisdiction in some matters outside the district. Required to keep records. Florida 

The Florida statute is in part as follows: "The governor may appoint as many notaries public as to him shall seem necessary; and upon the death, removal or resignation of any such notaries public, to appoint others in his or their room, which said notaries public shall hold their respective offices for four years, and shall use and exercise such office of notary public for such places and within such limits and precincts as the governor shall direct, to whose protestations, attestations and other instruments of publication due credence shall be given." 

Official bond, $500. Women eligible if over twenty-one. Official certificate must show expiration date of commission. Notary has usual powers and duties; may also solemnize marriages. Commission fee, $5.00. Must be citizen of the United States. Must keep records.

Georgia 

Four year term under appointment by State Librarian, with state jurisdiction, or by judge of superior court with jurisdiction limited to county of residence and appointment. Usual powers and duties. Must have seal but unnecessary to show commission expiration date. Required to keep records of official acts. A notary may also be appointed by superior court judge for each militia district outside of Macon and Atlanta upon grand jury recommendation but the commission issues from the governor's office for a term of four years. The appointee may exercise duties of notary public and justice of the peace. To be eligible for appointment one must be twenty-one or older or an attorney at law and of good moral character. County notaries have county jurisdiction only, state notaries have state-wide jurisdiction, notary appointed by superior court judge has district jurisdiction. Commission fee $2.00. Women are eligible. No bond required. Commission of notary appointed by State Librarian is revocable at any time by Librarian. 

Hawaiian Islands

Four year term under appointment by the attorney general of the Territory of Hawaii, and removable at discretion of appointing power. $1,000 bond. Must be citizen. Usual powers and duties with jurisdiction limited to the appointee's judicial district. 

Must keep official record. Seal must be engraved with name of the notary and the words, "Notary Public," "Territory of Hawaii." Commission fee $5.00 elsewhere than Honolulu, $10.00 in Honolulu. Term, until removed. Idaho 

Four year term under appointment of governor. Has statewide jurisdiction with usual powers and duties. Required to have seal showing "State of Idaho" and "Notary Public." Must keep record of official acts done by him. Must be qualified voter in 

Idaho. Not necessary for certificate to show commission expiration date but must show notary's official title and place of residence. Commission fee $10.00. Bond $1,000. Illinois

Appointment by governor for four year term subject to removal by apointing power. Must be twenty-one years of age or older, if male, or eighteen or older if female; a citizen of the United 

States, a resident of the county for which appointed and a resident of Illinois for one year or longer. Application for appointment

must be accompanied by petition signed by fifty legal voters. Appointment subject to advice and consent of the Senate. Commission fee $2.00. Bond $1,000. Usual powers and duties. Official seal required with words descriptive of the office and name of the appointee's county of residence. State-wide jurisdiction. Must keep records. Unnecessary for certificate to show expiration date of commission. Notary is exempt from the requirements of the state civil service laws. Indiana

Before he is appointed an Indiana notary public is required to furnish certificate of qualification and of moral character from the judge of the circuit court of his county. He is appointed for a four year term by the governor. Clothed with usual powers and duties he is required to give official bond of $1,000, has state-wide jurisdiction, and must have official seal which must indicate his official character. Certificate must show commission expiration date. Commission fee $1.00. Must keep records. A statute forbids certain corporation officers acting as notary. Iowa

Appointment is by the governor for a three year term, all commissions expiring July 4 of the third year, beginning with 1924.

Has usual powers and duties, $500 bond, must have seal, but unnecessary to show expiration date of commission. County jurisdiction only but may serve in adjoining county by complying with statutory requirements. Citizenship essential to eligibility. Commission fee $5.00. Seal must bear the words "Notarial Seal," "Iowa" and notary's name. Must keep records. Kansas

Appointment by the governor for four year term. $1,000 bond. 

Must keep fair record of his official acts. Has usual powers and duties. Must have official seal; certificate must show expiration date of commission. County jurisdiction, not state-wide, but notary residing in town or city located in more than one county may serve in each such county. Citizenship required. Women eligible. 

Seal must show notary's name and place of residence. Kentucky

In order to be appointed notary in Kentucky one must be at least twenty-one years of age, a resident of the county from which he makes his application, of good moral character and capable of discharging the duties imposed upon him as such notary. He must make application to the governor for appointment, which applica-

tion must be approved by a circuit judge, circuit clerk, county judge, county clerk, justice of the peace or a member of the general assembly of the county of such applicant's residence. Appointment is by the governor for a four year term. Women are eligible. $1,000 bond. Usual duties and powers. Certificate must state expiration date of commission. Required to keep record of protests. Should have seal but some certificates are valid without it. 

The relevant statutes in this state provide that: "The commission of the governor on making the appointment must designate the limits within which the notary is to act.' 

Further it is provided as follows: "A notary public may exercise all the functions of his office in any county of the state, by filing in the county clerk's office in such county his written signature and a certificate of the county clerk of the county for which he was appointed, setting forth the fact of his appointment and qualification as a notary public, and paying a fee of one dollar to the county clerk.*** The county clerk of a county in whose office any notary public has so filed his signature and certificate, shall when requested, subjoin to any certificate of proof or acknowledgment signed by the notary a certificate under his hand and seal, stating that such notary public has filed a certificate of his appointment and qualifications with his written signature in his office, and was at the time of taking such proof or acknowledgment duly authorized to take the same; that he is well acquainted with the handwriting of the notary public and believes that the signature to such proof or acknowledgment is genuine."

When this has been done the instrument so proved and certified by the notary shall be entitled to be read in evidence or to be recorded in any court the same as if it were certified by a county clerk. Louisiana

In Louisiana the state's geography affects somewhat the regulations for notarial activities. For the parish of Orleans the statutes and rules differ from those provided for other parishes in the state. 

Applicant may be appointed to serve during good behavior, which may mean practically for life. The statute calls for appointment by the governor of "as many notaries public in the different parishes of the state, as may be deemed necessary, subject to confirmation by the senate." It is further provided, though, that no appointment

of notaries public in and for the parish of Orleans shall be made. by the governor unless the number of those holding commission in that parish be less than 725, which "shall be the limit;" but if the number of notaries for that parish shall be less than 725, the governor, "shall, by and with the advice and consent of the senate and upon a certificate of competency granted by the supreme court of Louisiana, make the necessary appointment of notaries public to fill vacancies within the limit of 725 as aforesaid." Annual license fee, $5.00.

APPOINTMENT IN ORLEANS PARISH 

Applicant for appointment as notary public in Orleans Parish shall give ten days notice in a daily paper published in the city of New Orleans of his intention to apply for said commission. Upon producing to the supreme court proof of said publication, together with a certificate establishing his citizenship, residence and character, that is to say, "being of good moral character, integrity, competency and sober habits," sworn to and subscribed by at least two reputable citizens of the state, the supreme court may then proceed to examine said applicant in open court; and if he be found competent and possessed of the necessary qualifications, shall issue to him a certificate accordingly, signed by at least three judges of said supreme court. The public examination provided for may be dispensed with by the supreme court if the applicant produces proof of his having previously obtained a certificate from the court. 

Before the governor shall issue to the applicant a commission of notary public for the parish of Orleans, he shall require of him the production of: 

1. Certificate signed by at least three supreme court judges. 

2. His (the applicant's) oath of office. 

3. The applicant's bond, properly executed, approved and registered in the conveyances office. 

4. His official signature. 

5. The impress of his official seal.

All of which shall be deposited in the office of the secretary of state and annexed in the margin of a book to be kept for the purpose by the said secretary of state, to be styled "The Notarial 

Book of Orleans Parish."

APPOINTMENT OUTSIDE ORLEANS PARISH 

Outside the parish of Orleans, appointment may be made of male citizen, above the age of 21 years, resident of the parish five years or longer, of good character, who passes examination before a judge

of a parish, district or supreme court, proof of all of which may be submitted in writing with application. 

MUST ACT IN OFFICE, UNLESS 

"All notarial acts shall be made and executed in the office of the notary unless when the illness of the party, or some other sufficient cause shall prevent him from attending in such office." 

OATH AND BOND 

In parishes other than that of Orleans, the bond is fixed at $1,000, the oath is in substantially the form of such oaths the country over. In Orleans Parish, the bond is fixed at $5,000. 

During the recent world war, if a notary's bond expired during the period of his military service, he was allowed as long as 60 days after expiration of the period of his military service, to renew the same. Statutory provision in Orleans parish is made for testing at least once in each twelve months the soundness of the notary's official bond. The bond of notaries everywhere must be renewed every five years under penalty of suspension from office for failure to so renew. A notary may continue to serve so long as bond is kept in good standing, unless suspended by the supreme court. The bond does not operate or become an incumbrance on the notary's property unless or until suit is filed thereon and notice of lis pendens placed on record. 

POWERS AND DUTIES 

The Louisiana notary has the usual powers and duties of taking and certifying acknowledgments, affidavits, depositions and protests, plus other powers and duties not conferred on notaries in other states. "To make inventories, appraisements, partitions, to receive wills, make protests, matrimonial contracts, conveyances, and generally all contracts and instruments of writing; to hold family meetings and meetings of creditors; to receive acknowledgments of instruments under private signature; to affix the seals upon the effects of deceased persons, and to raise the same," etc. 

MAY HAVE LEAVE OF ABSENCE 

The governor of Louisiana may grant a leave of absence to notary, for a period not exceeding eight months, to date from the day permission so granted. Notary granted such leave must name and designate another notary to represent him during such absence. 

During the recent world war, a notary serving in the armed forces could have leave of absence for the period, and by reason of, such service.

OFFICIAL RECORDS 

In all Louisiana parishes it is required of the notary that he keep official records, and there is also the following provision: "It shall be the duty of every notary public for the Parish of Orleans to keep and preserve in his office and under his control the original of all acts, contracts and instruments of writing executed before him, with the exception only of affidavits and instruments under private signature, which may be acknowledged or proved before him, and depositions of witnesses, and he shall cause the said acts, contracts and instruments of writing to be bound in separate book form volumes for each year, with complete indices to each volume; provided, however, that should the number of acts passed by a notary not exceed fifty in any one year, he may wait until the end of the subsequent year to have them bound, but regardless of number, at the end of the subsequent year his acts for each of those years shall be bound as aforesaid, which acts together with protests made by him, will constitute the archives of his office. It shall be the duty of the custodian of notarial records in and for the Parish of Orleans at the time of trial of the rule required (for bond solvency) to require evidence from each notary public that he has caused his acts to be bound and indexed in the manner aforesaid, and in case it should then appear that any notary has neglected to comply with the provisions of this statute, the court shall allow said notary thirty (30) days within which to index and bind his acts; and on his failure to do so within that time he shall forfeit his commission and turn over his notarial acts and records to the custodian of notarial records in and for the Parish of Orleans in the same manner provided for failure to furnish proper bond." It is further required of a notary that he make sure that certain classes of acts passed before him are placed on public record.

NOTARIAL RECORDS CENTRAL Office 

Under a law specifically providing therefor, in New Orleans a central office of notarial records is maintained under charge of a bonded custodian, whose duty it is to collect together and safely keep the records of all notaries of the parish of Orleans who shall have ceased to be notary, either by death, removal or resignation or otherwise, except such records as are already in the custody of other notaries. It is required of the custodian that his office shall be kept in a fire-proof building and that classification of such records be made to facilitate ready access thereto. He has authority to demand and take possession of notarial records in event of death, removal or resignation of a notary.

NOTARY'S OFfice Kept OPEN, WHEN 

Every notary in Orleans Parish shall keep his office in a building covered with tile, slates or terrace, and keep such office open, for the ready examination of his records, every day from the hour of ten o'clock A. M. to 3 o'clock P. M., Sundays and holidays excepted. (Subject to $25 fine for each days violation, and forfeiture of commission and surrender of records to custodian, for 10 days violation.)

NOTARY MAY APPOINT DEPUTIES 

It shall be lawful for each and every notary public in New Orleans to appoint one or more deputies to assist him in the making of protests and delivery of notices of protests of bills of exchange and promissory notes; provided, that each notary shall be personally responsible for the acts of each deputy employed by him. Each deputy shall take an oath faithfully to perform his duties as such. 

The certificate of notice of protest shall state by whom served. 

Certificate of NonalienATION 

In Orleans parish it is the duty of every notary public and sheriff of the parish of Orleans, before passing any act of deed or sale, to demand a certificate from the register of conveyances, showing that the vendor had not alienated in any other way the property about to be sold by him. Maine

Must be a citizen. Commission fee, $5.00. Appointment by 

Governor for seven year term, or during good behavior. May be reappointed, or another appointed in place. Shall have seal, to be engraved "Notary Public," and "Maine" or "Me", and the state coat of arms or such other device as notary wishes. Upon removal or resignation, records to be deposited with clerk of judicial courts of county for which he was appointed. May solemnize marriages. 

May do all things that justices of the peace are or may be authorized to do and shall have the same territorial jurisdiction. When requested, shall enter on record all losses or damages sustained or apprehended by sea or land, and all averages, and such other matters as by mercantile usage appertain to the office; grant warrants of survey on vessels; all facts, extracts from documents and circumstances so noted, signed and sworn to by all the persons appearing to protest; he shall note, extend and record the protest so made; and grant authenticated copies thereof, under his signature and notarial seal, to those who request and pay for them. 

Certificates must be under seal, but are not required to show expiration date of commission. Oath and bond. May summon wit-

nesses to prove execution of deed. For wilful destruction, alteration or concealment of his official records, notary may forfeit $200 to $1000, besides becoming liable for money damages to party injured. Secretary of state to provide lists of notaries to certain officers listed. Maryland 

Appointment by the governor for two year term, subject to confirmation by the senate. Each county shall have at least two notaries. Women eligible. Appointee must be of known good character, and integrity and abilities, citizen of the United States and must have resided in Maryland two years previous to appointment. Usual powers and duties. Required to keep official records. 

Unnecessary to show commission expiration date. Required to have official seal, "on which seal shall be engraved such device as he may think proper, and for legend shall have the name, surname and office of the notary and the place of his residence.' 

Has statewide jurisdiction. Is required in the first week in January, April, July and October in each year to pay to the state treasurer one-half of all fees received by him for protest work, over and above the sum of $350 per year in any of the counties and over and above $500 per year in the city of Baltimore. Bond for $6000 if appointed for the city of Baltimore and $2000 if appointed for any county, conditioned for payment into the treasury of the State's part of the protest fees. Commission fee, $5.00 to state treasurer, 50c to clerk. Massachusetts

Appointment is by the governor subject to confirmation by the council. State wide jurisdiction. Certificate must show expiration date of commission. Commission fee, $5.00. Woman notary who has changed her name shall re-register under new name and pay fee of $1. Michigan

Four year term under appointment by the governor. Formal application for appointment is necessary which application must be indorsed by a member of the legislature or circuit or probate judge of applicant's county of residence. State wide jurisdiction. 

Usual powers and duties. $1000 bond. Certificate must show expiration date of commission. Appointee must be twenty-one or older, resident of county for which appointment is asked and citizen of the state. One or more notaries to be appointed in each county. Commission fee $1.00. Governor may remove for misconduct or incapacity. Must reside in county where appointed. 

Acts to be authenticated with seal.

Minnesota

Appointment by the governor for seven year term. $2,000 bond. 

State wide jurisdiction. Usual powers and duties. Appointee must be over twenty-one and resident of county for which appointed. 

Certificate must show commission expiration date. The statute further provides as follows: "Every notary public, in addition to signing his name to the jurat or certificate of acknowledgment, shall, immediately following his signature and immediately preceding his official description, endorse thereon his name with a typewriter or print the same legibly with a stamp or with pen and ink; provided that the failure to so endorse or print the name shall not invalidate any jurat or certificate of acknowledgment."

Notary may be removed from office for charging or receiving a fee or reward greater than the amount allowed by law or for dishonestly or unfaithfully discharging his duties of office. 

Every notary shall provide himself with an official seal, with which he shall authenticate his official acts and upon which shall be engraved the arms of this state, the words and notarial seal and the name of the county for which he was appointed. 

Must keep records. Mississippi

Justices of the peace and clerks of the circuit courts are notary ex officio. Notaries are also appointed by the governor for a four year term. Official bond of $2000. A statute with reference to notary seal is as follows: "The board of supervisors of every county which has not done so, shall provide a notarial seal with the inscription: "Notary Public of the county of... around the margin, and the image of an eagle in the center, which seal shall be kept in the office of the clerk of the circuit court; and all ex-officio notaries public may at all times have access to and use such seal for the authentication of any notarial act necessary to be authenticated.”

Mississippi notary has the usual duties and powers, and must. show date of expiration of commission on official certificates. 

County jurisdiction only. Required to keep official record of official acts, and is required to have a seal, having the name of the county for which he shall have been appointed and commissioned, with that of the state, and his own name on the margin thereof and the words, "notary public" across the center. In case of va-‘ cancy in office, records go to clerk of the circuit court of the county. Officers in armed forces clothed with notarial authority. On

and after May 1, 1944, failure of certificate to show expiration date, ground for revocation of commission, but such failure does not invalidate the acknowledgment. A statute reads as follows: "Every notary public shall have power to receive the proof or acknowledgment of all instruments of writing relating to commerce or navigation, such as bills of sale, bottomries, mortgages, and hypothecation of ships, vessels, letters of attorney, and such other writings as are commonly approved or acknowledged before notaries; and to perform all other duties required of notaries by commercial usage; and also to make declarations and certify the truth thereof, under his seal of office, concerning all matters done by him in virtue of his office." Missouri

Missouri notaries are required to give $2000 bond, if outside a county of more than 100,000 inhabitants; in counties of more than 100,000 inhabitants, bond is in the sum of $5000. If bond becomes insufficient, new bond may be required. Appointment is by the Governor for four year term. Women eligible. Appointee must be citizen of the United States and at least 21 years of age. 

Certificate must show date of expiration of commission. Required to keep a true and perfect record of official acts. Has usual duties and powers, and also may take and certify relinquishments of dower. Jurisdiction is county of residence and adjoining counties. 

The statute upon this point provides as follows: "It shall be the duty of every such notary when he performs an official act outside his or her own county, to state in his or her certificate that the county in which such act is performed adjoins the county within and for which he was appointed and commissioned." Required to have notarial seal on which shall be inscribed his name, the words, "notary public," the name of the county or city, if appointed for such city, in which he resides, and has his office, and the name of the state. Montana

Montana notary must be a citizen of the United States and of 

Montana for one year or longer before appointment. Appointment is by the Governor. Statewide jurisdiction. One thousand dollar bond. Seal required. In all cases where the notary public signs his name officially as a notary public, he must add to his signature the words, "Notary Public for the state of Montana, residing at (stating the name of his post office) and must endorse upon the instrument the date of expiration of his commission. 

Provision is made for exercise of notarial powers by officers in the armed forces. The governor may appoint as many notaries public for the state as he deems best.

Nebraska

Appointed by the Governor for 6 years. Appointment may be either as county notary with county-wide jurisdiction, or as general notary with state-wide jurisdiction. $2000.00 bond required for a county notary, $6,000.00 bond with corporate surety required for general notary. County notary must have seal bearing the words "Notarial Seal," the name of the county for which appointed, and the word "Nebraska." Seal may also show name or initials and the date of expiration of commission. General notary must have a seal bearing the words, "State of Nebraska, General Notary" or "State of Nebraska, General Notarial." Usual powers and duties. Expiration date of commission must be shown but may be included in seal. Application for appointment must be accompanied by petition of at least twenty-five legal voters of county in which he or she resides and applicant must certify under oath to the governor "that he or she has carefully read and understands the laws relating to the duties and records of notaries public and will, if commissioned, faithfully discharge the duties pertaining to said office and keep such records according to law." 

Another statute reads as follows: "Any person whose name is legally changed after a commission as a notary public is issued to him or her, may continue to act as such notary public and use the original commission, seal, and name until the expiration or termination of said commission. The bond given by such person shall continue in effect regardless of such legal change of name of such notary public if the notary public uses the name under which the commission is issued. Provision is made for hearing and trial if or when charges of official misconduct are filed with the governor against the notary public." Nevada

Appointment by the governor for four-year term. Women eligible if 21 or over after residence in county or district six months and in state one year. Man must be qualified elector. Usual duties and powers. $2000 bond, and oath. Statewide jurisdiction. Required to keep official records with names of parties to transactions passed before him. Governor may, for cause, revoke commission. $10.00 commission fee, payable to state library fund. The statute says: "Each notary public shall provide a notarial seal, an impression of which shall be made on his official bond, on which shall be engraved the name of the county for which he is commissioned, and the initials of the territory (state), the name of the notary, and the words "Notary Public," with which he shall authenticate all his official acts." "The statute also mentions "pre- 1

decessor" and "successor" of the appointee, though appointment "without limit" as to number, is authorized. 

New Hampshire

Appointment by the governor, with advice of the council. 5-year term. Appointee must be qualified to vote. Women eligible. Statewide jurisdiction. Certificate must show date of expiration of commission. Must keep official records. Commission fee, $5.00. Has usual dutes and powers, and, the statute provides: "same powers as a justice of the peace in relation to depositions and the acknowledgment of deeds and other instruments and the administration of oaths." No provision of law noted for official bond, or requiring use of official seal, though seal commonly used. When office vacated, records go to secretary of state who is custodian of notarial records for the state. 

New Jersey

Five year term, appointment by the governor, subject to removal at the governor's pleasure. Citizenship required. Women eligible, with provision for change of name, etc. State-wide jurisdiction.

Usual duties and powers, with certain added duties such as presence at opening of lock boxes. No provision of law noted requiring official bond. Official seal not required to certify affidavits, but required for certificate to other official acts. Must keep records. If office vacated, records deposited with county clerk. 

New Mexico

New Mexico notary is required to be of good moral character; competent to discharge the duties of the office; 21 years of age or older, and must have resided in the State one year or longer. 

Four year term under appointment by the Governor. $500.00 bond. Usual duties and powers. Women eligible. Required to keep record of official acts. Statewide jurisdiction. Official certificate must show date of expiration of commission and must be under official seal which is required to bear the name, official title and name of the county for which the notary is appointed. 

Governor also authorized to appoint notaries to reside at the different military posts in New Mexico. Notary to post notary commission in conspicuous place. Bond must be recorded by county clerk, then filed with secretary of state. Commission fee, $2.50. 

Must deposit with clerk of county court of county in which he resides the impression of his official seal, and his official signature, after which the clerk may certify (authenticate) his official acts as a notary public. An additional duty is that of attending at

opening of bank lock boxes, under certain stated conditions. Another is that of acting as referee for dependent and neglected children, for which latter duty he may receive a fee not in excess of $5. Officer in the armed forces is authorized to take and certify acknowledgments and affidavits outside the United States. If a 

New Mexico notary public gives a false certificate, or "appends his official certificate to acknowledgments or other documents when the parties executing same have not appeared in person before him" he shall be subject to fine or imprisonment or both. 

New York

Secretary of state may appoint as many notaries public for the state of New York as in his judgment may be deemed best. Twoyear term, to expire on March 30, but if appointment is not made on March 31, then expiration date is March 30 after next March 

31. Women are eligible. Application for appointment shall be on form prescribed by the secretary of state. To be appointed applicant must be citizen, must be resident of the state of New York at least one year preceding appointment and must continue to reside in state, though change of residence from one county to another does not vacate the office. If not an attorney duly admitted to practise, must be of good moral character, have the equivalent of a common school education and be familiar with the duties and responsibilities of a notary public, but under certain conditions the secretary of state may waive the qualifying conditions. Secretary of state may suspend or remove, but accused is entitled to hearing. 

No person can be appointed who has been convicted of a felony, or of either of a lengthy list of misdemeanors. $10 commission fee in city of New York, $5 in counties of more than 50,000 population and less than 1,000,000, and $2 for counties less than 50,ooo. Usual powers and duties. Person appointed shall file oath of office and official signature with county clerk within 30 days and same, with his commission, shall be recorded.
