Thereafter the secretary of state, or county clerk of any county wherein the oath of office, commission and official signature are on file may authenticate the notary's official acts. Fee for such authentication or certification, $1.00" **** a notary public, in addition to the venue of his act and his signature, shall print, typewrite, or stamp, beneath his signature in black ink, his name, the words, "Notary

Public, State of New York," the name of the county in which he originally qualified and the date upon which his commission expires and, in addition, whenever required, a notary public shall also include the name of the county in which his certificate of official character is filed, using the words, "Certificate filed in 

County." A notary public who has qualified or

who has filed a certificate of official character in the office of the clerk in a county or counties within the city of New York must also affix to each instrument his official number or numbers in black ink, as given to him by the clerk or clerks of such county or counties at the time such notary qualified in such county or counties, and if the instrument is to be recorded in an office of the register of the city of New York in any county within such city and the notary has been given a number or numbers by such register or his predecessor, in any county or counties, when his autographed signature and certificate are filed in such office or offices pursuant to section one hundred one of this chapter, he shall also affix such number or numbers. No official act of such notary shall be held invalid on account of the failure to comply with these provisions." Any properly registered practicing attorney may be appointed notary upon his request. Notary must have a seal, which is customarily affixed to his certificates, but no provision of law noted requiring general use of seal. County jurisdiction, but the law is so elastic that it is equivalent to state-wide jurisdiction when requirements are complied with. A notary who is appointed only for the county of his residence, may also officiate in other counties if he qualifies by filing with the county clerk in each such county his autograph signature and an official certificate of the county clerk of the county of his residence to the effect that he is a duly qualified and acting notary public for such appointive county. Must keep official records. When office vacated, it is provided that the records shall pass to the notary's successor. 

North Carolina

Governor may from time to time appoint one or more fit persons, men or women, who are citizens, notary public in each county, and may revoke the commission of such an appointee when complaint made, if satisfied that the interest of the public will be best served by such revocation. Usual duties and powers. No bond requirement noted; no requirement to keep official records noted. 

Must have seal to attest official acts, and certificate must show expiration date of commission. State-wide jurisdiction. Clerks of the superior courts may act as notaries in their respective courts, certifying under seal of the court. Failure to show date of expiration of commission does not invalidate official certificate. By acts of the legislature in 1945 notarial acts formerly done which lacked full compliance with legal requirements were declared validated. 

Commission fee, $3. 

North Dakota 

To be eligible to appointment as notary public, man or woman,

must have at time of appointment the qualifications of an elector as to age, time of residence and citizenship. Commission fee $5. 

Six year term under appointment by the governor. State-wide jurisdiction. $500 bond, oath. Usual duties and powers. Certificate must show expiration date of commission. Must keep official protest records. Upon vacancy records go to county clerk. 

Shall provide himself with an official seal bearing his name and shall deposit an impression of such seal, together with his oath and bond, in the office of the secretary of state and file his commission in the office of the clerk of the district court of county of which he is a resident, and deposit with such clerk an impression of his seal together with his official signature. After which the clerk may authenticate his acts. Ohio

Must be citizen of Ohio, resident of county for which appointed, 21 or older. Women not inhibited. If resident of city or village situated in two or more counties of Ohio, may be appointed for all the counties in which such city or village is located. Licensed attorney and counselor at law may be appointed with state-wide jurisdiction. The governor may revoke commission upon presentation of satisfactory evidence of misconduct or official incapacity. 

Oath and bond, $1500. 3-year term. Must keep records to be deposited with county recorder in event of death or expiration of term without reappointment. Seal shall have engraved thereon the emblem of the state of Ohio, the words, "Notary Public," or "Notarial Seal," or words of like effect, the name of such notary public and the county or counties for which he is commissioned, or, for an attorney commissioned as notary for the whole state, the words, "State of Ohio." Has usual notarial powers. Before appointment, applicant shall produce to the governor a certificate from a judge of the common pleas court, court of appeals or supreme court, that he is of good moral character, a citizen of the county in which he resides, and if an attorney that he is an attorney at law and "duly qualified and admitted to practice in this state, and possessed of sufficient qualifications and ability to discharge the duties of the office of notary public." Judge must be satisfied from personal knowledge, or from examination, before giving certificate. Acts done after expiration of commission may be valid. The name of the notary public and the limits of his jurisdiction may, instead of appearing on the seal, be printed, typewritten, or stamped in legible, printed letters near the signature of such notary on each and every document by him signed.

Oklahoma

Appointment for 4-year term, by secretary of state. Man must be 21 or over; woman eligible if 18; citizenship required. One statute is as follows: "Before entering upon the duties of his office every notary public so appointed and commissioned shall file in the office of the county clerk, in his capacity as clerk of the District Court, of the county in which such notary resides at the time he is commissioned, the commission issued to him, his oath of office, his official signature, an impression of his official seal, and a good and sufficient bond to the State of Oklahoma in the sum of One Thousand Dollars ($1000.00) with one or more sureties to be approved by the said court clerk, conditioned for the faithful performance of the duties of his office. Such commission, bond and oath shall be recorded in the office of such court clerk as clerk of the District Court, and immediately thereafter said commission shall be returned to the Notary, and said bond and oath shall be transmitted and recorded in his office. The filing of such commission, bond, official signature, and impression of official seal in the office of the court clerk shall be deemed sufficient evidence to enable such court clerk to certify that the person so commissioned is a notary public, duly commissioned and acting as such, during the time such commission is in force." $1 fee to secretary of state, $1 to court clerk. Required to have official seal, showing his name and place of residence. Certificate must show date of expiration of commission. Required to keep protest register, and fair record of other official acts. Upon vacancy records go to county clerk. May hold other office and still be notary public. (This is to be distinguished from other office conferring notary ex-officio status.) Oregon

Appointment for 4-year term, by the governor, unless sooner removed by the governor. Notaries public declared to be considered as state officers. $500 bond. State-wide jurisdiction. Usual duties and powers. Must be 21 or older and citizen of U. S. and of Oregon. Commission fee, $5. Must furnish county clerk of county of his residence with his official signature and an impression of his official seal, whereupon county clerk is authorized to authenticate his official certificates. Certificate must show date of expiration of commission. Impression of seal, together with oath and bond to be deposited in the office of the secretary of state. Must keep records. Upon vacancy, records go to county clerk.

Pennsylvania

Commission fee, $25. "The term of office for all notaries public shall be for four years, if they shall so long behave themselves. **** the governor shall appoint and commission a competent number of persons, of known good character, integrity and abilities, as notaries public, for the commonwealth of Pennsylvania, to reside within such place or places, within this state, as the governor shall, in and by the commissions direct **** no person shall be commissioned as a notary public, who shall not have resided within this commonwealth two years, next previous to his appointment." (An 

Act of March, 1791.) Women, if 21 and citizen of the commonwealth, are eligible. The statute, however, provides: "Whenever any female notary shall marry, she shall, before the performance of any notarial act, return her commission to the governor, stating the fact of her marriage and giving her married name, and the governor shall thereupon issue to her a new commission," etc. New bond required. Must keep fair register of official acts. Usual powers and duties. Certificate must show expiration date of commission. Upon vacancy, records go to county recorder of deeds in county where notary public resides, or resided, if deceased. Bond from $3000 to $10,000 to be approved by the governor. Seal required, on which shall be engraved the name, surname, the words "Notary Public," and location of the office of the notary using the same. Must keep an office in the borough, town or township of the county named in his commission. State-wide jurisdiction. 

Puerto Rico

Justice of the peace may perform notarial acts. Lawyer of good moral character, citizen of P.R. or U.S., 21 or older, may be appointed notary. Appointment is by supreme court based upon examination. Commission fee, $5. $2500 bond. Must keep record and report monthly to supreme court clerk. Island jurisdiction.

No term limitation. Notary and parties may use English if familiar therewith. 

Rhode Island

Usual duties and powers. No bond required. No seal required. 

State-wide jurisdiction. Record required of important notarial acts. Must be qualified elector to be eligible. Commission fee, $10. Term expires July 1, fifth year after appointment, unless sooner removed. May be removed by governor after due notice and hearing. 

South Carolina

Appointed by governor on endorsement of legislator. Usual

duties and powers. State-wide jurisdiction. Required to have seal, though omission of same from his official certificate does not invalidate, if official title is affixed. Acts by notaries in other jurisdictions must be under seal to entitle to recognition in this state. Bond stipulated, with sureties to be approved. Certificate not required to show date of expiration of commission. Term is at governor's pleasure. Notary who is also licensed attorney is not disqualified to act as both attorney and notary in a case. 

South Dakota

Men and women eligible if citizen resident in state. State-wide jurisdiction. Appointment by governor to four-year term unless sooner removed by the appointing power. $500 bond. A statute reads in part as follows: "It shall be unlawful for any notary public to affix his official signature to documents when the parties have not appeared before him." Notary required to keep his commission, or a duplicate, posted conspicuously in his office. 

Shall provide an official seal and file an impression of the same together with his oath and bond in the office of the secretary of state. Records of protests and names of parties to be kept, and upon vacancy such records go to clerk of the circuit court in the county of such notary's residence. Usual duties and powers. Tennessee

Commissioned by the governor on election by the justice of the county court, for four-year term to run from date of commission. 

Bond, $5000. Usual duties and powers. Certificate of acknowledgment is required to show date of expiration of commission, but failure to make such showing does not invalidate the certificate, though such failure does render notary liable to fine of $25 to $100. Required to keep office in the county for which appointed. 

County jurisdiction, but may act as notary in other counties by filing therein his autograph signature and certificate of county clerk certifying to his election, commission and qualification. Shall at his own expense procure a seal of office. Required to keep record of official protests. Women eligible if 21 or older in certain counties, in others if 18 or older. Texas

The secretary of state shall appoint "a convenient number of notaries public for each county of the State. Such appointments may be made at any time, and the term of all appointments made shall end on the first day of June of each odd numbered year.' 

Appointee, man or woman, must be at least 21, citizen of the state and resident of the county for which he is appointed. County

jurisdiction only. Usual duties and powers. $1000 bond. Not required to show date of expiration of commission on official certificate, but is required to print or stamp name under official signature, though failure to do this does not invalidate acknowledgment. 

Required to keep official records. Justice of the peace is notary ex-officio, but must qualify and give notary bond before serving. 

Official seal to have in the center a star of five points and "Notary 

Public, County of............. Texas," in the margin, with name of county filled in. Utah

Appointment by the governor, for four-year term. Appointee must be over 21, have the qualifications of an elector, resident of Utah and citizen of the U. S. State-wide jurisdiction. $500 bond. 

Usual duties and powers. Must keep fair record of protests, names of parties to transactions. Shall have an official seal, with which he shall authenticate all his official acts. Such seal must contain the words, "State of Utah," and "Notary Public," or "Notary

Seal," with his surname and at least the initials of his Christian name. Notary's official certificate must show his signature, his official title, his place of residence and date of expiration of his commission. Vermont

By virtue of their offices, county clerks and town clerks are notary public. A statute reads as follows: "The judges of the county court may appoint as many notaries public for the county as the public good requires, to hold office until ten days after the expiration of the term of office of such judges, whose jurisdiction shall extend throughout the state." Appointee must have seal of office. Women are eligible. No bond requirement noted. Two year term. County clerk is charged with the duty of notifying the secretary of state of notary appointments. Usual duties and powers. Virginia

Virginia notaries are of three kinds in a geographical sense, being for city, county or state at large. A statute provides that the governor shall appoint in and for the several counties and cities of the state as many notaries as to him may seem proper, for four years, *** who shall exercise the powers and functions of conservators of the peace, and who shall be removable by the governor at will for misconduct, incapacity or neglect of official duty." Notary is not required to be resident of county or city for which appointed. The governor **** may appoint the same person to serve

for two or more counties and cities, in which case only one commission shall be issued and the fee for issuing the same shall be three dollars for the first county or city and five dollars for each additional county or city, for which such notary is appointed to serve. 

A notary for a city shall also have authority to act as such in cities contiguous thereto." Commission fee for state at large, $25. 

Bond $500. Any person, man or woman, over 18 is eligible. Usual duties and powers, also conservator of the peace. Certificate must show expiration date of commission. Must keep record of official acts. Washington

Appointment by governor for four year term. Usual duties and powers. Commission fee, $10.00. $1000 bond. Appointment must be petitioned for by at least ten freeholders of the county where applicant resides, but for reappointment after expiration of first term, no freeholders' petition necessary. Must keep records, and shall: "Procure a seal on which shall be engraved the words, "Notary Public" and "State of Washington," and date of expiration of his commission, with surname in full and at least the initials of his christian name." It shall not be necessary however, to use seal in certifying an oath to be used in court. 

West Virginia

Applicant for appointment shall obtain from county court of his county a certificate showing him to be a person competent to perform the duties of such office, of good moral character, and a resident of the county. Appointee may be removed in the manner prescribed by law. Appointment is by the governor for ten year term. Notary duly appointed and qualified is conservator of the peace within his county and as such may exercise the powers of a justice of the peace. Official notarial certificate must show date of expiration of commission. Seal is stipulated for, but not required on certificates to affidavits, acknowledgments, depositions, oaths, or his acts as justice of the peace. Usual duties and powers. 

Bond $250 to $1000. Must keep records of official acts. County jurisdiction, but may be extended to other counties upon compliance with legal requirements. Wisconsin 

Four year term. Appointment by governor. Only qualified elector, of good moral character, with common school education, resident of county and familiar with duties of notary, can be appointed. State-wide jurisdiction. A statute approved July 6, 1945, is as follows: "Every notary public shall take and file the official

oath and execute and file an official bond in the sum of $500, with surety to be approved by the county judge or clerk of the circuit court of his county, or, when executed by a surety company, approved by the secretary of state. *** If the notary desires to have the expiration date of his commission appear upon each impression of his seal he may proceed as follows: Deposit the oath and bond with a statement of his post-office address in the office of the secretary of state. His commission shall issue and the secretary of state shall deliver to such notary a certificate of his appointment, stating the date his commission will expire, which, together with his autograph and an impression of his seal containing a statement of the date when his commission expires, in which statement the word "commission" may be abbreviated to "comm.", shall be filed in the office of the clerk of circuit court of the county wherein the notary resides. Such impression, together with the notary's autograph, shall likewise be filed in the office of the secretary of state. The impression of such notary's seal bearing the statement hereinbefore mentioned upon any instrument or writing or upon wafer, wax, or other adhesive substance affixed to any instrument or writing shall be deemed an affixation of the seal and a writing or stamping or adding of the date when his commission expires and of his county. Such impression, together with the notary's signature alone, shall be for all purposes as effective as if the signature therein contained were separately set out elsewhere in any instrument or writing, by any means or medium whatsoever, including the designation of his office. **** If the notary does not desire to have the expiration date of his commission appear upon each impression of his seal he may proceed as follows: Provide an engraved seal, which shall make a distinct and legible impression on paper, giving his name, office and county and shall deposit an impression of the same, together with his said oath and bond and a statement of his post-office address, in the office of the secretary of state, and thereupon his commission shall issue, and the secretary of state shall deliver to such notary a certificate of his appointment stating the date when his commission will expire, which together with his autograph and an impression of his official seal, shall be filed in the office of the clerk of the circuit court of the county where he resides. Not less than 30 or more than 60 days before the expiration of his commission the secretary of state shall notify by mail every notary of the time when his commission will expire. Notaries public whose commissions have not expired at the effective date of this amendment (1945) and those who may later desire to do so, may add to their seals such devices as will enable them to impress the date of the expiration of their commissions therewith.”

Wyoming

Appointment by the governor. Appointee, to be eligible, man or woman, must be 21 or over, of good moral character, citizen of the united States, an actual resident of the state of Wyoming, and of the county in which he seeks to be commissioned, and able to read and write the English language. Federal clerk, secretary or stenographer, eligible. Bond, $500. Certificate must show date of expiration of commission. State-wide jurisdiction. Statute is in part as follows: “*** shall appoint in each county upon petition of not less than five reputable freeholders of the county where such applicant resides as occasion may require, one or more notaries public, who shall hold their office for four years unless sooner removed according to law." Required to keep records which, upon vacancy, go to register of deeds in county of notary's residence. Seal required, with which all official papers shall be authenticated, and upon which shall be engraved the notary's name, the words "Notary Public", and the name of the county wherein he resides and the word "Wyoming." Woman notary who marries while in office may continue to serve, but shall file with the secretary of state a certificate of her marriage and thereafter sign her name as formerly but with her husband's surname added.

Chapter 

OFFICIAL FEES 

Q. What fees may a notary charge for his official services?

A. Only those fees which are prescribed for specific official acts and which are fixed in amount by general law in each State and by Federal law in U. S. jurisdictions.

UNOFFICIAL FEES 

Q. Are there unofficial fees or earnings which a notary may lawfully and honorably charge?

A. Yes, the general rule is this: For services performed by request, not part of his official duties, and which could be as appropriately performed by any other person, he may lawfully charge and receive remuneration. 

Q. What would be an example or instance of such service?

A. If a notary is asked to leave his office to go out and take an acknowledgment, or perform any other notarial duty, his time and expense of travel are proper items of unofficial charge. 

In some of the States a limit is placed by law upon what he may charge for travel; Missouri, for example, fixes the limit at 8 cents per mile, and thereby makes the same an official charge.

FEES FOR DRAWING PAPERS 

Q. What about writing a paper, a deed or contract or affidavit?

A. (1) In some of the states, for example, Utah and Wisconsin, a notary may lawfully charge for writing an affidavit, as well as for his certificate or jurat thereto, and in nearly all the states he may also charge for writing a deed, contract, corporation charter, will or other business paper, as well as for his certificate of acknowledgment thereto. No statute has come to the writer's attention fixing the amount of fee which a notary may charge for such a service except in case of an affidavit. In those jurisdictions where the fee for writing an affidavit is fixed by statute, such a fee becomes official, and the notary should charge the exact amount so fixed. (2). In any state where he is not forbidden by law to do so, the notary may lawfully charge for his services in writing business papers, as well as for his certificates thereto. In

America much of the people's business is carried on outside the cities and larger towns, in parts of the country where the services of any attorney are not always immediately available. 

Q. In those states where he is forbidden to draft instruments for pay, may a notary nevertheless write such papers if he does not charge any fee?

A. In some instances, but not always. If in doubt, consult a competent local attorney.

DIVISION OF FEES 

Q. May a notary lawfully divide his official earnings with another person; for example, may he pay to some other person part of his fees for bringing business to him? 

A. (1) In absence of a local statute or rule forbidding such practice, he may lawfully make such division. (2) Not uncommonly a corporation, such as a bank, or trust company or insurance company, may make a trade with a notary that the notary shall be an employee and shall receive as his compensation, all or part of the notarial fees that come to him by reason of such employment. In such instances of arrangements of these kinds as have come to attention of this writer, the parties have seemed satisfied, and no dispute or controversy has grown out of them, and unless a local statute or rule forbids, there is in such arrangements nothing unlawful, unethical or unbusinesslike.

NOTARY DE FACTO'S EARNINGS 

Q. May a Notary De Facto collect official fees?

A. The only authoritative court declarations upon this question that have come to attention of this writer, assert that the notary de facto can not at law collect official fees. Of course if the patron wishes to pay him, there would seem no reason why he should not do so, since it is the patron's money that is changing hands.

NOT OBLIGED TO CHARGE 

Q. Suppose a notary does not wish to make a charge for his official or unofficial services. May he lawfully donate such services?

A. Yes, he may, if he wishes, donate both his official and unofficial services.

FOR WHAT AND HOW MUCH 

Q. For what specific acts and in what amount may a notary charge for his official services? 

A. See the fee schedules of the various states hereinafter set out. 

LUMP SUM CHARGES 

Q. May a notary lawfully "lump" or combine his official fees with his unofficial earnings?

A. He may of course name a lump sum for his entire charge for any job given him. But in lawsuits which have occurred the courts have said that the notary, as a public officer, should keep his official earnings itemized and separate and apart from those that are unofficial. In presenting a bill, or filing a suit for unpaid services, without any doubt each item that is official should be kept clearly separated from those that are unofficial. If a notary writes a chattel mortgage, charging $2 therefor, and certifies its acknowledgment, for a charge of 50 cents, he may properly collect $2.50, but if he renders an account or statement, the items should be stated. 

FEES AND PUBLIC SALARIES 

Q. Suppose a public officer, for example, a mayor, or city secretary, or county clerk, or sheriff, or other, or a deputy or assistant of either, to be a notary. May a notary in such a position, charge official or unofficial fees for his services as notary?

A. Ordinarily such a deputy or officer will be receiving a fixed salary from the public treasury. In absence of any rule or statute forbidding him to do so, he may lawfully charge for either official or unofficial services. Of course as a matter of sound public policy all such official earnings should be openly shown, so that the public may know what the public servant is earning, and from what sources he receives his pay. 

Instances are on record where notarial earnings in such a situation, as for example, private secretary to a county judge, far exceeded the salary paid, and led to exciting campaign repercussions. The Federal law, noted elsewhere in these pages, is that no notary public on the Federal pay roll may charge for notarial services to any of the persons mentioned in that law.

EXTORTION 

Q. May a notary charge for any specific service a greater amount than is by law provided? 

A. No. 

Q. What is such an overcharge called? 

A. Commonly called extortion. 

Q. What is the penalty or punishment for extortion?

A. In every state whose laws were searched upon the point, there are criminal statutes forbidding extortion, with severe penalties. Also, in event of proof of extortion, the person imposed upon may recover money damages from the notary and the sureties on his official bond. 

WHAT LAW GOVERNS? 

Q. If a notary receives from a state other than that of his residence, papers to be executed before him and certified by him, which schedule of fees should he follow, that of his own state or that of the state from which the papers have come? 

A. (1) If the matter is one governed by a state fee schedule, he should charge the fees fixed in the schedule of the state where he lives. (2) If the matter is out of a Federal court or of Federal interest, he should charge U. S. fees, insofar as the same are fixed; otherwise those fixed in the schedule of his own state. 

OFFICIAL FEE RECORDS 

Q. Is a notary required to keep a record of official or unofficial fees charged by him?

A. In some of the states a notary, as well as every other public officer, is required by law to keep a public record of the fees charged by him. However, extended inquiry discloses that some notaries do not live up to this requirement of the law. 

Q. Has the matter of notaries' fees been before appellate courts for discussion and analysis?

A. Yes, numerous times. An eminent authority (Corpus Juris, Vol. 46, page 530), upon exhaustive review of what the courts have stated, thus condenses and re-states some of the rules announced:

CORPUS JURIS QUOTATION 

"The fees of notaries are fixed by statute, which must be strictly construed, and their charges for official services must be limited to the prescribed fees. They may make further charges for unofficial services. but the charging of a round sum for notarial and other services together is not permissible. A city ordinance which attempts to deprive of his fees a notary employed by the city and to substitute therefor a fixed salary is void as conflicting with the statute relating to notaries' fees, and a notary affected may recover the statutory fees from the city. Under a particular statute the failure of a notary to keep a register of his official acts or to enter therein certain acts was held to prevent recovery of fees for his services. A notary's right to fees for passing an authentic act may be proved by the instrument itself, signed, and executed by the parties. In general, a de facto notary can recover no fees. 

FEE NOT EARNED, WHEN "A notary is not entitled to a fee for an act done by him for a person not duly authorized, nor is he entitled to receive the fees for writing an affidavit or deposition when the work is actually done by a stenographer furnished by the party. He can not recover statutory fees for doing unnecessary and useless acts which he had no occasion to perform, as the preparation of lengthy verbose agreement for sale reciting many irrelevant matters, or the protesting of an inland bill not required to be protested; nor for doing notarial acts in an improper manner, as administering an oath in a form or model different from that required by statute. But if the impropriety or inefficacy of his act is due to the fault of the person who employs him, he is entitled to receive his fees as if the act had been properly and sufficiently performed."

WRITING PAPERS 

Chapter 3 

FORMS 

Q. These days when one writes a business paper what is likely to be his first move? 

A. To reach for a form book or a blank form. 

Q. Is the use of blank forms and form books to be commended?

A. Whether so or not the custom is now too firmly fixed to be changed or disturbed. 

Q. May a notary safely use form books and blank forms?

A. Generally, yes. True there may be incorrect or inadequate forms, but this writer knows of none such that are sold by reputable concerns.

Q. If printed forms for business papers may be safely used, then what need is there for instructions or suggestions to a notary for the writing of such papers?

A. There are three vital and essential features of a business paper which commonly can not be included in a printed form, for the reason that they will not be known at time of the printing, but are learned afterward, when it is desired to consummate or evidence some step or transaction by the paper's execution and delivery. These features are, first, the names of the parties; second, the consideration supporting the deal; and, third, the description of the property affected or involved. It is thought, therefore, that attention may properly be given these features, as is hereinafter done. 

BUSINESS PAPER 

Q. What is meant by the expression, "business paper"?

A. As used in these pages it is intended to include written instruments to which a notary may be asked to attach his official certificate. 

Q. Why have business papers?

A. Chiefly to evidence dealings in property of one or another kind, either as relating to a sale or creation of a lien.

PROPERTY, REAL AND PERSONAL 

Q. Into what classes is property divided?

A. Two classes, real property and personal property. Sometimes we hear of "mixed" property, but properly speaking all property is either real or personal. 

Q. What is real property?

A. (1) Real property is land and the fixtures thereon, such as a farm with its fences, barns, corrals, stables, etc., or a ctiy building with the ground upon which it rests. (2) Minerals in place in the earth, such as oil, gas, coal, gold, sulphur, etc., are real property, but when mined and removed they become personal property. 

Q. What is personal property?

A. Personal property is every kind of property except real. It includes every kind and class of movable wealth such as live stock, machinery, money, books, clothing, stocks of merchandise, household goods and the like. 

Q. Why does it seem proper here to give these definitions and distinctions?

A. Because any useful and intelligent study of the make-up of business papers involves first of all an understanding of the different classifications of property and the reasons for different kinds of papers in dealing with them. 

Q. What is title? 

TITLE 

A. (1) The word "title" signifies ownership of property. We say that one has title to property, personal or real, and we mean thereby that he has ownership thereof and is entitled to its dominion and control. (2) "Title" also is used to denote the written evidence of such ownership. Thus, we say that one has an abstract of his title and we mean that he has a compilation of the various instruments in writing which show his title. Or we say that he has a certificate of title and we mean that some authority has certified that he owns the property covered by such certificate. (3) Business papers which a notary will be asked to certify will in nearly all instances involve "title" in some way. 

This does not mean that all such papers necessarily relate to

WRITING PAPERS 

property titles, because such is not the case, but the very large majority of such papers will in some way relate to or involve a property title, either real or personal. 

TITLE, BY WHOM DETERMINED 

Q. By whom is title to be judged, or determined?

A. Title judging is not the field of the notary. It is strictly and positively the field of the lawyer. One who is not trained in title law should not meddle in title disputes, or presume to pass upon title validity. More often this is to be emphasized in real estate matters, but it may also call for attention in personal property dealings.

TITLE: DISTINGUISHED 

Q. What is meant by the expression, good title?

A. The courts say that good title is title free from litigation, palpable defects, and grave doubts; that it consists of both legal and equitable titles, fairly deductible of record, its holder having the right of possession. 

Q. What is meant by the expression, bad title?

A. "Bad title" is an expression resulting from misuse of words. 

There is no such thing as a bad title. A title may be weak, or imperfect; it may be effective as to only an interest or fractional part, but if it is title at all, it is good title. There may be one or several or a dozen different owners of property, each holding a fractional part or interest, but the title of each, if title at all, is good title; it may easily be that neither has a perfect title to the whole property, but each may nevertheless have a perfect title to a stated part or interest, and it is not correct to state that either has a "bad title", though the expression "bad title", is a perfectly common and ordinary one in our every-day speech.

MERCHANTABLE, MARKETABle, Title 

Q. What is meant by merchantable, or marketable, title?

A. These expressions are used synonymously, and are a bit difficult of exact definition. Merchantable or marketable title is title free from reasonable doubt; a good record title, not merely a title that can be made good. Neither expression, apparently, is intended to denote a clear and perfect record title but, rather, one that a court of equity considers to be so clear that it will enforce its acceptance by a purchaser. Ordinarily, if the contract of sale calls for a marketable title, then if the

court finds the title to be as just described, the seller will be adjudged to have furnished what he contracted to furnish, and the buyer will be ordered to accept the deed and pay off. 

FEE SIMPLE, LESSER, TITLES 

Q. What is a fee simple title to real estate?

A. Fee simple is a perfect title, combining in its holder the whole of the written or printed record title, together with possession and the right of possession. 

Q. What titles are of lesser strength than fee simple?

A. Many, such as homestead, curtsy, dower, easement, emblement, joint tenancy, which is where several persons hold property jointly among them in equal shares by purchase; tenancy in common, where lands and tenements are held by different and distinct titles, but occupancy is common; trust estates; life estates; remainders; water rights; rights-of-way; timber rights; dedications for public use, such as streets, alleys; mineral conveyances or reservations, widow's exemptions, etc. 

TITLE, HOW ACQUIRED 

Q. How is title acquired?

A. Title is acquired in either of two ways, namely: 

1. By purchase. 

2. By inheritance.

When used in this connection the word "purchase" is an elastic and meaningful one. It includes not only the acquisition of property bought outright, but also property acquired by gift or in trade. It includes within its meaning even the acquirement of property through hostile and adverse possession, or "limitation." Property acquired in any other way than by inheritance is said to be acquired by purchase. 

TITLE, HOW CONVEYED 

Q. How may title and ownership of property be conveyed? 

A. (1) Title and ownership may pass by inheritance, but no reason is perceived for going into that angle of the subject here, since inheritance is the field of the lawyer, not of the notary. (2) Title and ownership of property may pass under order of court-that's what courts are for-but that angle also, is the lawyer's.

WRITING PAPERS 

83 

THE NOTARY'S CONCERN 

Q. With what kind of conveyance is the notary concerned?

A. Title and ownership of property, real or personal, may be voluntarily conveyed by means of instruments written or printed, in the form and the manner prescribed and followed in law and by custom. It is with conveyance in this manner that the notary public is concerned, and it is with this type of conveyance that these pages primarily deal. 

Q. What is meant by "lien"?

LIENS 

A. A lien on property is simply the right to subject the property to sale, proceeds of sale to be applied on the debt which the lien secures until the debt is satisfied, remainder of proceeds of sale belonging to debtor. It is purely a creature of the law and does not exist except where its creation and existence have been authorized by law. A lien may be created to secure performance of an obligation or trust, as well as payment of money. It exists in practically every case, anywhere, when property, real or personal, is charged with payment of a debt, or held for the faithful performance of a duty or task. 

Q. Are there different kinds of liens?

A. Several; commonly differentiated as statutory, common-law, maritime, equitable, contract, constitutional. 

Q. Is lien the same as ownership?

A. It is not. One may not own property and at the same time have a lien thereon; the two estates are repugnant and irreconcilable.

AN IMPORTANT SUBJECT 

Q. Is the subject of liens important to a notary public?

A. Yes, very important. "Lien," as an instrumentality for the support of credit, is known and used in one form or another throughout the world. To say that it is what makes industry "click" is hardly an exaggeration, for without the facilities that its use affords, business would slow down to the point of stagnation unless and until other dependable means of security might be devised. "The cattle on a thousand hills," sang the ancient poet, "They are the Lord's." But the earthly owner of such cattle will in most cases owe the banker over in town, or someone else, a sum of money secured by a

lien on the said cattle, and without the convenience which the lien affords, he'd never have been able to buy, or feed or market them. "The Englishman's home is his castle," declared a later writer, and no other doctrine of the English law has been more firmly adopted into the American judicial system. But though the house may be its owner's "castle," nevertheless, the carpenter, the bricklayer, the painter, the electrician, the plumber and the workman-each of these will have as law-given security for his pay a lien under which the owner may be ousted if he fails or refuses to pay. So, too, has the lumber, paint, brick, glass, wire or other materialman, as well as the former owner of the soil, a lien guaranteeing pay for his contribution to the "castle's" erection and construction. Without the credit provided by the lien the owner could not build; without the confidence its strength inspires, neither the workman would risk his skill, labor or time, nor the materialman or landowner his extension of credit or help. With many other similar, or dissimilar, references the necessity and importance of the "lien" may be illustrated. And whenever a lien is created, fixed, assigned, extended, divided, foreclosed, or released, by any written instrument, a notary's services are likely to be called into play to certify the acknowledgment or affidavit of the person executing such instrument of fixation, assignment, extension, division, foreclosure or release.

MORTGAGES, DEEDS OF TRUST 

Q. What is a mortgage?

A. (1) Commonly when we speak of a mortgage, a mortgage on realty is meant. A mortgage on personalty is ordinarily mentioned as a chattel mortgage. A mortgage, then, may be defined as a conveyance by deed of real estate by the owner, who is both debtor and mortgagor, to one who is his creditor and who is called mortagee, the conveyance being intended as a pledge, guaranty or security that the mortgagor will repay to mortgagee the money borrowed, or that he will do or not do a certain stipulated thing; with further proviso that if the mortgagor does the thing promised to be done, or refrains from doing the thing promised to be not done, that the conveyance thereupon will be void and of no effect; or upon failure to pay, or to perform, the deed of conveyance becomes absolute and gives the title to mortgagee. In simpler words, a real estate mortgage is commonly given to secure payment of money borrowed, and mortgagor's failure to pay, will give the creditor, or mortgagee, the property.

WRITING PAPERS 

85 (2) Usually a note or bond is given with the mortgage, evidencing the debt. Often the note or bond will be sold or transferred, necessitating transfer of the mortgage lien, and the use of a notary's services. (3) In many of the states, Texas, for example, use of the mortgage form is being, or has been, practically discontinued in favor of the deed of trust form, which is by many considered a more satisfactory form than the straight mortgage. The deed of trust is made to a third party, with power of sale, allowing attorneys fees, costs, commissions, etc. (4) The real estate mortgage, the lien which it secures, and developments under it, result in very frequent use of the notarial certificate and seal.

LIENS OF MECHANICS, OTHERS 

Q. What is the chief purpose of creation or reservation of such liens as are given by Mechanics, Materialmen, Laborers?

A. Same as that of the mortgagor, the borrower, in any line, or anywhere, namely, to give security for money loaned. A mechanics lien, or a tax lien, or a laborers lien, or a materialman's lien is but a mortgage in another form. 

LIEN ASSIGNMENT, Transfer 

Q. What liens, and what part of their respective obligations, if any, are transferrable?

A. Practically all liens, and the debts they secure, are transferrable. This is true under the laws of practically all jurisdictions, though there are exceptions to the general rule. 

LIEN DESCRIPTION 

Q. If a notary is called upon to write a lien description in a mortgage, or deed, or release, or transfer, or contract of sale, or any other instrument, are there, and what, features to which he should give special attention? 

A. (1) Remembering that many, many deals in both personalty and realty are initiated and carried through to a finish without a contract of sale; nevertheless, commonly the contract of sale is the first writing that evidences an agreement of the parties. And nearly always, the contract of sale is written by a notary who has negotiated the parties into a trading state of mind. At the moment when the parties' minds have met and it is time to draw the instrument evidencing that fact, an attorney may not be immediately or conveniently available, and

the notary goes ahead on his own. He should know what to write into the blank lines if he's using a form, and if not using a form he should know how to write the whole instrument. (2) If by the agreement a lien, whether mortgage, or some other, is to be dealt with, it should be clearly and definitely mentioned and described. If to be paid off, so state; if to be assumed, so state whatever the agreement, put it in black and white. 

(3) If the instrument under preparation is a deed, deed of trust, mortgage, or any other, the lien description should state the kind of lien, that is to say, whether contract, materialmen's, mechanics', statutory or other; should state the exact amount of money it is given or reserved to secure, manner and time of payment thereof, names of all parties to the instrument, careful description of the real estate and all other property, if any, affected by the lien; if labor or materials are to be furnished, same should be specifically mentioned; in nearly all jurisdictions, if the lien is to rest upon a homestead, or community property, or the wife's separate property or there is dower or curtsy involved, both husband and wife should join in execution of the instrument. In those states where a wife's separate acknowledgment is required, no chance should be taken of imperiling or de-vitalizing the lien by overlooking or ignoring that provision of the law. (4) The same careful attention should be given to drawing a chattel mortgage as to drawing a mortgage on real estate, particularly with reference to description of the lien, the debt to be secured, and the property upon which the lien is being placed.

WRITTEN INSTRUMENT NECESSARY, WHEN 

Q. When is a written instrument necessary to effectuate a transaction, business or other?

A GENERAL RULE 

A. (1) The general rule is that any transatcion may be verbal unless the law requires that it be in writing. No doubt the greater part of the world's business is carried on by word of mouth. As one instance consider sales of merchandise in stores day by day-practically all of it verbal. But human experience long ago demonstrated the necessity for writings, and almost countless laws have followed, prescribing rules

WRITING PAPERS 

87 and regulations for the giving of such writings. Obviously it is not possible within the space available here to differentiate fully between all business transactions, but it is thought that the following explanations will be helpful. 

REAL PROPERTY 

(2) In all American jurisdictions it is required that a sale of land or a lien on land be evidenced by a writing; that there are certain minor exceptions to this rule is true but these exceptions are of rare application, and with them the notary is not officially concerned. This requirement of a writing applies whether such sale be in city, town or country. 

Likewise it applies if the sale be of all or only an undivided interest, or a part, such as the minerals in the soil, or timber growing on the surface. Further, it applies to any contract for the sale of such land. The instrument evidencing any such transaction or contract, may properly be certified by an officer (notary or other). It must be so certified or otherwise proved or authenticated if intended for public record or for use as evidence in court. In some of the states, if the conveyance be one involving the homestead, or the wife's separate property, the instrument is wholly ineffective if not properly certified, whether intended for record or not and whether intended for use as evidence or not. See the chapter on Certificates and Acknowledgments. 

SUBSEQUENT InstrumenTS 

(3) A conveyance of real estate, land, will be by deed. A transaction relating to a lien may be evidenced by a reservation of such lien in the deed, or by separately executed deed of trust or mortgage. Execution and delivery of the deed will close that part of the transaction, so far as the notary's services are concerned; the land has sold and "that is that." 

Not so with a lien, which may be afterward assigned, transferred, waived, released, renewed or foreclosed, either of which steps may call for a certificate. The lien exists only in support of a debt or obligation; it can not outlive the debt or obligation, though it may be outlived thereby; that is to say it may, for any one of several reasons, be released before the debt is paid or the obligation satisfied. In other words the debt may survive the lien, but the lien can not survive the debt. And while it remains alive, a notary's services may be invoked at any time for a certificate relating to or involving such lien.

PERSONAL PROPERTY 

(4) In all American jurisdictions, the general rule with reference to a sale of personal property, or any act dealing with a lien thereon, is that unless the law provides for a written instrument, the transaction may be verbal. But the instances where a writing is required by law are very numerous. Thus the motor vehicle is commonly liened or sold only by written instrument. Live stock too, ordinarily can be legally sold or liened only by written instrument, at least as against an innocent purchaser for value and without notice. In some states, household furniture can be sold or liened only by written instrument. Many other instances of necessary writings will occur, and again it is time to remind that it is solely and only with written instruments that the notary is concerned. If the deal, whether of lien or gift or sale, is by word of mouth or order of court or by inheritance, so that no written acknowledgment or affidavit certificate is required of him, the notary needs give it no thought or study in connection with his notarial duties. Without doubt by habit and custom the laws requiring written instruments to evidence deals in personalty are very generally disregarded, because the parties regard them as useless and unnecessary. 

BILL OF SALE, CHATTEL MORTGAGE 

(5) If a writing is used in the sale of personal property it will be called-in most places-a bill of sale. If intended for record or for use in evidence such bill of sale should be certified. If not so intended, in some instances the certificate is required anyhow, while in others it is not. In any event, when the sale has been closed, the personalty delivered and the bill of sale duly certified, the notary's interest ceases. "That is that." But not so with a lien on such personalty. 

The lien may be a reserved one, for payment of purchase money, or a created one to secure a loan, in either of which instances a chattel mortgage is ordinarily necessary. But execution and delivery of the chattel mortgage do not close the notary's possible connection with it, because thereafter the lien is still alive. Like the real estate lien it may in the future be assigned, transferred, waived, enforced, renewed, released. 

The chattel mortgage is not the only form that a lien on personalty may take, but any lien on personalty is nevertheless a chattel mortgage, whether technically so called or not. 

Liens on personalty are also created and enforced by means of pledge, bailment, etc.

WRITING PAPERS 

89 

CONDITIONAL SALE 

Q. What is meant by conditional sale?

A. (1) A sale may be conditional or it may be absolute. A bill of sale is the written evidence of a sale of personal property. 

A conditional bill of sale is a form of chattel mortgage, by another name, or reservation of lien, by another name, under which personal property, usually merchandise, is transferred from one owner to another upon provision or condition that the ownership shall come back and re-vest, upon the doing or not doing of some certain thing, or the paying or failure to pay of certain moneys, or the happening or not of a certain contingency. It is quite generally used in the automobile business. Goods sold on the installment plan are quite commonly sold under a form of writing conditional in form. (2) Practically everywhere a conditional bill of sale is signed and acknowledged, or sworn to before a notary or other officer, and such acknowledgment or oath duly certified, and the instrument thereupon filed for public record. 

"PERSONAL" INSTRUMENTS 

Q. What is meant by a “personal” conveyance?

A. A "personal" conveyance is one wherein the person who signs the instrument acts as an individual, for himself or herself. 

This is of course the most common form of instrument presented to a notary for certification. 

"OFFICIAL" INSTRUMENTS 

Q. What is meant by an "official" conveyance?

A. (1) One may sell or incumber or otherwise deal with the title to property for some other person or organization or group and such a conveyance will ordinarily be called an "official" one. Thus a guardian selling property for his ward, under court order, delivers an "official" deed, because he is acting in an official capacity; or a sheriff, selling a tract of land under writ of execution, delivers an "official" deed, because the sale he makes is an official one, not personal; he acts in the capacity of an official of the government, and not merely as an individual citizen. If the person so acting as guardian, at another time sells for himself a tract of his own land, the instrument which he delivers will be a "personal" one; likewise if the person so acting as sheriff, at a different time sells a tract of his own land, acting for himself alone,

the instrument which he delivers will be a "personal" one, not "official". (2) Obviously, official conveyances will include many others than merely those of a sheriff or guardian; acts of adminis trators and executors, trustees, receivers, constables, commissioners, and others, are so classified. 

IMPORTANT TO NOTARY 

Q. For the notary, what is the most important fact about a written instrument presented to him for certification?

A. That there is a name signed, which he must certify. 

SIGN, DEFINED 

Q. What is meant in this connection by the word "sign"?

A. We quote from Black's Law Dictionary: 

(1) "To affix one's name to a writing or instrument for the purpose of authenticating it, or to give it effect as one's act." (2) "To affix a signature, to attach a name, or to cause it to be attached, to a writing by any of the known methods of impressing a name on paper." 

(3) "To affix a signature to; to ratify by hand and seal; to subscribe in one's own handwriting.' (4) "To make any mark, as upon a document, in token of knowledge, approval, acceptance or obligation." (5) "To 'sign' is merely to write one's name on paper, or declare assent or attestation by some sign or mark, and does not, like 'subscribe', require that one should write at the bottom of the instrument signed." 

SIGNATURE, Defined 

Q. What is meant by "signature"?

A. We quote from Black's Law Dictionary: 

(1) "The act of writing one's name upon a deed, note, contract, or other instrument, either to identify or authenticate it, or to give it validity as one's own act.' (2) "The name of a person as written or set down by himself." (3) "A sign, stamp, or mark impressed as by a seal, or the name of any person written in his own hand, signifying that the writing preceding it accords with his own wishes or intentions."

WRITING PAPERS 

91 (4) "A printed, typewritten, or rubber-stamped name may constitute a valid signature." (5) "The 'signature' to a deed may be made either by the grantor affixing his own signature, or by adopting one written for him, or by making his mark, or impressing some other sign or symbol on the paper by which the signature, though written by another for him, may be identified." (6) "The word 'subscribe' is more restricted than the word 'signature'. The word 'signature' in its origin involves merely a sign. The word 'subscribe' involves a writing. The signing of a written instrument has a much broader and more extended meaning than attaching one's written signature to it implies. When a person attaches his name or causes it to be attached to a writing by any of the known methods of impressing his name upon paper with the intention of signing it, he is regarded as having 'signed' in writing."

SIGNATURE, NOTARY'S DUTY 

Q. Is it any part of the notary's business or duty to see that the grantor's name is signed in proper form?

A. It is clearly proper for the notary to see that the signature is in the right place, wherever that place may be, whether at the end or the middle or the beginning, or some other part of a written instrument. The signature is not always necessarily appended to the bottom or final end of a written instrument. How the signer signs his name is his affair and not the notary's. It is the notary's duty, however, to see that he (the notary) writes into the body of the instrument, if he draws it, the name of the signer spelled just as the signer spells it, and it is the notary's duty to see that his certificate spells the name of the signer exactly as the signer spells it.

SIGNER'S SIGNATURE, FORM OF 

Q. Are there rules of law prescribing how any person's name shall be signed to any written instrument?

A. There are some such rules, and there are certain kinds and classes of papers which require specified forms of signature. 

It is not possible within this limited space to distinguish and classify all such instruments. In the state of Louisiana the notary is required to see that the signer's Christian names

92 are spelled in full, as well as the surname. In many formal papers for governmental records, state and Federal, it is required that one's name shall be written in full, first, middle, and last name. The more common and more convenient way, perhaps the best way, in ordinary business papers, is to spell the first name in full, give the middle initial, and spell the last name in full. Remembering always that how the signer signs his name is the signer's affair and not the notary's, there remains, nevertheless, the fact that the notary may properly and without officiousness offer to a signer a suggestion as to how a proper name may be best signed to a written instrument. 

Q. Are there any further points with regard to the signature that the notary should carefully watch?

A. (1) He should see that each name is properly stated and correctly spelled, that it is written and spelled the same way in the signature, the body of the instrument, and in his official certificate, and that the place of residence of the signer is stated. (2) If any party is acting in an official capacity, as official capacity is elsewhere explained, the fact should be clearly stated in the body of the paper, as well as in the certificate. 

MARITAL STATUS, CertificATE 

Q. Is it proper or necessary for a notary to mention in his certificate the marital status of a party to a paper? 

A. If the party, or either of the parties, is a woman, the certificate should state clearly whether she is married or single. 

Likewise, it is commonly advisable, but not often necessary, that the marital status of any man signer be mentioned in the certificate. Under the laws of Louisiana it is required that the marital status of all parties, male and female, shall be stated. The rule is a good one. 

MARITAL STATUS, INSTRUMENTS 

Q. Is it advisable that the body of the written instrument show the marital status of any party or parties to such instrument? 

A. Every business paper which a woman signs and to which a notary's certificate is desired, should state whether she is married or single. If she is married, it should refer to her as being "the wife of............", naming her husband. If she is not married, that is to say, if she has never been married, or if her husband has died, or if she has been divorced, in either of

WRITING PAPERS 

93 these circumstances she may be described in the paper as "a single woman" or "a feme sole".

Q. If a written instrument is executed by a husband and wife, is it proper or necessary to mention them as husband and wife? 

A. It may not in every case be necessary, but it is certainly proper and advisable to so refer to them. If the written instrument is primarily the act of the husband, then it should say "(naming him), joined therein by his wife, (naming her)", or if it is primarily the act of the wife, then it should name her as such actor and state that she is joined therein by her husband, naming him.

MARRIED WOMAN'S SIGNATURE 

Q. How should a married woman sign her name to a written instrument? 

A. (1) She should sign her maiden name and her husband's surname. If she has a middle initial or name, it is well enough to add the same. When she marries, she takes her husband's surname, but not his given name. If Ada Brown marries John T.

Smith, she may become, for social purposes, Mrs. John T. 

Smith, but legally and for her own protection, she is Ada 

Brown Smith, and her name should be signed "Ada Smith" or "Ada Brown Smith". The "Mrs." is no part of her name, but is simply a form of address which millions of others use; it may be signed with the name or omitted, without in any way affecting the validity of the signature. (2) It is not intended in the foregoing to imply that it would be unlawful or improper for her to sign as "Mrs. John T.

Smith," but only to state that it is better to sign with her maiden name.

Several reasons exist. An illustration may point out one of them. Suppose Ada Brown married John T.

Smith, and they have four children. During the marital union, in a state where the community property status is recognized, they acquire a valuable tract of land. Ada dies. 

John marries again, this time to Mary Butler. In course of time an oil lease is given on the land signed by John T. Smith and his wife, Mrs. John T. Smith. Now, under the laws of descent and inheritance, when Ada died, one-half of the land passed to her children, but the buyer of the oil lease, having obtained a written instrument signed by John T. Smith and 

Mrs. John T. Smith, and having assumed that there was only one Mrs. John T. Smith, would have a toe-hold, at least, on the interest in the land owned, not by the second wife, but by

the children of the first wife, and thus Ada's heirs might find themselves faced with an expensive and difficult law suit. If the lease were properly signed by Mary, the abstract of title would show the date of marriage of John and Ada, the date of acquisition of the land, and the marriage of Mary and John after the land had been acquired, thus showing that Mary had no interest, and the rightful interest of the heirs would be better protected. (As stated, this illustration is posed for a community-property state.) (3) Numerous court opinions in point and supporting the foregoing might be cited. One directly in point is that of the 

Supreme Court of Alabama, in the case of Roberts v. Grayson, 173 Southern Reporter, page 38, wherein the court says: "A married woman's name consists, in law of her own Christian name and her husband's surname.' 

(4) Finally, it may be stated that the reason for any person having a name is to identify that person, and that a married woman may be identified by either her own or her husband's 

Christian name, added to her husband's surname.

INTRODUCTORY IDENTIFICATION 

Q. Is there any general rule for identification of a party, in the body of the instrument?

A. (1) Obviously any and every party to any written instrument should be sufficiently described to enable ready identification. (2) If John T. Doe, an unmarried man, or Laura May

Smith, an unmarried woman, executes a written instrument, the beginning (identification) may read somewhat as follows: "That I, John T. Doe, an unmarried man residing at 

416 Delta Street, Louisville, Jefferson County, State of 

Kentucky," etc., and the signature would be "John T. Doe." 

Or, "That I, Laura May Smith, an unmarried woman, residing at 314 South Dewey Avenue, Indianapolis, Clarke County, State of Indiana," etc., and the signature would be "Laura May Smith." (3) If John T. Doe and his wife, Julia Jones Doe, are executing a written instrument, the beginning may be somewhat as follows: "That I, John T. Doe, joined herein by my wife, Julia

Jones Doe, both residing at 2100 East Grand Avenue,

WRITING PAPERS 

95 

Des Moines, Wilson County, State of Iowa," etc., and the signature would be: 

John T. Doe 

Julia Jones Doe.

CORPORATION 

Q. What about corporation identification and signature?

A. A corporation may execute a written instrument only by and through some individual who is clothed with authority. Ordinarily the president signs and acknowledges or verifies its papers but this authority is sometimes delegated to another or others than the president. If Doeville Mercantile Company, a private corporation, wishes to execute a business paper, it may begin somewhat as follows: "That Doeville Mercantile Company, a private corporation, duly incorporated under the laws of the state of Texas, having its domicile and principal place of business at Doeville, Doe County, Texas, acting herein by and through its president and general manager, Robert 

J. Bullick," etc., and the signature would be: 

Doeville Mercantile Company 

By Robert J. Bullick, Pres. and Gen. Mgr. attested by the corporation secretary and with the corporate seal attached. The notary's certificate would be that required for corporate acknowledgment. This is not intended to imply that when a corporation executes a written instrument, it must always do so with such formality. Instances are easily imagined where a paper might read thus: Doeville 

Mercantile Co. sells to Jones & Smith two carloads of merchandise, signed, Doeville Merc. Co., by some agent, and be sufficient. But where formality is thought necessary or advisable, the company should be named, place of its principal office stated, and the name and official position of the officer or employe through whom it is acting should be stated. 

Stating that the corporation is incorporated under the laws of a certain state facilitates accurate identification and does no harm.

PARTNERSHIP 

Q. How about partnership identification and signature?

A. A partnership paper may be executed by all of the partners, or by one or more, if there be several. If John T. Doe, a member of the firm of Doe & Roe, wishes to execute a writ-

ten instrument for and on behalf and as the act of Doe & 

Roe, it may begin somewhat as follows: "That Doe & Roe, a partnership, consisting of John T. 

Doe and Richard J. Roe, partners and doing business (or trading) under the name of Doe & Roe, at Memphis,

Shelby County, Tenn., acting by and through John 

T. Doe, a member of said partnership, who resides at 

711 West Poplar Street in the city of Richmond, State of Virginia," etc., and the signature would be: 

Doe & Roe,

By John T. Doe. 

SIGNATURE BY MARK 

Q. If one is unable to write, how may he sign a paper?

A. The signature of a person unable to write, either because of illiteracy or physical disability, is taken and certified in the same manner as that of a person not thus incapacitated. Such signature, though, is by "mark", or "cross" in lieu of the written name, or as supplementary to the written name. Statutes of the states generally authorize signature by mark or cross, but none was found specifically prescribing how such mark or cross may be made. By custom which seems universal and long established a signature by mark is expected to consist of two diagonal cross marks with the name of the signer and the words "his mark" written into the pattern. It may appear in this form: His 

John X Smith Mark

Neatness and appearance of the paper will be improved if the cross marks are so drawn as to leave the signature, that is to say, the name, resting on the line of writing, the word "his" above the line and the word "mark" below the line. If requested to do so, the notary may draw the cross marks and write the name for the signer, but he should do this only upon request, and when he does it, should require the signer to take hold of the pen while the mark is being made. A reason for this precaution is that a signer by mark, like a signer by name, will sometimes deny his signature, thus causing embarassment to the notary, and he is somehow less likely to so deny if he himself holds the pen while the mark is being made. 

If a signer by name denies his signature, handwriting experts and other disinterested means may be used to settle

WRITING PAPERS 

97 the resulting dispute, but if a signer by mark denies his signature, facilities for establishing the truth are not so ready. A stranger or bystander may make the mark for the signer, or a witness or witnesses may be called upon to sign, thus fortifying the notary's position in event his certificate of acknowledgment is challenged.

SIGNATURE BY ANOTHER 

Q. May one person legally sign another's name to a business paper?

A. Yes. If authority, or permission, has been given previous to the signing, it is accepted as legal. Also, if no authority has been given, but the person whose name is signed, after such signing "adopts" the signature as his own, it will be legal and binding. A may sign B's name to a paper without authority, and if B denies and repudiates the signature, it is of course not valid or binding upon B. But if B adopts the signature as his own and assents to A's signing his name, then it is and becomes B's signature as perfectly and effectually as if he himself had first signed it, and he may legally and properly acknowledge it to the notary.

THE ENGLISH LANGUAGE 

Q. Is there any requirement of law that business papers shall be written and signed in English?

A. In all states it is required that the public records be in 

English. If therefore a paper is to be recorded, its record will be facilitated if it is in English. A certified translation may be recorded, however. In some of the states, Louisiana and Texas, for example, certain records originally were in other languages than English. Also in certain Federal jurisdictions-Hawaii, the Philippines, Puerto Rico, etc. Legality of a business paper is not destroyed by the fact that it happens to have been drawn and signed in other than the English language.

THE DATE 

Q. How important is it to show the date of execution of a business paper?

A. Very important; should always be shown; notary should see to it, if overlooked by the parties.

SUNDAYS AND HOLIDAYS 

Q. May a notary legally certify a business paper on Sunday or a legal holiday? 

A. Yes, generally, but there are a few exceptions.

DESCRIPTION OF PROPERTY 

Q. In describing the property affected by a written instrument, what should be the writer's aim?

A. To describe the property so clearly that it may be readily identified and pointed out on the ground. If the description attains this purpose it is sufficient, whether lengthy or brief, and whether the property be personal or real. 

Q. How important is the property description in a business paper?

A. Very important. A correct and sufficient description does not lead to misunderstanding or litigation. Imperfect, inaccurate or incomplete description has led to many lawsuits. 

Q. Will less than a perfect description suffice?

A. Sometimes, yes. The rule is this: "If the description within its terms or words suggests inquiries or means of identification, which, if pursued, will disclose the property affected, it will be sufficient." This rule is based on the maxim that "that is certain which can be made certain." Whether the property be real or personal, it should be described, the place where it may be found should be stated and the person in possession should be named, so that a third person, though a stranger, may find the place mentioned and go there and select and identify the property. 

DESCRIPTION MAY BE ADOPTED 

Q. Must the description be original, or may a description be adopted from another paper or record?

A. (1) A description may be original or it may be adopted. 

If real estate is to be described, and the same has been previously described sufficiently in a deed, mortgage, will or other instrument which is public record, then the writer may adopt the said recorded description, stating in what record, volume and page it may be found. The safety of this method obviously will depend upon the sufficiency of the description so adopted. (2) If personal property is to be described, and the same has

WRITING PAPERS 

99 been previously sufficiently described in a bill of sale, chattel mortgage, will or other paper which is on file or public record, then the description will be sufficient if it cites and adopts by reference the description in the said filed or recorded instrument. Such adoption might read thus: "And being the same property more fully described in a chattel mortgage on file in the office of the county recorder of Willis county, Montana, dated March 10, 1943, signed by Henry

R. Jones, mortgagor, in favor of First National Bank of Lewiston, mortgagee.' (3) A property description should not be adopted from an older paper unless such older paper is somewhere on public file or record, or unless its permanent preservation is in some other way assured. Nor is it intended here to commend the practice of adopting property descriptions, but only to explain that a description by adoption may be sufficient in some instances.

PERSONALTY 

Q. What data may a personal property description properly include?

A. (1) It should state the name and address of the owner, or who claims to be the owner. (2) Name and address of person in possession should be stated, and location of the property should be given, not only the state and county, but as well the locale, on street or road or highway; in a warehouse, store, or garage; pasture; oil field; mine, factory, shipyard, boat; lumberyard, etc. If consisting of different parts or units, that fact should be stated and explained. (3) If the property is any kind of manufactured article, such as a watch or piano, a wagon, an automobile, motorcycle, airplane, oil field equipment, harvesting machinery, office equipment, household furniture, or a group of such articles, each item, or class of items should be listed and described. (4) If the property is of live stock, horses, cows, goats, sheep, swine, anything else on foot or wing, state the number, ages, sex, approximate heights, weights, brands, if any; ear marks, if any; flesh marks or blemishes, if any; approximate values, any other pertinent identifying information. (5) If the property is a crop, or part of a crop, after stating on what farm or ranch same is to be planted, grown, harvested, state what year or season, and what part of the crop is

dealt with. Thus if the mortgage is on the fourth, fifth and sixth bales of cotton or on the tenth and twelfth bags of rice or the third and fourth carloads of wheat, so state; if the bill of sale is of the whole crop for a year or a season or for more than one year or season, state that fact. (6) Paper and ink do not cost much; take plenty of time, be sure you've got everything in; be sure that the parties understand fully what they're about, and there'll be fewer "hereafters".

REALTY 

Q. How may real property be correctly described?

A. (1) Real estate-land-in a city or town may in perhaps all cases be described by reference to a map or plat, and lot and block number. True, where only a part of a mapped or platted unit is affected, "field notes" become necessary. "Field notes," is a term well understood, and thus defined by a supreme court: "the notes made by the surveyor in the field, while making the survey, describing by course and distance and by artificial marks found or made by him, where he ran the lines and made the corner." (2) Land outside a city or town, land anywhere which is not mapped or plattel by map or plat of record in official place or places, will require a greater amount of descriptive information than is necessary for mapped or platted areas. (3) If the land, whether urban or suburban-in the city or country has been mapped or platted into lot, block, or parcel units, and entire units are affected, then ordinarily no other description is required than statement of the number or name of such unit, and reference to the said map or plat, naming the volume and page where found. (4) In those states and areas where farm or ranch landssuburban properties of any class-are describable by section number, range and townsite, the description is so simple as to require no comment. (5) In states and areas where original grants from sovereignty of the soil were in irregular shape, and of not standard size or amount, and where through the years such grants have been divided, subdivided, re-combined and subdivided again, difficulties in description have grown. (6) One describing land by metes and bounds or by field notes will find the following suggestions helpful: Be sure of your starting point, and be sure that your described boun-

WRITING PAPERS 

101 daries or lines get back to that same point, and that your lines or field notes, close.
