Do not fail to mention natural objects, if any, along either line, such as rivers, springs, mountains, hills, trees; or artificial objects such as fences, railroads, highways, monuments, houses, etc. In contests involving field notes, natural objects hold first place, artificial second, course (direction) third and distance, fourth. The metes and bounds are stated in different units of measurement in different localities, miles, yards, feet, poles, chains, varas, inches. The use of other than one unit in a description is to be avoided. (7) Remembering that real property descriptions are ordinarily easier and more simple than personal property descriptions, nevertheless, if the notary has doubts of his ability to properly describe the land dealt with, then he should take the job to someone who can show him how it ought to be done. (8) Here are some of the things that may be shown in the description, where needed or proper: Name of state and county in which the land is situated; name of original grantee; townsite, range and section number and subdivision or sectional part; abstract number; certificate number with name of authority by which issued, date and place; date of issuance of patent, by what authority and volume and page of public record where same may be found; number of acres, with more or less clause if not pretty definitely known; whether occupied, and if so, by whom; whether improved, and if so the general nature and kind of such improvements. 

THE CONSIDERATION 

Q. Why is recitation of consideration necessary?

A. (1) A fundamental rule of law is that every sale or incumbrance of property must be supported by a consideration; a contract or promise without any consideration is not binding upon the parties and cannot be enforced. Want or failure of consideration is ground for recission or cancellation of a contract, since as to a person who receives nothing whatever in exchange for property the transaction may be deemed fraudulent. These legal facts are pointed out in order to emphasize the importance of an accurate recital of the consideration in any paper which a notary may be called upon to certify. (2) On occasion it will happen that the parties do not wish to state the actual consideration, but only one that is legally sufficient and in such case it is of course proper for the notary to oblige them, since the responsibility is theirs, not his.

A recital of a fictitious consideration, however, may be inquired into on a court trial, and the actual consideration made known. (3) Recital of the consideration may take any one of a wide number of forms. It may be a stated sum in cash only, or a stated sum in cash and other valuable considerations, or a stated sum in cash and a note or notes, with or without express lien; or services, or love and affection, or assumption of existing encumbrance; or one or more notes without cash, or exchange of property with or without other consideration, or oil payments, etc. Very important in any event that it be stated accurately and clearly. (4) If a note is given, or several notes, the recital should state whether such notes are secured by lien, the date, the amount, the due date or dates, interest rate, place of payment, names of payor, payee, etc. If attorneys fees and acceleration of payment are provided, these also should be mentioned. While it is true that some creditors purposely omit from the instrument any detailed description of the note or notes, the better practice undoubtedly is to describe fully. (5) If payment of a note is assumed, or property is taken subject to such note; if the instrument is an extension of time of payment of a note, or a transfer or release of a note and lien-in either of these events, description of the note should follow that in the original instrument, so that there may be no mistake as to which obligation is affected. (6) Obviously it is not practicable within the limits of a book of this kind to present in full a description of every consideration that may be paid or given. Where land is being sold for part cash, balance on time, payment secured by lien, the description may be somewhat as follows: "in consideration of the sum of One Thousand ($1,000.00) Dollars to us paid and secured to be paid by John Doe, as follows, One Hundred ($100.00) Dollars cash in hand, receipt acknowledged, and Nine Hundred ($900.00) Dollars evidenced by the said John 

Doe's nine (9) certain promissory notes, each for the principal sum of One Hundred ($100.00) Dollars, of even date herewith, signed and delivered by the said 

John Doe, payable in one, two, three, four, five, six, seven, eight and nine years after date respectively, to 

Richard Roe, or order, at San Antonio, Texas, bearing interest from date until paid at the rate of six per cent (6%) per annum, interest payable annually as it accrues

WRITING PAPERS 

103 and all past due interest to bear interest at the rate of 10% per annum, secured by vendors lien herein expressly retained on the land and premises herein conveyed, providing for ten per cent additional on the whole amount due as attorney's fees if placed in the hands of an attorney for collection after maturity or if collected through probate court or any other judicial proceeding and providing that failure to pay either of said notes or any installment of interest thereon when due shall, at the option of the holder of said notes or either of them, mature all of said notes," etc. (7) When a vendors lien is retained to secure payment of a note or notes, the following or some similar clause should be inserted somewhere in the instrument, preferably, but not necessarily, following the warranty clause and above the signature: "But it is understood and agreed that a vendors lien is expressly retained against the above described property, premises and improvements until the above described not.............paid in full, both principal and interest, when this deed shall become absolute.' 

ANY LAWFUL PROVISION, SPECIAL AGREEMENTS, ETC. 

Q. What special agreements, or reservations or restrictions may be written into a business paper?

A. It is fundamental that contracting parties may write into a business paper any lawful provision that pleases them, and this applies to deeds, mortgages, and deeds of trust as well as other instruments. If they wish to incorporate special agreements, as for the planting and harvesting of certain crops, or the pasturing of certain breeds of livestock, or the payment of taxes or insurance, or the maintenance of sewers or fire escapes, or covering other like points, they are free to do so. 

The only duty of the notary is to furnish efficient and attentive service.

RESERVATIONS AND RESTRICTIONS COMMON 

Formerly a deed was a deed; it passed title, or evidenced the passing of title, and that was that. Not so, now; at least not in all cases. The deed still passes title, some title, but no longer does it stand in all instances for the passing of all titles. Times and conditions have changed and the complexities of life have multiplied, so that even the commonplace conveyance of real estate has felt the impact and surge of

the intensified struggle for existence that daily living has become. Reservations of interest and restrictions of use have grown common and one will write few real estate papers these days without in some way contacting one or another of these practices. The parties may incorporate in the paper practically anything they desire with reference to reservations and restrictions.

INTEREST RESERVED, OIL, TIMBER, CROPS, Easement 

Reservations of interest will more often cover oil, gas or other minerals, occasionally other things such as standing timber, or growing crops, or easements, or water rights, or roadways. Restrictions of use will more often relate to the size, cost, exact location and appearance of houses or other structures to be erected on the land, but occasionally to other things, such as occupations forbidden by law or which menace public health, peace or morals.

VENUE SHOWN IN INSTRUMENT 

Q. What is meant by "venue" in a business paper?

A. The venue is that part of the paper showing where it is being executed. This will ordinarily be shown twice, that is to say, at the top of the paper, and just preceding the signature. Thus: 

The State of Indiana 

County of Marion and just preceding the signature: 

Witness my hand at Indianapolis, Indiana, this .... day of …………………………., 19……....... 

The place, county and state, named in the beginning and the place named in the signature line should correspond, though one should name a state and county and the other a state and a city or town, or at least, a postoffice.

OFFICIAL CERTIFICATES 

Chapter 4 

DEFINITIONS 

Q. What is meant by the word "certify"?

A. From Black's Law Dictionary we take these definitions: "To testify in writing, to make known or establish as a fact." a. b. "To vouch for a thing in writing." c. "To give a certificate, or make a declaration about a writing." 

Q. What is meant by the word “certificate"?

A. From Black's Law Dictionary we take these definitions: 

a. "A written assurance, or official representation, that some act has or has not been done, or some event occurred, or some legality complied with." b. "A declaration in writing." c. "A writing by which testimony is given that a fact has or has not taken place." d. “A statement by a public officer, written and signed, but not necessarily or customarily sworn to, which is by law made evidence of the truth of the facts for all of or certain purposes. 99 

NOTARY'S MOST IMPORTANT WORD 

Q. What is the notary's most important word?

A. The notary's most important word is "certify". The notary's official duty is to certify. When he understands the official meaning of the word "certify," he has made progress toward notarial efficiency. Until he has such understanding, he should not attempt to do notarial work unless some informed person is present to show him how. Two other words of prime importance to the notary are "acknowledge" and "swear (or affirm)." Not that he either swears or acknowledges or affirms, for officially he does neither, but his numerous and important notarial certificates will be given because others have acknowledged or sworn before him.

CERTIFY, SIGNATURE, SEAL 

Q. What does it mean for a notary to certify a paper?

A. The statutes of the different states which require a notary to certify a written instrument all mean that he must sign the certificate officially, and in practically all of the states that he shall attach his official seal as a part of his official certificate. As indicated above, the legal meaning and the ordinary meaning of the word "certify" are identical. To certify is to testify in writing, to make known or establish a fact. Thus, a bank's officer may certify a check, that being the bank's testimony that the check is good, or election officers may certify election returns, that being their official testimony that the election resulted a certain way. So a notary may take and certify an acknowledgment or an affidavit, that being his official testimony that there was such an acknowledgment or such an oath. 

CERTIFICATE, DECLARATION 

Q. What does a notary's official certificate signify?

A. As is indicated above, a certificate is a declaration in writing, the usual and customary method to indicate that which has or has not been done. Thus, a certificate of deposit declares that deposit has been made; a land certificate declares that the owner thereof has been entitled to receive land. A notary's certificate declares that some person has appeared before him and has acknowledged or sworn or affirmed. 

CERTIFY, ACKNOWLEDGE, SWEAR

Q. Why is it necessary to distinguish so clearly between the words "certify," "acknowledge," "swear"?

A. That the notary's official duty is to certify can hardly be stated too often. He does not acknowledge, nor does he swear. He only certifies that some one else appeared before him and acknowledged or swore or affirmed. "Certify" is one word, "swear" is another word, and "acknowledge" is yet another. These words are not synonymous. They are not even of similar meaning. There is no reason for confusing them. The word

The word "affirm" is ordinarily included within the meaning of the word "swear." The acknowledging and the swearing or affirming are done by those who appear before the notary for that purpose, not by the notary himself. As stated, the notary's duty is to certify. Quite true, he fills in blanks, administers oaths to witnesses, may

OFFICIAL CERTIFICATES 

107 issue a subpoena to a witness, may deliver a demand for payment as part of his protest activities, or may do other things of like nature preliminary or preparatory to the affixing of his official certificate. But his important act is his certificate, and any or all of these incidental things are of no consequence unless and until he finally signs and seals. 

At most, they are done merely to pave the way for his certificate.

OFFICIAL CERTIFICATE AUTHORIZED, WHEN 

Q. When may a notary give his official certificate?

A. (1) When a person or persons appear before him and tender to him a written instrument signed by such person or persons, who state to him that they have signed the said instrument, and that they acknowledge its execution, conforming such acknowledgment to the language of the certificate required by the laws of his jurisdiction, he may officially certify the acknowledgment of such instrument. (2) When a person or persons appear before him with a written instrument duly signed and tell him that oath is made thereto, he may properly attach to such written instrument his jurat or official certificate to an affidavit. (3) When he has properly taken the deposition of a witness or witnesses, he may attach his certificate thereto, as more fully explained in the chapter on depositions. (4) When he has performed the necessary preliminary steps and the facts exist which justify him to do so, he may issue his certificate of protest on commercial paper. (5) When any person has requested or demanded of him a certified copy of any of his official records, he may furnish such copy and attach thereto his official certificate. (6) If in certain jurisdictions there are laws which provide for other certificates by him, he may give such certificates when and if called upon to do so. 

MAY CERTIFY IF SIGNED, DATED, PROPER FORM 

Q. In taking and certifying an acknowledgment, or in administering and certifying an affidavit, has the notary any duty other than to attach his official certificate?

A. Before he can safely give the certificate, there are several facts which he should make sure of: a. That he has before him the identical person named in the instrument and named in his certificate.

b. That the instrument is signed by the same name or names shown in the body thereof. c. That the instrument is correctly dated, and if shown to be dated and signed at any given place, that the said place is stated correctly. d. He may properly glance over the instrument briefly to ascertain whether or not there is a description of the property, a recital of the consideration, and a proper showing of the names and addresses of the party or parties to the instrument. True, the exact contents or the general purpose of the instrument may be none of his business, but he has a right to know that the instrument which he is certifying at least appears to be in proper form, and it is his duty and obligation to avoid certifying a blank instrument. 

CERTIFICATE FORMS 

Q. Are there certificate forms prescribed by law for every notarial need?

A. Elsewhere in these pages will be found certificate forms for many notarial needs. Some of the state laws prescribe the form of certificates in those states. But these certificates are not the same in all the states; that is to say, acknowledgment certificates are not identical in all the states, jurats o affidavit certificates are not the same in all states, deposition certificates are not the same in all states, etc. 

FORMS FOLLOWED 

Q. Where a certificate form is prescribed by statute, must the notary follow that form exactly?

A. Of course he should follow it exactly, but the holding of the courts in practically all of the states has been to the effect that a statutory certificate need not be followed exactly, but must be followed substantially. 

CERTIFICATE ESSENTIALS 

Q. What are the requisites of a notary's official certificate?

A. (1) Certain features must appear in the certificate, or it will be worthless. If properly drawn, it will show venue, date, the notary's signature and seal, in those states where seal is required, and will show the official style or title of the notary himself, and it will properly identify the paper which is being certified.

OFFICIAL CERTIFICATES 

109 VENUE (2) The venue of a certificate is that part of it which shows where the certificate is made. Ordinarily it appears at the beginning or caption, thus: 

The State of Colorado 

County of Wray

Or it may be shown in the official signature, thus: 

Notary Public in and for 

County, Arkansas

Or if relating to a Federal matter, thus: 

United States of America 

District of Missouri

It is sufficient if shown in either place, but the better form is to show the venue in both the caption and the signature. 

The chief necessity for this feature lies in its implication that the notary is acting within his own jurisdiction. He has no authority to act except as authorized by law, and the law has not authorized him to act in any other than one jurisdiction. Simple as this venue matter may seem, it nevertheless has been the pivotal point in many appealed law suits. It is expected that venue will be shown both in the caption of the certificate and in the official signature. The venue should be shown in the instrument itself. In absence of any other showing, the seal may be looked to for the venue if there is a seal and if it shows a venue. By the expression above used, that the venue should be shown both in the caption and in the signature, it is intended to imply that the notary, following his signed name, will show the state or county or territorial jurisdiction within which he is commissioned to act, thus: 

John T. Doe,

Notary Public in and for Union County, Pennsylvania. DATE (3) In some cases where the certificate showed no date, the courts have held that there was no certificate. In other cases, certificates have been upheld which showed no date. Obviously the certificate should be dated, and the notary who omits to date the certificate or to see that the instrument iself is dated is guilty of negligence in the performance of his official duty.

OFFICIAL TITLE 

(4) Obviously the official title of the notary should be shown; that is to say, that he is a notary public of a certain state and the place of his residence. In some of the states it is required that his post office address be given; in others, only that the county of his residence be stated. Clearly it is better that he show his official title and his place of residence. SEAL (5) In those states where a seal is required, no certificate is complete until the seal has been impressed on the certificate. 

NOTARY'S SIGNATURE 

(6) The notary's signed name is of course essential to the certificate. Without his signature, there is no certificate.

This would seem so obvious that one is prompted to wonder how a test case could arise; yet there have been many cases taken to court because a notary failed to place his signature on the certificate. In every case which this writer has examined, the court has ruled that if the signature is missing, there is no certificate. Though he may have filled all blanks, affixed his seal, and collected his fee, yet if he did not sign, then he has not certified. The Supreme Court of Texas in one opinion said: "The instrument was wanting the signature of the officer before whom it was executed. The signature of the notary, it may be said, is that which gives an instrument executed before him its character as a public instrument, and it cannot be considered authentic without it. Covenants made before a notary public carry along with them the proof of their truth by the signature of the notary."

As is more fully explained elsewhere in these pages, especially in the chapter on writing papers, one's signature may be personal or official. The form of a personal signature is too well understood to require analysis. 

INSTRUMENT IDENTIFIED 

(7) It (the certificate) must mention and identify the written instrument which is being certified, thus: "The above and foregoing deed," or "The attached contract in writing,” or other like expression.

OFFICIAL CERTIFICATES 

111 

NOTARY IDENTIFIED 

(8) The body of the certificate should identify the notary. 

These, or like words, "Before me, John J. Smith, a notary public in and for Beaver county, Montana," etc., may be used.

WHAT CERTIFICATES LAWFUL 

Q. What certificates may a notary lawfully give?

A. Only those certificates which he is authorized or required by law to give. A certificate which a notary may give which he is not authorized or required by law to give is of no cogency or weight in court. This does not mean that he is forbidden to certify any other papers than those which he is authorized to certify. It is not improper for a notary to certify a fact, or a copy of a paper from records other than his own, or a paper which is not even recorded. He might certify a copy of a private letter as being a copy of a private letter. The value of such certificate would depend entirely upon the circumstances of the case. A certificate to the copy of a probate letter would not render such letter admissable in evidence. Five kinds of certificates the notary is generally authorized to give: First, acknowledgments; second, affidavits; third, depositions; fourth, protests; fifth, copies of his own official records. In some of the states some other minor certificate matters are entrusted to him, but generally speaking, these five will cover the whole field of his notarial certificates.

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS 

Chapter 5 

DEFINITIONS AND DISTINCTIONS 

Q. What is acknowledgment?

A. (1) Acknowledgment is a proceeding provided by law whereby a person who has executed an instrument in writing may, by going before a competent court or officer and declaring it to be his act and deed, entitle it to be publicly recorded and to be received as evidence without further proof of execution. (2) To "acknowledge" is to confess or admit some prior act; the signer of a paper, therefore, who "acknowledges" an instrument, "confesses" or "admits" or "declares" that he did execute such instrument. (3) To perfect an acknowledgment two persons and only two are necessary, namely the signer who acknowledges that he did sign, and the officer who certifies that the signer did so acknowledge. Both are indispensible to an acknowledgment because until the signer does so acknowledge there is nothing for the officer to certify, and upon the other hand, the signer's acknowledgment is of no effect or consequence until the officer has certified it. True, several persons may sign and acknowledge the same instrument, but they are as one person in such case. (4) Quite commonly the "act" of acknowledgment and the "certificate" of acknowledgment are mentioned interchangeably as "acknowledgment." If the meaning is clear, this may make no difference, yet it is well to understand clearly that the "acknowledgment" is wholly the act of the signer, who acknowledges, and that the "certificate" is wholly the act of the officer, who certifies. (5) Acknowledgment differs from affidavit in this, that acknowledgment verifies only the signature to a written instrument, that is to say, its execution, while an affidavit verifies the contents of the instrument itself. See the chapter on Affidavits. (6) "Verification" is an expression commonly used to denote the use of an oath or affidavit. "Acknowledgment" is not "verification," as that term is commonly used. Acknowledgment verifies the execution of the instrument but does not

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS 

113 verify its contents; "verification" is generally used to verify contents, not execution. (7) "Jurat" is an officer's certificate in the form of a simple statement that an instrument has been subscribed and sworn to, or affirmed, before him. Jurat does not involve the element of acknowledgment; the two are not identical in meaning and should not be confusing in the reader's mind. (8) An attestation is distinguishable from acknowledgment in that it is the act of a third person, not of the signer or the officer. (9) Of the origin of acknowledgment, there seems no reason for searching further back than a date prior to the founding of the American Colonies, when it was in general use in England. Its history is one of steady growth. (10) In some jurisdictions “acknowledgment" is more commonly called "probate."

WHAT LAW GOVERNS 

Q. What law governs acknowledgments?

A. The law of the place where the written instrument is to be used. Thus, for illustration, if a written instrument affects the title to land in Nebraska, but is itself to be executed in Florida, then the acknowledgment and its certificate should conform to the laws of Nebraska. Ordinarily a certificate should be so drawn as to fit the law of the place where it is to be used, whether property is involved or not. If no property is involved, the certificate may in some instances properly conform to the law of the place where it is drawn, even though intended for use in a different forum. If to be used in the same jurisdiction where drawn, it should conform to the law of that place.

PURPOSE 

Q. What is the purpose of acknowledgment?

A. The purpose of acknowledgment is to "authenticate" the instrument acknowledged. The general rule is that when a paper is acknowledged-authenticated-it is thereby rendered: 

1 Entitled to be publicly recorded. 

2 Entitled to be received in evidence without further proof of execution.

This general rule, it will be perceived, is subject to some restrictions and exceptions, but these are few in number and of minor importance. The acknowledgment and the certifi-

cate of the notary (or other officer) are said to "authenticate" the paper because they identify the signer and verify his signature. There are other means of "authenticating" a paper than by acknowledgment. Of this more anon. Likewise there are uses for acknowledgment other than to entitle the paper to public record or to facilitate its use as evidence, but it will be perceived upon due thought that these purposes embrace within their meaning the major uses to which acknowledgment is put. EFFECT 

Q. What is the effect of acknowledgment?

A. (1) The general effect follows the general purpose, namely, to entitle the instrument to public record and render it admissible in evidence without further proof of execution. The purpose and the effect in any jurisdiction are whatever they are declared to be by the law of that jurisdiction. An acknowledgment is of course intended to show that the signer (grantor) executed the instrument; it is not intended to show that the grantor owned the title conveyed; ownership of the title is provable if at all by other means. 

FOR RECORD 

(2) The statutes in most states require that an instrument be acknowledged before it can be recorded, and there follows the further rule that no instrument can be recorded until it has been acknowledged. But these are not hard and fast rules everywhere. In some jurisdictions, certain instruments are admitted to public record without acknowledgment or proof or certificate of any kind. (3) If, though, there be a paper which is not required to be acknowledged, the fact of acknowledgment does not necessarily entitle it to public record unless it is otherwise so entitled. (4) If an instrument is signed and executed by several persons, but acknowledged by only one of them, this will entitle it to be recorded. Or if only one of the certificates is correct, the others defective, it is entitled to record. (5) In a jurisdiction where there are other legal requirements for recordation, as for example, that certain fees must be paid, or other such provisions, then the acknowledgment alone will not entitle the paper to record, unless and until such other legal reguirements are met.

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS 

115 

FOR USE AS EVIDENCE 

(6) Admissibility of a written instrument in evidence without proof of execution may seem to be-indeed it is—rather a legal than a notarial question. Nevertheless, we may give it a little study here. Suppose A and B to be engaged in trial of a lawsuit, as adversaries. A produces and offers in evidence a paper which he says C signed, it being of a kind that is required to be acknowledged. If it shows an acknowledgment certificate, then A does not have to prove by C or anyone else that C did in fact sign it. (As to matters where outright forgery is asserted the rule is different in some jurisdictions.) But if it does not bear an acknowledgment certificate, then A must bring in some kind of proof of C's signature, sometimes an easy thing to do, sometimes difficult and sometimes impossible. Very little thought is needed to convince that in such a case it is a decided benefit to have the instrument duly acknowledged and certified. A more involved case might be imagined. Suppose that A gives B a deed to a tract of land. B gives C a deed to the same land and later 

C gives D a deed to it. There are by now four actors on the stage. Along comes a fifth, E, who claims the land as his own, asserting that A deeded same to him for a valuable consideration, says that he had no actual knowledge of the conveyance to B, and demands that the land be surrendered to him. 

E exhibits an unrecorded and unacknowledged deed from A, direct to him, but no other proof. Thus D and E lock horns in contest over the land. But the deed from A to B, the deed from B to C and the deed from C to D have all been properly acknowledged and recorded. Now how will D prove his title? Shall he prove by his own oral testimony that he bought from C, C from B, B from A?. No; links in a chain of title are not provable by oral testimony of that kind. Can he bring into court A and B and C and prove his whole chain of title by them? Maybe so, but human nature being what it is, he'd be gambling against misfortune; either or all of them may deny any knowledge of such sales; again, A may have died; 

B may be in jail, C in South America. Here it is that the advantage and usefulness of the acknowledgments becomes apparent. Each of the deeds having been duly authenticated and placed of record, D has only to produce the record of said deeds and rest his case. Go a little further: Imagine D to have died and the task of defending his title to have devolved upon his widow or administrator or executor. How obvious is the advantage of the authentication! Many illustrations could be given.

NECESSITY 

Q. For what papers is acknowledgment necessary? 

A. In practice these days nearly all business papers are certified by a notary. The certification may be either of an affidavit or an acknowledgment, sometimes both. Even papers which do not require an acknowledgment or affidavit are quite commonly acknowledged or sworn to. This habit, or custom, is to be commended. Attachment of an acknowledgment certificate to a business paper can do no harm, and it may quite often be the means of settling a dispute or preventing a lawsuit. The notary's duty is simple-if requested to certify an acknowledgment, he should do so, whether the certificate is essential to the validity of the instrument, or only necessary for authentication, or not required at all. But though it is proper for a notary to certify an acknowledgment when requested to do so, without concerning himself about its legal necessity, some explanation of the law may be interesting. 

Two rules are stated, namely: a. If by law the acknowledgment is made a part of the instrument itself, then obviously the instrument is incomplete, therefore void, unless acknowledged. b. An instrument in writing (unless otherwise proved) if intended for record or for use as evidence, must be acknowledged for authentication, the general rule being that a written instrument may not be recorded or received in evidence unless acknowledged (or otherwise authenticated.) There are exceptions to this rule, perhaps the best known being that of WILLS. 

Stated differently, these rules mean that acknowledgment is not essential to the validity of the instrument itself unless made so by law, but that acknowledgment (or other proof) is essential for authentication.

FIRST RULE 

A common example of the first stated rule is the deed of a married woman to her homestead or separate property, in those states where such an instrument without her acknowledgment is void, this being so because the statutes are so worded as to make her acknowledgment a part of the instrument itself. Other instances exist, in nearly all the states, of instruments which must be acknowledged to become effective at all; for example, articles of incorporation; articles of co-partnership; articles for co-operatives; assignments for creditors pre-nuptial agreements; powers of attorney, etc.

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS 

117 

SECOND RULE 

The second rule includes all instruments intended for record or for use as evidence whose execution is not otherwise proved. 

If statutory law, then, requires an instrument to be acknowledged, obviously it is not complete until so acknowledged. 

But there is not, to this writer's knowledge, any statute in any state which requires all written instruments to be authenticated, either by acknowledgment or otherwise. To list all instruments which must be acknowledged is not feasible here. All instruments affecting the title to land should be acknowledged. Numerous papers dealing only with personal property likewise require acknowledgment. True the notary is not every time the one who must decide whether an instrument should be acknowledged, yet he should have at least an intelligent understanding of the general subject. 

WHO MAY MAKE ACKNOWLEDGMENT 

Q. Who may make acknowledgment of written instruments?

A. The person whose name is signed to the instrument should make such acknowledgment; it is strictly a personal duty and function.

Q. If several names are signed, may one make acknowledgment for others?

A. No; each acknowledges for himself or herself. 

IN WHAT CAPACITY 

Q. May one acknowledge an instrument in other than his own individual capacity? 

A. (1) Yes. An acknowledgment may be given as the individual act of the signer himself, he having signed his own name only to the instrument; or, (2) As the act of a principal for whom the signer is agent or attorney, the principal's name appearing first, followed by that of the actual signer; or, (3) As the act of a firm or partnership, the firm name showing first; or, (4) As the act of a corporation, the corporate name being first shown, then that of the signer; or, (5) As the act of an officer, that is to say, of a guardian, sheriff, trustee, receiver, or other official acting under a trust or court order, the said officer's name appearing first, followed by words descriptive of the capacity in which he signs and acknowledges.

(6) Obviously in whatever capacity a signer acts, in that same capacity he should acknowledge, and thus we find that although he must always acknowledge that he himself executed the instrument, he must-if he acted otherwise than as his own individual self-acknowledge that he acted for some other person, firm or corporation or in some other than his own individual capacity. (7) Obviously it is the duty of a notary when called to certify any acknowledgment, to take note of the capacity in which the signer acts, and certify accordingly. 

ACKNOWLEDGMENT OF "MARK” 

Q. If one is unable to sign a paper, how may he sign and acknowledge?

A. (1) Acknowledging just as any other person might do, he may sign by mark or cross, as elsewhere explained. (2) He may let another person sign his name and adopt the signature as his own. (3) He may allow an authorized agent or attorney to sign his name, as such agent or attorney.

AGENT OR ATTORNEY 

Q. If the signing and acknowledging are done by an agent or attorney, or for a corporation or partnership, how will the signature appear? 

A. Generally thus: John T. Doe, by Richard W. Roe. To such a signature Richard W. Roe makes acknowledgment, he having signed thereto his own and his principal's name, and he acknowledges that in so signing he acted in the capacity of an agent or attorney.

SHERIFF, GUARDIAN, TRUSTEE 

Q. If the signing and acknowledging are done by a sheriff or guardian or trustee, or in some other like capacity, how will the signature appear? 

A. Generally thus: John R. Smith, Sheriff of Oshkosh county,

Missouri; or, Henry W. Butler, guardian of the person and estate of Sidney Latham Brown, a minor; or, Ada G. Henry, trustee for First National Bank of Woodson, Texas, the signer making acknowledgment in the capacity shown, and the notary's certificate to recite that fact.

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS 

119 

INTERPRETER 

Q. May an acknowledgment be made through an interpreter? 

A. Yes. The interpreter should be sworn to interpret correctly and the notary's certificate of acknowledgment should set out clearly the facts of such interpretation, giving the reason and the necessity therefor, the fact of the swearing of the interpreter, etc. CORPORATION 

Q. How may a corporation acknowledge a signature to a paper?

A. The individual who signs such paper acknowledges that he signed, or executed, the same as the act and deed of such corporation. See the Certificate Forms.

PARTNERSHIP 

Q. How may a partnership acknowledge a partnership signature?

A. Ordinarily any partner may sign the partnership or firm name and his own, and acknowledge the firm signature as his and as being for and on behalf of such partnership or firm. 

In some partnership matters in some jurisdictions it is required that every member of the firm sign and acknowledge, and in those instances, one partner can not acknowledge for another or for the firm.

WHO MAY TAKE ACKNOWLEDGMENTS 

Q. Who, that is to say, what officer or officers, may take and certify acknowledgments?

A. Only those officers who are by law expressly authorized to do so. It will suffice for our purposes here to state that notaries are clothed with that power and authority in all the states and in all Federal jurisdictions. 

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS IN THE ARMED FORCES 

In many of the states, during the recent World War, laws were enacted providing that officers in any of the ARMED FORCES, anywhere, may take and certify the acknowledgment of any person in any of such ARMED FORCES, and dispensing with necessity for use of official seal in the said officer's certificate to such acknowledgment. 

DISQUALIFICATION 

Q. May a notary sign an instrument and then take and certify his own acknowledgment thereto?

A. No. It is not permitted that he certify his own acknowledgment. Certification of his own acknowledgment would simply result in a null and void certificate. 

DisqualifieD IF INTERESTED 

Q. If a notary is interested in the transaction to which a paper relates, may he take and certify the acknowledgment of a signer?

A. The general rule is that he is disqualified to certify all acknowledgments to any paper dealing with a transaction in which he is interested. Courts everywhere have concurred in holding it to be of utmost importance that the officer, notary or other, taking an acknowledgment, be disinterested, and the rule that a notary is disqualified to certify an acknowledgment of a conveyance in which he is interested is well established. This rule is not, so far as this writer has discovered, based upon a statute anywhere, but is based by the courts upon what is called sound public policy. 

WHAT DEGREE OF INTEREST DISQUALIFIES 

Q. What degree of interest in a transaction will disqualify a notary to certify the acknowledgment? 

A. (1) Upon this point as upon many others, the courts are not in full accord. It seems agreed generally that if the notary's name appears in the instrument, either as grantor or grantee, then he (or she) may not certify. This rule, though, has been ignored in at least one of the decisions. In that case it appeared that the wife's separate property was being sold, and the husband, a notary, joined her in the conveyance, certifying her acknowledgment. On appeal the certificate was not set aside. (2) The ruling is practically unanimous that if the notary is either husband or wife of a party to the suit, he (or she) is thereby disqualified. (3) A member of a partnership is by such membership disqualified to take and certify an acknowledgment where the partnership is interested. (4) In nearly all the states, if a corporation is a party to a transaction, that fact disqualifies a notary who is agent, employee, officer, director, or stockholder of such corporation. 

But while this is the long established rule upon this point, yet in some of the states it has been set aside by statute and

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS 

121 certain officers, agents or employees of certain named classes of corporations are definitely authorized to act. (5) In many of the states, a notary is disqualified to certify the acknowledgment of a near relative, a brother, sister, aunt, uncle, neice, nephew or other, while in others, such relationship does not disqualify. (6) Summing up, it is agreed that interest disqualifies, but the measure of such interest, or what character or nature of interest, is not a point upon which agreement has been reached. 

WHAT SHOULD THE NOTARY DO 

Q. If a notary is asked to certify an instrument to which he is in any way a party, what course should he follow?

A. He should consult a competent attorney and follow the attorney's advice. Stated differently, don't do anything "till you talk to your lawyer."

DISQUALIFIED NOTARY'S ACT, EFFECT OF 

Q. If a disqualified notary certifies an acknowledgment, what is the effect: a. Upon the certifiacte; b. Upon the paper certified?

A. a. The certificate is invalid, does not entitle the paper to public record, or for use as evidence, or in any other way authenticate it; b. The paper, the instrument, is in no way affected by such a certificate, either for good or for evil; if the paper is one which does not require an acknowledgment, then such a certificate does it no harm; if it is one which does require an acknowledgment, then such a certificate does it no good, it remains incomplete, awaiting proper acknowledgment and certification.

DISQUALIFICATION REMEDY 

Q. How may an error in having a disqualified notary certify an instrument, be corrected?

A. Such an error may be corrected by simply having the paper again certified, by a notary not disqualified. In other words, disregard the error, treat the paper as not having been acknowledged, have it acknowledged and certified and the error is thereby undone. This is simple enough if the instrument has not been recorded, and if no third person's rights have intervened. If the instrument has been recorded, it may nevertheless be re-acknowledged and re-recorded, but damage may have resulted to third persons.

PLACE OF TAKING 

Q. Where may an acknowledgment be taken and certified?

A. At any place within the notary's official jurisdiction. The place of residence of the signer is of no consequence; he may sign and acknowledge at home or abroad; he may sign and acknowledge at the same place, or he may sign at one place and acknowledge at another.

TIME OF TAKING 

Q. When may an acknowledgment be taken and certified? 

A. At any time that suits the convenience of the parties, after it has been signed. It should not be done in violation of local 

Sunday laws. This writer has found no general law militating against the taking of an acknowledgment at any time, week day, Sunday or holiday, although in some jurisdictions an acknowledgment taken and certified on Sunday or a legal holiday is a void act. It is suggested that on ordinary business days is a better time. The date of the acknowledgment should be correctly stated. This date need not be the same as the date of the instrument.

TAKEN, HOW 

Q. How should an acknowledgment be taken?

A. Formerly acknowledgments were taken with due solemnity in open court. That rule has been relaxed and now they are taken anywhere, any time, that suits the convenience of the parties. About the simple matter of taking and certifying an acknowledgment there need be no rigid formality, yet for the protection of the persons whose interests are at stake, the substance of the law should be undeviatingly followed, and the form as well. The notary who adopts a correct practice and adheres to it will find his patrons quick to perceive and appreciate efficiency and courtesy; it is always so. Of three things he should be certain: (a) That the person whose acknowledgment he is asked to certify is personally in his presence. (b) That the said person before him is undoubtedly the very person whose signature he is expected to certify. (c) That the said person does unequivocally acknowledge the signature.

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS 

123 

PERSONAL APPEARANCE 

Q. Why is it important that the acknowledger be personally in the notary's presence?

A. Because the acknowledgment certificates assert that the signer personally appeared before the notary. One may authorize another to sign his name and thereafter lawfully adopt the authorized signature as his own by acknowledgment; he may, after another has, even without permission or authority, signed his name, vitalize such signature by his acknowledgment and become thereby bound; he may sign for another and lawfully acknowledge that he did sign for such other. But though he may thus delegate to another or others the signing of his name, yet, when arrives the moment for acknowledgment of the execution of the paper to which his name is signed, he must perform that duty himself-he can not delegate to any other person the authority to give, or make, his acknowledgment. So it is essential that he in person appear before the notary to give the acknowledgment. Reference to the 

FORMS section will disclose that the commonest form of acknowledgment certificate recites that "personally appeared before me" etc. This does not mean that the signer must come to the notary, but only that there must be a personal meeting. 

A notary may leave his office to visit the signer, and the contact so arranged fully meets the requirement of a "personal appearance before" such officer. The intent of the phrase is that no signer may permit any other person to appear and acknowledge for him; that he must in person appear, so that he may be identified and his signature verified by his own expression. It is this requirement in the certificates of a personal appearance before the officer that inhibits telephone acknowledgments. 

Q. Are there instances in which one person may, as deputy, or substitute, or agent, acknowledge for another?

A. Certainly. But when one has a deputy, or substitute or agent to acknowledge for him, then it is the deputy, or substitute or agent who "personally appears" before the notary. And the notary's certificate, remember, should state the exact facts, otherwise it is a false certificate. A certificate under such circumstances should state that the deputy, or substitute or agent personally appeared before the notary, or that the principal personally appeared before him in the person of the deputy, or substitute or agent. To certify that the principal so personally appeared when in fact he did not appear but the

deputy, agent or substitute did appear is simply to give a false certificate. One may authorize another to acknowledge his signature to an instrument, and the authority so given may be followed up and acted under, but when he does so, it is finally and inevitably the agent-not the principal-who "personally appears before" the notary and makes the acknowledgment. 

And the notary's certificate should state the facts.

PERSONALLY KNOWN 

Q. Why is it important for the notary to be certain that the person before him is indeed the signer of the paper? 

A. If the person who appears before the notary is not in fact the right person; if he is a forger, or an imposter, then the acknowledgment will be invalid. Since it is wholly and entirely the notary's responsibility to vouch for the identity of the acknowledger, he should be certain every time that the person appearing before him is the person whose name is signed to the paper. If a mistake is made, it is the notary's mistake, and he and his official bondsmen are liable for repair of any resulting damages. The notary's certificate is his own responsibility; the law presumes him to be intelligent and honest; provides that he shall be paid for his work, and exacts of him a penal bond guaranteeing the accuracy and integrity of his official acts. It is not then an unreasonable thing for the law to expect of him that he will take care in every instance to certify the right person. Yet, the records abundantly show that it is disregard of this important part of his duties that has caused notaries more bond lawsuits than any other. Perhaps out of any ten suits on notary bonds, nine have grown out of the notary's carelessness in failing to make sure that he had before him the very person whose name was signed to the paper. See the chapter on Civil Liability.

DEGREE OF CARE 

Q. To what degree of care is the notary held in identifying the person "appearing before him?"

A. Attention is directed to the language of the acknowledgment certificates in the various states, as shown elsewhere in these pages. These certificates are not exactly alike in all cases. 

The notary is held to the language of the certificate for his own jurisdiction. It is not expected of him that he will be previously acquainted with every person who comes seeking his services. In some of the states he must certify either that the acknowledger is known to him, or that such ac-

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS 

125 knowledger was proved to him on oath of a witness. Where there is a law declaring how a stranger's identity shall be established, the notary can lawfully follow only the method outlined. If he does this, he will be clear of legal fault, even though deceived as to the signer's identity. It is not essential that he shall have previously known the acknowledger. No hard and fast rules can be laid down as to how he shall satisfy himself of the identity of strangers, but it is required of him that he be satisfied, or that, otherwise, he will withhold his certificate. Ordinarily a stranger will be introduced by an acquaintance of the notary, and this introduction can be reduced to affidavit form if desired, or if the acquaintance is one who does not swear, it may be affirmed. If the notary is in doubt, he should refuse his certificate until the stranger is properly vouched for. In this as in all other notarial problems and certificate matters, the notary will be safe if he follows the law; it is only when he disregards the statutes and decisions that he flirts with Madam Trouble. In a case before the supreme court of Texas, that court made use of the following language: "The law has not prescribed the extent of acquaintance which is necessary to justify an officer in certifying that the person who presents himself is known to the officer to be the person who signed the instrument in question; but that question is, and necessarily must be, submitted to the decision of each officer, under the facts as they exist at the time the act is done. When such officer has determined, upon the evidence presented to him, that he identifies the person in question as being the same that executed the instrument under consideration, and when he so certifies according to law, the certificate must be held to be sufficient, unless upon its face it shows that in fact the statement of such knowledge is untrue." 

STATUTORY CERTIFICATE FORMS 

Q. Is there an acknowledgment form prescribed in every state?

A. Practically every state does prescribe by law a form for acknowledgment certificates. À careful study of the acknowledgment certificates shown on other pages of this volume is recommended to the reader.

SUBSTANTIALLY FOLLOWED 

Q. Where state law prescribes an acknowledgment form, is it required that the form shall be exactly followed?

A. It is required in all cases where there is a statutory form that the form be substantially followed, but this writer has not found any decision holding an acknowledgment certificate void for mere minor infractions or deviations. 

CERTIFICATE, WHERE 

Q. Where should the acknowledgment certificate be shown?

A. Without doubt it should immediately follow at the finish or end of the written instrument, after the acknowledger's signature. In law suits which have hinged in whole or in part on the validity of acknowledgment certificates it has been held that the acknowledgment certificate does not of necessity follow the acknowledger's signature. It may appear at the beginning of the instrument or on a margin and still be held good. Nevertheless, it is just plain logic and common sense to have the certificate immediately follow the acknowledger's signature.

NOT ON DETACHED SHEET 

Q. Will an acknowledgment certificate be valid if on a sheet of paper separate and detached from the instrument purporting to be acknowledged?

A. Very commonly an acknowledgment may appear on a different sheet of paper from that on which the acknowledger's signature appears, but such sheets of paper should be so attached or fastened together as to leave no doubt that they are parts of one document.

TIME OF CERTIFYING 

Q. When should an acknowledgment certificate be made?

A. At the time of the signing of the paper or any time thereafter. 

Obviously it is better that the acknowledgment certificate be given at the time, while the parties are together and the incident is fresh in their minds. The courts have held, however, that acknowledgments certified after lapse of considerable time may still be valid.

ACKNOWLEDGER IDENTIFIED 

Q. Must the signer and acknowledger be identified in the acknowledgment certificate?

A. Obviously yes. The name of the signer must be recited in the certificate, and there must be some kind of expression in the certificate indicating that such person is the same person

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS 

127 who signed the written instrument. It will be observed that the language of the acknowledgment certificates in the different states is not by any means identical upon this feature. 

Nevertheless, it is in all instances necessary that there be some kind of expression indicating that the acknowledger was also the signer. 

Q. Is it necessary that the certificate identify the instrument acknowledged?

A. The certificate of acknowledgment should in every instance identify the paper which has been acknowledged by some such expression as "the foregoing instrument" or "the above and foregoing instrument" or "the attached deed," etc.

CERTIFICATE ESSENTIALS 

Q. What are the essentials of an acknowledgment certificate?

A. Not in every state the same, but no acknowledgment will be sufficient unless it shows the venue, the name of the acknowledger, that the acknowledger personally appeared before the notary, a mention of the instrument acknowledged, the date, and the notary's official signature. 

VENUE 

Q. What is the venue, and why should it be shown in an acknowledgment certificate?

A. As recited elsewhere in these pages, a notary public can legally certify an instrument only in his own jurisdiction, and a statement of the venue must be looked to to determine whether or not such notary is within his own jurisdiction. 

The venue must correspond to his own official signature. 

The venue is ordinarily stated thus: "The State of..........

County of............... 

Or in Louisiana: 

"The State of Louisiana

Parish of................. 

Or if the instrument is intended for use in Federal matters it may read: "United States of America 

.District of................”

OFFICIAL SIGNATURE 

Q. Of what does the notary's official signature consist?

A. (1) His name signed, a statement of his official title, and, in those states (nearly all) where an official seal is required, the imprint of his official seal. Where the law directs the use of an official seal, no official signature will be complete without it. Let it be remembered, though, that in some states a seal is not required at all, and in others its use is optional with the notary in execution of his official certificates. (2) In many of the states it is now required that a notary must not only sign his name by his own hand, to his official certificates, but he must also stamp or print the same, the purpose of the requirement being to make the signature definitely legible, not all notarial handwritings being up to that standard. In some of the states this requirement is greatly modified, even emasculated, by a further stipulation that failure of the notary to so stamp or print his name shall not invalidate the "acknowledgment". The Texas statute upon this point, enacted in 1941, is typical and illustrative. It is in part as follows: "that all notaries public shall print or stamp their name under their signatures on all such written instrument, provided that failure to so print or stamp their names under their signatures shall not invalidate such acknowledgment." (3) Whether by the provision that failure to stamp or print the name shall not invalidate the "acknowledgment" means that the actual acknowledgment by the signer remains valid, despite such failure, or whether it means that the certificate remains valid despite such failure has not been, so far as this writer has observed, decided by any court. Doubtless in time a test case will develop. This writer's guess is that the failure of the notary to print or stamp his name as directed leaves valid both the acknowledgment and the certificate. (4) A notary signing his certificates should avoid the use of abbreviations. Thus, if his proper official title is: "Notary Public in and for Jones County, Alabama," it is hardly permissible for him to sign as: "N.P. J.Co. Ala."

Abbreviations may save ink, time and space, but abbreviations of the kind here shown have produced some very tedious and expensive law suits, as the court records abundantly show. In an early case there was before the supreme court

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS 

129 of Michigan on appeal, involving the validity and sufficiency of an acknowledgment certificate, the following form: "signed, sealed, and acknowledged in my presence the day and year aforesaid. John J. Kaiting, Notary Public."

The court, holding the certificate not good, said, in part: "This falls short of any such certificate of acknowledgment as has been held sufficient to make legal evidence. 

There is also nothing in the certificate to show where it was made, or in what state or county Mr. Kaiting was a notary public. (Cooper v. Smith, 42 N. W. 

Rep. 815.)"

That expression of that great court is strictly in line with the weight of authoritative opinion, notwithstanding many official certificates have been sustained which lacked one or more of the above mentioned essentials. (5) If the notary holds his office ex-officio, it may not be necessary, but it is certainly advisable that he state in his official certificate his official title in full. Thus: "John T. Doe, Justice of the Peace, Precinct No. 1,

Beaver County, Missouri, and ex-officio Notary Public." 

"MY COMMISSION EXPIRES" 

Q. In those states where a notary is required to show the expiraing date of his commission, how and where should that showing be made?

A. The words used everywhere for this purpose are these: "My commission expires........

The writer has not found any statute stating exactly where that is, on what part of the notary's official certificate, this showing shall be made. The statutes on the subject merely require that the notary's official certificate shall state the date of the expiration of his commission. Now, that would seem to make the said showing a part of the official certificate. 

If it is a part of the official certificate, then it should precede the signature of the notary, because obviously the notary's signature should come at the end of his official certificate. 

Generally this showing of the expiration date of the commission is made to follow the seal and the signature. When so presented, it becomes merely a postscript, unsigned, and the question might be asked whether or not, if so presented, it is actually a part of the official certificate. It will be observed that the certificate forms in these pages show the expression,

"My commission expires," preceding the notary's signature and seal. The notary is advised in this matter to follow the custom, the law and the advice of a competent attorney in his own jurisdiction.

SIGNATURE NECESSARY 

Q. Is the notary's own signature essential to his official certificate?

A. Yes. The certificate is not complete without his written signature. The fact that his name, official title, and address may appear in his seal of office does not dispense with the necessity for his signed name. His written signature is essential.

PRIVATE SEAL 

Q. In those states where private seals are still used, what relation does such private seal have to the notary's official certificate?

A. A condition is conceivable where three seals might be used on a single paper, namely, the private seal of the signer, the private seal of the notary and the official seal of the notary. 

In some states the signer uses a private seal, while in others all private seals have been abolished. It would seem, though, that a notary should never include his private seal, if any, in his official certificate, since it is the seal of his office that stamps his certificate with the authorty of law. His private seal is merely part of his own individual signature. In an 

Illinois case the use of a private seal was held to be justified, where there was no requirement of statute that the officer have an official seal, but in Florida a different ruling was followed. In a Missouri case (Rivard v. M. P. Ry., 165

S.W. Rep. 763) where it was shown that the mayor of Kansas City who was authorized to take and certify acknowledgments, had made use of his private seal, stating in his certificate that no official seal had been provided, the supreme court of that state held the private seal sufficient. Finally, let it be remembered, that the notary is a public officer. If the law of his jurisdiction requires him to use a seal of office in his certificates, he should procure and use such seal, and take no chances. He should remember that it is his patron's interest which will suffer if he makes a mistake, not his own, and that he owes the duties of skill, diligence and intelligent attention.

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS 

131 

SEAL OF OFFICE, WHERE SHOWN 

Q. Where should the seal be shown on the official certificate?

A. So that it clearly appears to be a part of the certificate, its exact location is not important. The writer has not found any law requiring that the seal be on any particular part of the certificate.

SIGNATURE, NOTARY EX OFFICIO 

Q. Is the official signature of a notary ex officio required to be different from that of a notary appointed? 

A. The notary appointed may state merely that he is a notary public, stating his jurisdiction, address, etc., as may be required by statute in his state. The official signature of a notary ex officio should state what other office he holds, by virtue of which he has become a notary public. See the form shown on a preceding page. In this connection it may be observed that commonly a justice of the peace is not required to have a seal, while a notary is almost universally required to have a seal. In some of those states where a justice of the peace is ex officio notary public, he is required to have a notary seal, though not required to have a seal as justice of the peace. 

Q. May any one other than the notary himself give his official certificate?

A. Not lawfully. Of course, a notary might authorize some one else to sign and fill in the blanks of his official certificate, and his adoption of the signature and authorization of the work would make the certificate legal.

MARRIED WOMAN'S ACKNOWLEDGMENT 

Q. It being shown that some of the states require a married woman's acknowledgment to be taken separate and apart from her husband, what is the notary's duty in those states in that regard?

A. (1) Under the law of any state which requires a married woman's acknowledgment to be taken separate and apart from her husband, the acknowledgment must be taken just that way. The husband must be out of hearing; the acknowledgment must be taken privily. In case of an instrument which does not require to be signed or acknowledged by the wife, her acknowledgment may be taken in the presence of her husband, or entirely omitted. It is only in those instances. where her signature and acknowledgment are required, that

the provision for privy examination and acknowledgment must be followed. The language of the certificate, as laid down in the statute of the particular jurisdiction, must be exactly and literally followed. (2) The foregoing does not mean that two separate certificates are required, for husband and wife. Both certificates may be in the same form, both names shown, but care should be taken to have the certificate recite that the lady who signs and acknowledges is the wife of the man who signs and acknowledges. (3) Words spelled wrong, errors in grammar, inaccuracies in punctuation, mere clerical mistakes, will not vitiate such a certificate. (4) In some of the states it is required that the instrument acknowledged be fully explained to the lady, and that she shall state to the notary her free willingness to sign and acknowledge. These requirements, wherever laid down, must be literally followed by the notary and fully recited in his official certificate. (5) In some of the states, the notary's certificate must show her recital of her free and voluntary relinguishment of her dower rights-in those instances where she does in fact release her dower-while in others relinquishment of homestead is required to be shown. In each jurisdiction the requirements of the laws upon such points in that jurisdiction should be strictly followed. (6) Finally it may be stated that one of a notary's most important tasks is the proper taking and certifying of married women's acknowledgments. The court records show that many lawsuits grow out of carelessness, or alleged carelessness, in performance of this task. 

PROOF FOR RECORD 

Q. What is meant by the expression "proof for record" or "proof by subscribing witness"?

A. The proof of any instrument of writing for the purpose of being recorded generally is made by one or more of the subscribing witnesses personally appearing before some officer authorized to take such proof and stating on oath that he or they saw the grantor or person who executed such instrument of writing sign the same, or that the grantor or person who executed such instrument of writing acknowledged in his or

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS 

133 their presence that he had executed the same, and that he or they had signed the same as witnesses at the request of the grantor or person who executed such instrument. The use of this method for getting instruments onto the public records is not now quite as common or general as it was in former years. The exact requirements for this procedure are not exactly the same in all the states. Nevertheless, they are more nearly similar than the acknowledgment certificates. Attention is directed to the Forms section. 

PROOF BY HANDWRITING 

Q. May an instrument be proved for record by handwriting alone of the signer? 

A. Yes, in some jurisdictions a written instrument apparently signed by any person and otherwise fitted for record, may be admitted to record on proof of the handwriting of such signer. It is suggested, however, that this is a procedure which the notary, unless he is a lawyer or has had previous experience, should not undertake except under legal guidance. 

COMPELLING ACKNOWLEDGMENT 

Q. May a person be compelled to acknowledge execution of an instrument in writing?

A. Yes, by lawful procedure in a court of competent jurisdiction, one may be compelled to give such acknowledgment. This rule would not apply to a married woman, however, in ordinary cases; in some of the states it would not apply to her at all, for the reason that in such states she can not be compelled to sign or give conveyance of her lands. Refusal of a man to give due acknowledgment of his signature might be punished under contempt of court proceedings. However, a forced acknowledgment may be procured only upon equitable grounds, and the statement may be safely made that it is a remedy seldom invoked.

AFFIDAVITS Chapter 6 DEFINITIONS AND DISTINCTIONS 

Q. What is an affidavit?

A. (1) Affidavit means a statement in writing of a fact or facts signed by the party making it, sworn to before some officer authorized to administer oaths, and officially certified by such officer. In some of the states the official certificate may omit the official seal and yet be valid; in others the affiant's signature is not essential, but generally speaking there can be no affidavit unless and until someone has sworn to a writing, signed it, and had it officially certified by an officer authorized to make such official certificate. A common test of whether or not a paper is an affidavit is this: Could a prosecution for perjury be based upon it, if it is shown to be false? 

SWEAR OR AFFIRM 

(2) Some persons will not swear, but only "affirm." In practically all jurisdictions "affirmation" has all the force and effect of an oath or affidavit, but it is in fact neither, because it carries no invocation of Divine vengeance for falsity. In earlier ages the expression: "So help me God," was: "So help me, God, in the day of thy judgment only if now I swear the truth." No such meaning is attached to affirmation. Prosecution for perjury, however, may be based upon an affirmation as well as upon an affidavit. As is shown elsewhere in these pages, a notary who certifies an "affirmation" should certify that the party affirmed, not that he swore. 

NOT ACKNOWLEDGMENT (3) "Acknowledgment" is merely a confession or admission of some prior act; in its essential elements it does not even resemble affidavit. Truth of this becomes readily apparent when we recall that in these days many business and legal papers are required to be both sworn to and acknowledged. (4) The difference between acknowledgment and affidavit lies in this: that whereas an acknowledgment involves merely the signature, affidavit goes to the contents of the instru-

AFFIDAVITS 

135 ment itself. By an acknowledgment one admits that he signed the paper; by an affidavit he declares under oath that the contents of the paper are true. 

NOT DEPOSITION 

(5) Like an affidavit, a "deposition" is given under oath, and is attested by the swearer's signature, but there the likeness ends. A deposition is taken only under commission or order duly authorized by court or officer; it may commonly be used as evidence in court or before board or tribunal, whereas an affidavit is not, commonly, so admissable, and may be taken without commission or authority from any source. A deposition is more often taken under cross-examination, whereas in an affidavit there is no opportunity for cross-examination. A further distinction is that whereas an affidavit is a voluntary statement of the affiant, given without compulsion, the deposition may be given only because the deponent is ordered or compelled to give it. See the chapter on Depositions.

NOT INDICTMENT, INFORMATION 

(6) An "affidavit" is not an "information" or "indictment" in criminal action. True, an information may be little more than a mere affidavit, but it is always something more, and the words are not identical in meaning.

AFFIDAVIT, AFFIRMATION, OATH 

Q. Are there, and what, distinctions and differences in the meaning of the words, "affidavits", "affirmations", "oaths"?

A. (1) The foregoing question can not be satisfactorily answered in merely a few words. The use of several paragraphs is unavoidable, if accuracy, clarity and completeness of definition are to be achieved.

AFFIRMATION 

(2) In all, or nearly all, American jurisdictions it is provided that one shall not be compelled to swear, or make oath, but that he may be allowed to affirm, instead. This for conscience's sake. Affirmation has been defined as "a solemn religious asservation in the nature of an oath," and the definition has had judicial approval. The word is in perfectly common use. Its legal meaning and its ordinary meaning are identical, so that no technical elaboration is called for. In court, in business, it has the same effect, achieves the same purpose, as affidavit or oath. Let it therefore be understood that wherever in these pages oath and affidavit are mentioned, af-

firmation is assured, unless otherwise specified. In some of the states the law clothing a notary with his official powers and duties expressly mentions the administration of affirmations as one of such powers and duties; in others, less comprehensive language is used, and the power to administer oaths and/or to certify affidavits is interpreted to include affirmations.

OATHS, AFFIDAVITS 

(3) These words have been defined many times by eminent American authority, and a selected few of those definitions will follow, but if a writer more humble than distinguished may be pardoned for having such "nerve," herewith are submitted a few lines in simple English in an attempt to make clear and unmistakable their pointed meanings, and the differences in those meanings. Thus: 

When one orally swears, he is making oath; when he swears in writing, he is making affidavit. When his oath or affirmation relates to the past, it will be more likely an affidavit; if his oath or affirmation relates to the future, it will likely remain an oath or affirmation. An oath does not necessarily become an affidavit, but an affidavit does necessarily include an oath. (4) Consider an affidavit of heirship. It will deal with incidents of the past, births, deaths, and marriages, and a notary public will certify such affidavit. An affidavit of possession or ownership of property will go back for its starting point to the beginning of such possession, and will stop with the moment of execution, and the affidavit (oath reduced to writing) will be certified by a notary. Countless affidavit examples might be cited, calling necessarily for certification by an officer, most commonly a notary public. But an oath will deal in most instances with a future. Thus, one elected to public office, will take the constitutional oath, which is a pledge as to his future conduct. But that oath of office will seldom be administered by a notary. Another public officer more commonly will officiate. True, the notary may have the authority to administer such an oath, but custom, we may believe, the country over, will leave the administration of the oath of office to other officers. Again, consider the juror in court. He will take an oath, an oath as to his future conduct as such juror, an oath that has nothing to do with the past, and that oath commonly will be administered to him by a notary, but by the judge or the clerk of the court. 

not

AFFIDAVITS 

137 Or an election officer, before beginning his duties, will take a solemn oath as to how he will perform at the polls on election day. That oath, though, will very likely be administered by another member of the election staff, and not by a notary public. 

(5) Black's Law Dictionary: "Oath an external pledge or asservation made in verification of statements made, or to be made, coupled with an appeal to a sacred or venerated object, in evidence of the serious and reverent state of mind of the party, or with an invocation to a supreme being to witness the words of the party, and to visit him with punishment if they be false." "Affidavit" A written or printed declaration or statement of facts, made voluntarily, and confirmed by the oath or affirmation of the party making it, taken before an officer having authority to administer such an oath." (6) Words and Phrases: "An affidavit is an oath in writing." (13 A. 2d, 305.) “An affidavit is a written declaration under oath." (4 Kan. 124) "A paper which lacks a jurat is not an affidavit." (20 N.Y.S. 2d, 875). “An affidavit is an oath or affirmation reduced to writing and sworn to or affirmed before some officer who has lawful authority to administer it." (123 S.W.2, 306). “Affidavit is not an ambiguous word. It signifies a written attestation or asservation of the truth, in contradistinction to oath, which is a mere oral attestation." (21 Pac. Ct. Rep. 326). (7) American Jurisprudence: "In its broadest sense an oath is any form of attestation by which a person signifies that he is bound in conscience to perform an act, faithfully and truthfully." (Vol. 39, page 494.) "An affidavit is any voluntary ex parte statement reduced to writing and sworn to or affirmed before some person legally authorized to administer an oath or affirmation. It is made without notice to the adverse party and without opportunity to cross-examine." (Vol. 1, page 984.)

An appellate court (278 N.W. 676) thus quotes a definition often approved: "The difference between an affidavit and an oath appears to be that an affidavit consists of statements of fact, which is sworn to as the truth, while an oath is a pledge." 

Q. Of what use is an affidavit?

A. (1) The affidavit (affirmation) is one of the commonest things in the court room, and one of the commonest things

in the business world. Under court practice in many jurisdictions a case can not be instituted in court unless or until someone has sworn (affirmed) some fact. In certain types of litigation, for example, forced collections, an affidavit often will be an essential part of the first pleadings filed. If a receivership is sought, or an injunction or mandamus asked, or an accounting, then an affidavit either in support of the bill, complaint or petition, or as part thereof, or as an exhibit, may be expected. Not infrequently, affidavits, one or more, will constitute part of the answer or defense, or may make appearance during pendency of the litigation. True, with affidavits having to do with court matters, the notary will generally have the guidance of an attorney in the case, yet he should understand the court affidavit as well as the business affidavit, for the simple reason that notarially there is little, perhaps no, difference in them, even though they bear widely different names and widely varying contents. 

MANY BUSINESS USES 

(2) Most frequent use of the affidavit is in ordinary business transactions, where it is used to fortify representations and statements of fact regarding any number of things. Statements of the financial condition of an applicant for credit, statements of heirship, of the pedigrees of animals, of titles to property, of possession of property, of payment or nonpayment of debt-these are but a few of the many, many uses to which the affidavit is subjected in modern business. 

WHO MAY MAKE
