Q. Who may make an affidavit? 

A. (1) Where a statute provides who may make an affidavit, that statute will control, but in absence of statutory provision for the making of an affidavit in any case, then as stated in a common legal definition, "Anyone having knowledge of the facts and the ability to make an oath may make an affidavit."

COVERTURE No Bar 

(2) Marital coverture is no bar, nor is minority; either a married woman or a person below legal age may make an affidavit.

AFFIANT'S INTEREST, NO DISQUALIFICATION 

(3) As elsewhere noted, a notary may be disqualified to certify an affidavit concerning a matter in which he is interested.

AFFIDAVITS 

139 But a distinction to be noted as between the person who makes an affidavit, and the notary who certifies such affidavit. The notary may be disqualified by interest. The affiant, though, unless a special statute controls, is not disqualified by interest.

The fact that he is interested may go to the weight and merit, or value, of his affidavit or affirmation, but it does not disqualify him to make it, swear to it, or affirm it, and have it certified. Thus one who is a party to a lawsuit may make affidavit concerning such lawsuit, or its merits, its progress, status or conclusion. Or one who owns property may make affidavit concerning such property, its possession, condition, location, value, age, size, weight etc., and there is no general rule of law forbidding him to make such affidavit. The fact of his interest then, does not disqualify, but goes merely to the weight of his sworn statement.

WHO MAY MAKE FOR ANOTHER 

Q. Who may make an affidavit for another?

A. (1) Generally speaking, an affidavit may be made by one person for another, as his, or her, agent or attorney. Such affidavit is actually of course the oath of such agent or attorney who does the swearing, and who signs the name of the principal, by himself as agent or attorney, but it is in such cases taken to be, and is accepted as, the act of the principal. In some jurisdictions there are statutes expressly providing that any affidavit or affirmation or oath which any person may make, may be made agent or attorney.

PARTNERSHIP AFFIDAVIT 

(2) In behalf of a partnership, generally any member acquainted with the facts may make an affidavit as to any partnership matter.

CORPORATION AFFIDAVIT 

(3) Since a corporation can not itself make an affidavit, such a paper must be executed, if at all, by some individual acting on behalf of the corporation, as its agent, representative, officer or employee. For some purposes, and on behalf of certain classes of corporations in some jurisdictions the power to make such affidavit is limited to designated individuals. 

In most others, though, the corporation's board of directors, or governing body, may designate the affiant.

HOW TAKEN 

Q. How shall an affidavit be taken, or administered?

A. (1) All oaths and affirmations should be administered in the mode most binding upon the conscience of the individual making same. The law is elastic in this requirement, in the different jurisdictions, and the notary may vary the method to conform to the affiant's religious belief or unbelief. 

KISSING THE Bible 

(2) It is said that in some foreign jurisdictions a Mohammedan is allowed to swear only on the Koran; a Chinese on a broken saucer; a Brahman only when touching the foot of another Brahman. In some American states the law requires that a person swearing (or affirming) be made to hold up his right hand; in others to kiss an open Bible. The notary should conform to the laws in his own jurisdiction. A Georgia statute reads as follows: "False swearing shall consist in wilfully, knowingly, absolutely and falsely swearing, either with or without laying the hand on the Holy Evangelist of Almighty God, or affirming in any matter or thing other than a judicial proceeding by a person to whom a lawful oath or affirmation has been administered."

In 1940 a case was before the Court of Appeals of that state, on appeal from a conviction for false swearing, in a lower court. The alleged false swearing grew out of a sworn application for a real estate loan of $1500, and it was asserted by the defendant's attorneys as a defense to the prosecution that "when he (the defendant) was told to hold up his hand (to swear) and was then asked whether or not the same was true, he made no reply."

Overruling this defense and affirming the conviction, the appellate court said, in part: "In the making of an affidavit of the character in question it is not necessary that the affiant shall hold up his hand and swear in order to make his act an oath. If both the affiant and the officer understand that what is done is all that is necessary to complete the act of swearing, his act is an oath in legal contemplation. If the defendant at the (time of) tendering the paper to the officer administering the oath, used language signifying that he consciously took upon himself the obligation of an oath, and the officer so understood, and immediately signed the jurat, this amounted to such concurrence of act and intention as constituted a legal swearing."

AFFIDAVITS 

141 

OATH'S ORIGIN 

(3) Origin of the oath as a means of binding the conscience of a witness and holding him to clearer memory and more strict truth, is traceable to remote antiquity. Its use was a settled practice among the ancient Romans and is mentioned in the Bible many times.

METHOD RECITED IN CERTIFICATE, WHEN 

(4) A notary approached by one wishing to execute an affidavit in an unusual manner, or under any procedure with which the notary is not familiar, should accede to the request, since the manner of taking the oath is protected by law, but if the manner of taking it is out of the ordinary, the notary should make mention of that fact in his official certificate, reciting the material and important facts of the occasion. 

PRESCRIBED WORDS 

(5) In only a few instances are words prescribed for use by the notary and the affiant in executing an affidavit. Generally, some such words as: Is this your signature? Or, Are you swearing to this? Or, Do you swear to the contents of this paper, and is this your signature? are used, and are sufficient. 

The notary may say to the affiant: Do you solemnly swear that the statements in this paper contained are true and correct, So help you, God? And: Is this your signature? Or, Did you sign this paper? The notary should of course make sure that the signature is that of the person before him, and that such person is either affirming or swearing, but there is no form of word or action generally prescribed to fit such a ceremony. In some early cases it was held that in answer to definite question by the notary, definite statement must be made by the affiant. Later rulings have been less rigid. But it may be very earnestly urged that neither of the parties. should relax too greatly the requirement of solemn understanding. Property values may be at stake. What is more important, truth is involved. Also, perjury and false swearing are punishable as crimes, and much trouble and grief can grow out of a false affidavit or affirmation, or out of a false certificate to either. 

Be reasonable 

yes but be careful! 

INTERPRETER 

(6) The affiant, if unable to speak English, may make the affidavit through an interpreter. The interpreter should be

sworn to correctly interpret, and the official certificate should set forth that the affidavit was given through an interpreter, naming him, who was duly sworn to correctly interpret, and declaring the language used by affiant, namely, French, German, etc.

READING PAPER TO SIGNER 

(7) It will be presumed of course that the affiant has read an instrument, or had the same read to him, before signing, yet an amazing number of people simply write their names wherever told to do so, without taking the trouble to read what they are signing. If the affiant requests the notary to read the paper to him, the notary should comply with the request, reading slowly and clearly the better to enable the affiant to understand; if he is illiterate, blind, mute, or otherwise physically disabled, the notary may assist as explained in the chapter on Writing Papers. 

MARK SIGNATURE 

(8) The signature may be made by mark, if desired, as more fully explained in the chapter on Writing Papers. 

NOTARY MAY REFUSE SERVICE 

(9) A notary is under no official obligation to certify an affidavit, and he may refuse to give his certificate if he wishes. He is a public officer, though, and ordinarily he should not refuse his official certificate when requested. He may refuse for reasons of conscience. For example, if he personally knows the contents of the instrument to be false, or knows that it is being given under duress, or that it involves unworthy morals, or that the affiant is mentally incompetent, he may lawfully and properly refuse to certify. 

WHO MAY TAKE 

Q. What officer or officers may take an affidavit?

A. Many different officers are entrusted with that power. 

For our purposes here it may be sufficient to state, without naming any other, that authority to administer oaths and certify affidavits is one of the "usual powers" given to American notaries.

AFFIDAVITS IN THE ARMED FORCES 

During the recent World War laws were enacted in most of the states, authorizing any officer in any of the Armed Forces

AFFIDAVITS 

143 to administer oaths and certify the same, for any person, stationed anywhere, in any of said Armed Forces, and dispensing with necessity for use of any official seal in such officer's certificate.

DISQUALIFICATION 

Q. Is there any instance where a notary may be disqualified to administer an oath or affirmation, and certify the same as an affidavit?

A. (1) There are circumstances under which a notary will be disqualified to certify an affidavit. An attorney who is also a notary may be disqualified to certify the oath or affidavit of his client in any given case, though the fact of the clientand-attorney relation will not generally extend the disqualification to matters in which the attorney has no interest. In some of the states, an attorney-notary will be disqualified to give a notary certificate in either a civil or a criminal case in which he is serving as attorney; in others, he may be disqualified in civil but not in criminal cases, and in yet others disqualified in criminal but not in civil cases. The general rule, though, is as stated, namely, if one is an attorney in a court matter, he can not certify the oath or affidavit of his client in any matter relating to that case. (2) In some of the states, statutes have been enacted clarifying the disqualification handicap as to bank employees and certain other corporation personnel. (3) While the nature of disqualification is not just the same in acknowledgment matters as in affidavit matters, yet the basis of disqualification remains virtually the same, that is to say, if the notary is personally interested, himself, he is disqualified to certify. (4) The difference in the nature of a certificate to an affidavit, and of a certificate to a deposition or acknowledgment, is such that perhaps less attention is given to disqualification in affidavit matters. At any rate, the question of disqualification to certify acknowledgments and depositions has been featured in many lawsuits, and many fact cases have been reviewed and analyzed, for the people's guidance. The question of disqualification in affidavit matters, has not often been raised. Only a few times, apparently, have the appellate courts given it attentive study. So that here we have fewer guide points. The affidavit certificate is of a different nature from the acknowledgment certificate, in this, that while the latter fits the certified paper for use in evidence, or for public

record, the former in many instances, is never intended for court or record. The one may form the basis of an indictment for perjury or false swearing, which the other can not, and there are other differences. If either certificate is falsely given, the officer giving same may be subject to indictment therefor, as well as liable for money damages for injury done. (5) Whether or not a notary is disqualified to certify an affidavit is a question of law of his own state. If he is in doubt, he should withhold the certificate until he has opportunity to consult a competent local attorney.

AFFIDAVIT BY TELEPHONE 

Q. May a notary lawfully certify an affidavit taken by telephone? 

A. (1) Some of the courts have said that a telephone affidavit certificate is necessarily illegal; others have upheld such certificates. The notary's safest policy is to refuse to certify the affidavit of any affiant who does not personally appear before him for that purpose. The reason for this fact is found in the wording of the certificate.

CERTIFICATE SAYS "PERSONALLY APPEARED" 

(2) Note that such certificate commonly contains words to the effect that the affiant "personally appeared" before the notary or that the affidavit was "subscribed and sworn to before me," etc. It all depends upon the wording of the official certificate.

AN ILLUSTRATION 

(3) Suppose A is a notary in Richmond, Virginia. A patron phones him to write an affidavit stating certain things. 

This the notary does, and notifies the patron that it is ready for his signature. The patron says: "I am swearing to it; just sign it for me (or let someone else there sign it for me) and place your certificate on it." Now, what should A do?

Well, he should refuse to certify that the patron has appeared before him, because the patron has not appeared before him. 

He might certify thus: "On this day (the patron) phoned me the following facts (here stating the facts set forth in the affidavit) and thereafter on this same day and date he (the patron) by telephone authorized me to sign (or authorized some other person, naming him, to sign) his (the patron's) name to the above and foregoing paper. And thereupon I (or the other person) did sign thereto the name (of the patron),

AFFIDAVITS 

145 and the said (patron) did then and there on said date, by telephone, swear to the facts hereinabove set forth."

FALSE CERTIFICATE, WHEN 

(4) Now, if the notary should attach to an affidavit an official certificate so worded, and if it would be accepted by the person or persons, or court, at whose request, or order, it was to be furnished, then it would seem that a telephone jurat might be good. But would it be accepted? That question could be answered only in each specific case as it arises. But if the notary certifies that an affiant has personally appeared before him, or has subscribed and sworn to a paper before him, when in fact the affiant has merely talked with him by telephone, then obviously the notary has simply given a false certificate. And this is true, no matter how worthy or innocent the notary's intentions may be.

THE NOTARY'S FEES 

Q. To what fee or compensation is a notary entitled for writing or certifying an affidavit?

A. In all the states, it is lawful for him to charge a fee for his official certificate. In some of the states he is allowed to charge for his services in writing the affidavit; in others he is forbidden to make such charge. (See the chapter on Fees and Earnings.)

FORM AND REQUISITES 

Q. Is it required that an affidavit shall be in a certain form?

A. (1) In some of the states a general affidavit form is prescribed by statute. (See the FORMS in this volume.) In many of the states, there are forms, or requisites for forms, prescribed for special or particular kinds of affidavits. The general rule is that any paper, to constitute an affidavit must be: 

First, in writing; 

Second, signed by the affiant; 

Third, officially certified.

The analysis which follows is believed to cover adequately the subject of the form and requisites of an affidavit of any kind well known to American jurisdictions. (2) An affidavit may consist of as many as nine constituent

parts, but the ordinary business affidavit may be clearly divided into five parts, namely: a. Venue, or caption; b. Introduction, or premises; c. Body, or statement; d. Signature; e. Jurat, or official certificate.

These five parts are essential; no affidavit is satisfactorily complete if either is omitted, notwithstanding the courts have at different times sustained as valid papers which omitted one or more of them. (3) A date is important, in fact, commonly necessary, and should never be omitted, but since it is part of any properly drawn business paper, it is not here mentioned as a separate constituent part. It may appear as part of the introduction or as related to the signature. (4) In some instances what is known as a preamble is desirable, beginning generally thus: Whereas, heretofore, etc. and reciting the facts which have led to the giving of the affidavit. More commonly this feature is altogether omitted. 

It should be included where it can be availed of to clarify the intent of the affiant, or purpose of the affidavit. (5) If the affidavit is for use in court it should have an additional feature, namely the title of the lawsuit with which it is identified and to be used. (6) If the instrument is for use in another jurisdiction, it may properly have attached an authentication of the official status of the notary who officially certifies it. 

TITLE 

Other than for use in court, need seldom exists for a title to an affidavit, and if it is intended for court use, one or more lawyers will be on hand to supervise its drafting, so that no reason is apparent for further attention here to the affidavit's title. If, or when, required, such title should show the style and number of the lawsuit, the names of the parties and any other fact or circumstance essential to its clear identification. It may be in any of several different forms. The following is often used: 

John Doe V. 

Richard Roe 

No. 5000 

In the Circuit Court 

Albany County, Georgia 

November Term, 1943.

AFFIDAVITS 

147 

VENUE OR CAPTION 

By venue is meant the place where the affidavit is being executed, the territory of the notary's official jurisdiction. An affidavit is a nullity unless sworn to and signed before, and officially certified by, some officer authorized to certify affidavits in the locality or jurisdiction where the affidavit is given. The venue is shown, therefore, to indicate the jurisdiction of the officer who does the certifying. The venue should be shown at the top or beginning, thus:

The State of Nebraska : 

County of 

Or if the instrument is of Federal nature: 

United States of America : 

District of Iowa :

Without an adequate showing of venue, there is no sufficient affidavit. The venue may be shown in any part of the instrument, that is to say, the beginning, the middle or the end, but it belongs at the beginning and should be shown there. The top or beginning of an instrument is commonly called its caption. 

For some reason the top or beginning of an affidavit has come to be called the venue.

INTRODUCTION OR PREMISE 

Good order requires that there be an introduction, or premise, or preliminary remark. If the instrument is drawn by a lawyer for court use it will ordinarily begin somewhat like this: "Now comes Mike Jones, plaintiff in above styled and numbered cause and shows to the court," etc. Or, "Mike Jones, plaintiff herein, being duly sworn, doth depose and say," etc.

An ordinary business affidavit, for use out of court, may introduce the affiant in terms substantially as follows: "Before me, the undersigned authority, (or before me, John T. Smith), a notary public in and for Jackson County, Missouri, on this day personally appeared William Brown, who resides at 412 E. Sycamore

Street in the city of Springfield, Eastland county, Ohio, who, being by me duly sworn, on his oath deposed and said:" etc.

BODY OR STATEMENT 

As elsewhere stated, the test of the sufficiency of an affidavit ordinarily is: Will it support a prosecution for perjury or false swearing, if proved to be untrue? This test is answered by the content of the body or statement, not the other parts. The body or statement is, therefore, the chiefly important part of the paper; without it, the other sections are merely parts of an empty shell.

SHOULD STATE FACTS 

It is fundamental that the purpose of an affidavit is to state facts. It should do this, nothing more or less. In court cases, such as attachment, garnishment, sequestration, continuance, injunction and the like, certain facts must be made to appear, or the affidavit is ineffective; the requirements being fixed by law they may not be varied or materially changed. But in ordinary business affairs, the body, statement, or content may be whatever the occasion requires, whatever is the truth and whatever the affiant wishes to swear.

WHO? WHAT? WHEN? WHERE? WHY? 

One who wishes to give competent service in writing an affidavit should avoid being rushed, take plenty of time, be sure he understands what the affiant wishes to swear, and that he is putting that—just that and nothing else into the paper. Keeping in mind the newspaper reporter's five questions will promote accuracy and comprehensiveness, namely: Who? What? When? 

Where? Why? Thus: Who is doing the swearing or is being sworn about? What is being sworn; what took place, what happened; what is going to happen? When did these things occur? 

Where, at what place, did the events unfold, or is the obligation to be fulfilled? Why, for what reason, was the thing done; the affidavit given, or why is he agreeing to do something? 

The introduction having projected John Doe into the picture, the body or statement may then take up the narrative and go on somewhat like this: "My name is John T. Doe. I am 62 years of age. I was born in Nelson County, Idaho, and have lived in said county all my life. I now reside at the Winchell ranch, five miles southeast of the town of Harrisburg." (Here follow with the statement.) "I swear to the foregoing statement, which is given solely in the interest of truth." 

AFFIANT'S SIGNATURE 

(1) Logical place for the affiant's signature is at the termination of the body of statement and immediately preceding the certificate, but it may be shown elsewhere without violating the instrument. Apparently if the signed name of the person swearing is shown anywhere on the paper, placed there with the intent to identify the signer as the maker of the affidavit, its exact location is not important. This writer thinks such signature should

AFFIDAVITS 149 be placed where it belongs, namely, following the affiant's own words in the statement, but it may be attached elsewhere and still be effective. (2) The notary's difficulty, if any, with the affiant's signature, is more likely to arise in those instances where the affidavit is made by one person for another or others, that is to say, in a representative or official capacity, as by one partner for the partnership, or a guardian for his ward, or a deputy for his chief, or an agent or officer for a corporation-than in those cases where the affiant signs for himself. State laws generally do not prescribe any particular form for such signature, and the courts' decisions have not engaged in detailed discussion. In one test case, the paper was signed thus: 

Markham Irrigation Company, by J. M. Moore, Its President and Agent. 

The court's comment was in part as follows: "The affidavit to the plea of abatement before set out was sufficient. The jurat of the clerk immediately beneath this signature, which is in the usual form, 'Sworn to and subscribed before me this 19th day of August, 1912,' can only be taken as a certificate or official statement that the plea was sworn to by Moore, who, as shown by the signature of the petition, had signed it as 'president and agent' of the appellant (Irrigation Company). We think it clear that a prosecution against 

Moore for false swearing might be predicated upon this affidavit, and this is the test of its sufficiency as an affidavit of Moore." In another test case, signature of the affiant, merely as "affiant," thus: 

Mike Jones, Affiant, was sustained, though the case was reversed on other grounds. (3) Affiant's signature must fit in with and correspond to the notary's certificate and the remainder of the instrument; in other words, he should sign in the same capacity that he swears. His name may appear first, followed by recital of his status of representative character; or the principal's name may appear first, followed by that of affiant as agent or representative. 

JURAT, OR OFFICIAL CERTIFICATE 

The officer's certificate to an affidavit is generally called "jurat," though in some jurisdictions it is referred to simply as the certificate or the official certificate.

"The jurat of an affidavit," says Corpus Juris, "Is that part of it, or addition to it, in which the officer certifies that it was sworn to before him." "It is essential," says Words & Phrases, "Not as a part of the affidavit, but as official evidence that the oath was taken before the proper officer."

In the making of an affidavit there are two parties, and only two, namely, the affiant and the officer who administers and certifies. (Of course more than one person may join in an affidavit, but in such cases, the act is as of one person, since they both swear to the same facts.) The affiant's only part is the body or statement, his own words; all the remainder is the work of the notary. 

Anything of official nature, that the notary says or does about the instrument is part of his certificate. The jurat, then, or certificate, consists not merely of the officer's signature and seal (if seal is used) but includes as well the title, caption and introduction.

Since, however, the word jurat is ordinarily used to mean only the following part, namely: 

Sworn to and subscribed before me this.. ..............day of 194......... (SEAL) 

Notary Public in and for..............County, State of………………………………….. it is, unless otherwise explained, so used in these pages. The logical place for the jurat is at the end or finish of the affidavit, but it may appear elsewhere without being for that reason alone defective. A jurat sufficient in form will contain at least these features: Name of the notary certifying, and his signature in official character; name of affiant, as signed; that he personally appeared before the notary; that he signed the paper and that he swore to it; date and seal. If venue is shown at the top or elsewhere in the affidavit itself, it need not appear in the jurat, but if venue is not shown in the affidavit itself, it should be shown in the jurat. The giving of a false jurat by any officer is everywhere a penal offense, punishable generally as a felony. 

AFFIDAVIT FOR RECORD 

Q. Is an affidavit, when properly certified, entitled to public record?

A. It is acknowledgment, rather than jurat, that entitles an instrument to be publicly recorded. If, therefore, it is desired to record an instrument, and if it is such an one as is properly entitled to record, then an acknowledgment certificate. should be attached, and the instrument filed with the proper

AFFIDAVITS 

151 recording officer. There are, though, numerous affidavits which are entitled to public record "on their own." Thus, in most states, the laborer's lien may be filed and recorded under an affidavit jurat, and there are others admissable to public record without acknowledgment or proof, other than the jurat. 

Under a Texas statute, an affidavit of heirship may be first sworn to, then acknowledged, whereupon it becomes admissable to public record, and after five years on record, unchallenged, it becomes prima face proof of its contents. No reason is perceived why any other affidavit may not be likewise acknowledged. Finally, though, the general rule is that an affidavit is not entitled, as such, to public record, unless under a particular statute providing therefor. 

AUTHENTICATION 

Q. If an affidavit be given in one jurisdiction for use in another, are any additional steps necessary to facilitate and insure such use?

A. The official status of the notary or other officer who certifies the affidavit, may require authentication. In some jurisdictions, any notary certificate under official seal is accepted as evidence without further authentication from any source. 

The general rule, though, still is that the official certificate of a notary or other officer in one jurisdiction will require authentication of such officer if his certificate is to be used in another jurisdiction. See the chapter on AUTHENTI 

CATION.

DEPOSITIONS Chapter 7 

Q. What is a deposition?

DEFINITION 

A. (1) A statement under oath, taken in writing, to be used in court, instead of the production of the witness. (2) The testimony of a witness reduced to writing, in due form of law, by virtue of a commission or other authority of a competent tribunal, or according to the provisions of some statute law, to be used on the trial of some question of fact. (3) It is the duly signed and sworn testimony in writing of a witness, legally taken for use in a judicial proceeding by a duly authorized officer and properly certified and returned by such officer.

DISTINGUISHED FROM AFFIDAVIT 

(4) Deposition is not to be confused with affidavit. The two are alike only in the one detail that in each a witness is testifying in writing under oath, ordinarily being called in the former a deponent and in the latter an affiant. A deposition can be given only when and where duly authorized by competent court or officer, while an affidavit may be given anywhere, any time, by any person able to sign his name or make his mark before an officer authorized to administer oaths. The deposition is taken for use, and may be used, on the trial of a cause before judge, jury, commissioner, council or tribunal; the affidavit is not ordinarily taken for court trial purposes and can not ordinarily be used as evidence in court, though there are instances where it may become admissable. In a deposition the testimony of a witness is limited, theoretically at least, by the requirement of specific answers to direct and cross interrogatories, oral or written; in an affidavit there is no such limitation, and the testimony may be whatever his conscience, judgment and knowledge of the facts may prompt the affiant to state. 

NOT ONE AND THE SAME 

(5) "An affidavit and a deposition," says the New York supreme court in the case of Zinner et al v. Meyers, 43 N.Y.

DEPOSITIONS 

153 

S. 2d, 319, date of June, 1943, "are not one and the same, merely because the affidavit is under oath. An affidavit is an ex parte statement, made without notice, and with no opportunity for cross-examination; a deposition is testimony given under oath pursuant to notice upon oral or written interrogatories, and with opportunity for cross-examination." 

EX PARTE, COMPULSORY 

"A deposition," says American Jurisprudence, Vol. 1, page 

935, "in its more technical and appropriate sense, is limited to the written testimony of a witness given in the course of a judicial proceeding, either at law or in equity, in response to interrogatories, oral or written, with an opportunity for cross-examination. An affidavit is a voluntary statement made ex parte, without notice to the adverse party or an opportunity to cross-examine. Depositions, on the other hand, are taken only after notice, whereby the adverse party is given an opportunity to cross-examine the witness concerning the subject matter. Moreover, the giving of a deposition may be compelled, so that it is not in all instances, a voluntary statement. 

ALL WRITTEN EVIDENCE 

"A deposition," says the Supreme Court of Michigan, in 

Walden v. Gregg's Estate, 285 N. W. 461, date of April, 1939, "is a general expression, embracing all written evidence. verified by oath, including affidavits." 

VOLUNTARY, SUBPOENAED 

"The distinction," says a Federal Circuit Court, in Crenshaw v. Miller, 111 Fed. 450, "between an affidavit and a deposition is that the former is ex parte and voluntary, and the latter is made after notice, and is compulsory. An affidavit is a voluntary, ex parte statement formally reduced to writing and sworn to or affirmed before some officer authorized by law to take it. If the witness is subpoenaed, sworn, and required to answer, his evidence reduced to writing is his deposition, and it could only be taken on notice with the right of cross-examination."

LEGAL TERMINOLOGY 

"A deposition," says Cor. Juris, Sec., "is a generic expression which embraces all written evidence verified by oath, including affidavits, and is sometimes used as synonymous with oath; but as a word of legal terminology, it is usually limited

to the testimony of a witness, taken in writing, under oath or affirmation, before some judicial officer, in answer to interrogatives, oral or written."

WITNESS, AFFIANT, DEPONENT 

Q. What difference is there in the words, Witness, Affiant, Deponent?

A. The word witness may be either noun or verb. In these pages it is used as a noun, i. e., a person giving testimony under oath, in or out of court. Affiant is one who signs and makes oath in form of an affidavit. Deponent is one who gives testimony in the form of a deposition, a person, we say, whose "deposition has been, or is being, taken." 

Q. What is a subpoena?

SUBPOENA 

A. (1) The paper issued by court clerk or other issuing authority, by order of the judge of the court, or other officer or tribunal, commanding the person or persons therein named to be and appear before the issuing authority at a time and place therein named, to give testimony in a named cause. (2) If the witness is ordered to bring with him files, records or books in his possession or control, whether his or another's, for use as evidence, the summons or command is called subpoena duces tecum; if only his individual testimony is sought, the summons or command is called subpoena ad testificandum, or just plain "subpoena". (3) A subpoena is "served" or delivered to the summoned witness by an officer, sheriff, constable, marshal, or other, or deputy, which officer makes on a copy thereof his notation of the time and place where service made, returning such copy to the issuing authority for record in the case. 

LETTERS ROGATORY 

Q. What is meant by the expression, "Letters Rogatory"?

A. By letters rogatory is meant a formal communication from a court in which an action is pending to a foreign court requesting that the testimony of a witness residing in such foreign jurisdiction may be taken under the direction of the court addressed, and transmitted to the court making the request. 

This is a procedure seldom utilized these days, it having been practically discontinued in favor of the deposition. However, once in a while it is called into practice. A notary served with

DEPOSITIONS 

155 notice of a letters rogatory commission will find his duties thereunder practically the same as under a deposition commission. If he understands deposition procedure he need not anticipate any special difficulty with letters rogatory. 

DE BENE ESSE 

Q. What is a deposition de bene esse?

A. The phrase "de bene esse" means "provisionally" or "conditionally", referring to the right to use the deposition in absence of deponent at time of trial. Thus a witness may be sick, or infirm, or for some reason prospectively unable to attend a trial, and a party to the pending proceeding may wish to take the deposition for use if deponent absent, but will use deponent as a witness, instead of the deposition, if deponent present at time of trial. The expression has no bearing upon the notary's duties under the commission. 

DEDIMUS POTESTATEM 

Q. What is meant by "dedimus potestatem?

A. The phrase means literally "we have given power." In legal use it refers to the writ sometimes issued by a court or judge, or justice or tribunal, authorizing an individual therein named to act as a judge, question witnesses, etc. 

USE 

Q. What use is made of depositions? 

A. Depositions are used in judicial proceedings, as in trial of causes, civil or criminal, in any trial court, state or Federal also in hearings before any tribunal or board, or bureau of the government, such as the Inter-State Commerce Commission, a state railroad commission, etc. The use of depositions for the presentation and preservation of testimony is general everywhere.

NATURE OF NOTARY'S DUTY 

Q. In taking a deposition, what is the nature of the notary's duty?

A. (1) Whether a notary in taking a deposition is actually participating in the trial of the cause or is only in charge of a preparatory step is something the appellate courts have not agreed about and need not be argued here. Though not a court, nor clothed with the usual paraphernalia of such a tribunal, he is charged with the high function of seeing that testimony intend-

ed for use on the trial is developed according to law. His responsibility is real, not merely imagined; his duty is an important one; trust and confidence have been reposed in him and he should respond with diligent and efficient service. (2) The notary appointed to take a deposition is not a mere agent of a party or parties to a lawsuit. Rather he is an agent of, and substitute for, the court wherein the alwsuit is pending.

SOURCE OF NOTARY'S AUTHORITY 

In the case of Burnett v. Prince, 197 Southwestern Reporter, page 241, the supreme court of Missouri said: "An officer taking depositions in a cause acts in a judicial capacity, and his authority is derived from the court in which the cause is pending; by conformity to the statutes certain officials in Section 6387 may become temporary substitutes for the court, and as such take testimony in the form of depositions."

In the case of State ex rel Bechtel v. McCabe, 20th Northeastern Reporter, 2nd Series, the Court of Appeals of Ohio said: "The statutes of Ohio (cited) give the notary the powers, authority and processes of law, independent of the trial court, to take depositions and to punish, by proceedings in contempt, a witness who refuses to answer. The notary's powers and processes flow from the statutory law on the subject-not from the court in which a trial is pending."

DEPOSITION SUPPRESSED, WHEN 

Q. What may happen if the notary fails in his duty?

A. If a deposition is improperly executed, or improperly returned, in whole or in part, it may be suppressed in court, and its use forbidden. When that happens, justice may miscarry, and the parties who have relied upon the notary to attend to his duties properly may suffer serious annoyance, inconvenience and loss. Court records show the suppression of depositions to be of fairly common occurrence. 

NOT DIFFICULT 

Q. Is the taking of a deposition a difficult work?

A. The details or "mechanics" are fairly simple, if studied and understood. However, if a notary undertakes such a task

DEPOSITIONS 

157 without understanding what his duties are, he may easily "ball up" the situation. Occasionally he may have the benefit of legal counsel, but oftener not.

NOTARY RESPONSIBLE, WHEN 

Q. For what part, or stage, of the taking of a deposition, is the notary responsible?

A. (1) For convenience and clarity of expression we may here. divide the life of a deposition into three stages: 

1. Prior to delivery of the commission to a notary; 

2. While the commission is in the notary's hands; 

3. After the commission's execution and return. 

Steps taken during the first and third stages are in charge of litigants or their lawyers, subject to direction of the court or bureau in which suit or action is pending; the notary has no control over these proceedings, is not responsible for their proper discharge and we need not inquire into them here. (2) For things done in the second stage, however, from the moment the commission is delivered to the notary until he returns it to the source from whence it came, he is responsible; during this stage he earns his money or fails to earn it; does his lawful duty or fails to do it; takes the deposition properly and makes his return in due and legal form, or fails to do so. Hence it is to the second stage that this entire chapter is primarily directed, reference to any period of the first or third stage is merely to clarify some feature of the second. To repeat: A notary's first contact with a deposition is when the commission with interrogatories is delivered to him; his connection terminates when he returns the commission, interrogatories, answers, exhibits, certificate to the source from which he received the commission. His responsibility begins when he receives and accepts the commission, ends when he makes. his return.

TAKEN HOW? 

Q. How should a deposition be taken?

A. No argument is needed to prove that a deposition should be fairly and honestly taken. Yet court records show that sometimes a lawyer or a witness will resort to one or another of various clever devices to fortify or emphasize part or all of a witness' testimony. Perhaps one of the most frequently used of such devices is that of the lawyer writing out in full the de-

sired answers of the witness and the witness repeating such answers parrot-like when called and interrogated by the notary. Concerning which device one of the higher courts has said: "A witness ought not to write his deposition or his answers beforehand, nor should they be written beforehand for him by counsel or any other person, but he ought to answer the questions orally, or from memory as they are propounded to him. A deposition drawn up privately by one of the counsel in the cause from the mouth of the witness and afterward sworn to before a justice, under a rule to take depositions, is not admissible. A deposition ought to be reduced to writing from the mouth of the witness, in the presence of the magistrate."

MAY HAVE HELP 

Q. Does that mean that neither a notary nor a witness may receive help in giving and taking answers to questions? 

A. No, it does not mean that. A well recognized rule is that a witness when testifying may refer to memoranda to refresh his memory and the foregoing expression of judicial opinion does not forbid that practice, nor is it to be understood as always forbidding a notary to receive help from others in the mere preparation of his official papers. For example, in a case where it was shown that an attorney prepared the return envelope and the notary merely adopted and used such envelope, complaint and objection having been made by the adverse side, the court on appeal said it was not improper for the lawyer to give and the notary to receive such assistance, it not appearing that the merits of the deposition were in any way affected. 

But in another case, where the record disclosed that an attorney for one of the parties to the lawsuit furnished the notary, in another state, a lengthy and detailed memorandum of the answers that were desired from a certain witness and the notary made use of such memorandum in getting the answers a supreme court declared that a deposition so obtained could not be used.

MAY CONSULT LAWYER 

The most important part of the notary's duty is to see that the deposition is taken fairly and honestly according to law. What happens to it before it reaches him, or after he returns it, are things beyond his control, but while it is in his hands, he is responsible. If, as frequently happens, he feels the need of a

DEPOSITIONS 

159 lawyer's advice upon any point, his safest course is to consult a lawyer not interested in the case. 

UPON RECEIPT OF COMMISSION 

Q. When he receives the deposition commission what then is the notary's primary duty?

A. To get the deposition. He is not, of course, under any legal compulsion to take the deposition; if he does not wish to take it he is at perfect liberty to return the commission unexecuted, to the source from which he received it. But it is to be taken for granted that he will wish to execute the commission. 

Q. May he ask for additional instructions, and from whom?

A. If he is in doubt about any particular matter, he may inquire of the clerk or court from whom or which the commission was received. Or he may consult a competent and disinterested attorney, about the procedure to be followed. 

SHOULD GET THE DEPOSITION 

Q.

If a witness is not qualified to testify, what may the notary do? 

A. Under the law some persons are not permitted to testify as witnesses under any circumstances and others are not permitted to testify in certain classes of cases or upon certain points, but any review of the laws providing who may testify and to what, would be out of place here, since, if a commission is placed in a notary's hands it becomes his duty to get the deposition. Whether the testimony can be used on trial or not, or whether it is of any value to either party to the lawsuit, is no concern of his. He should get the deposition of the witness or witnesses, whether it is legal for them to testify or not; the legality of their testimony is something for the lawyers and the court to worry over, not the notary. If the witness is ill, aged, infirm, of doubtful mentality, or if, for any other reason the notary does not wish to go forward with the taking of the testimony, he is under no penalty of the law to do so. 

He may as before stated, decline to execute the commission, but if he does this he should act very promptly so that the litigant may have time to make other arrangements if he wishes. A fact that may properly be emphasized here is that even though it may not be lawful for the witness to testify in the particular case, yet if the notary has received and accepted the commission, it is his duty to go ahead and get the deposition, make due return thereof and then the court wherein

the case is pending can and will pass upon the value or admissibility of the testimony. A notary may receive a commission to take the deposition of a person of unsound mind, or of a husband or wife as witness in a criminal case wherein the other spouse is defendant. Now if he takes such depositions and returns them they may be rejected and suppressed in court, nevertheless, he will have done his duty to obey the commission and will be entitled to collect his official fees therefor. 

It will sometimes happen that the notary will be asked to certify in his return that the witness is of sound mind. If he doubts the sanity of the witness in such case, he should decline to take the deposition, simply returning the commission to the source from whence it came.

NOTARY DISQUALIFIED 

Q. Under what facts may a notary be disqualified to take a deposition?

A. (1) He may be disqualified because he is not a disinterested person. Thus, if he is a party to the lawsuit, he is clearly disqualified. On one occasion the supreme court of Texas declared: "The duties of the officer in taking down the answers of the witness involve in some degree the exercise of judgment and discretion, and it must be held, on general principles, that he should stand impartial as between the parties." (2) If a deposition is taken by a notary who is disqualified by interest, it may be suppressed and rejected in court, when the facts are made known to the court. When a notary is preparing to take a deposition, or at any stage of the proceedings, if it appears that he is disqualified, he should return the commission not executed. (3) As to the nature or kind of interest that will disqualify, the authorities are not agreed. But, generally speaking, the following rules seem fairly well established, namely, that he is disqualified, if: a. He is a party to the suit, or attorney for a party; b. He is a member of a firm or partnership that is party to the suit; c. He is stockholder in a corporation that is party to the suit; d. There is any fact in the situation which gives to such notary the relation of employe of any party to the suit; e. If he is surety for costs in the suit;

DEPOSITIONS 

161 f. He is close kin, by blood or marriage to any party to the suit. This disqualification has been extended to parents, brothers and sisters, children, even to uncles and cousins, though in some jurisdictions the fact of relationship to a party to the suit is not ground for disqualification. (4) In a case which went up on appeal, a notary who was employed in a mercantile establishment belonging to a party to the suit, was held to be disqualified. (5) In another case, where parties to a lawsuit employed a notary-lawyer in a distant city to search for and find witnesses and interview them, and paid him a cash fee for that service, then sent the commission for the depositions of such witnesses to him for execution, the appellate court held that such employment disqualified him and that the depositions taken by him could not be used on trial of such lawsuit. (6) Where the question of disqualification for interest was raised on a trial, and it was proved that the witness whose deposition was under challenge, was a client in another case of the lawyer-notary who took the deposition, the deposition was ruled out. (7) In a case where a notary who was also a lawyer, took a deposition, not being at that time an attorney in the case, but was afterward so employed, the court held that such subsequent employment did not invalidate or nullify the deposition. (8) Where the evidence showed that the notary-lawyer who took the deposition had an office in the same room as the attorneys for a party to the suit, and was in fact the attorney for one of the parties in other lawsuits of like nature, but not in this one, the court held on appeal that there was no disqualification because of such employment and association. (9) Many other cases might be cited, but it is thought that the foregoing will illustrate the statement previously made that the authorities are not agreed as to the kind or nature of interest that will disqualify. A notary in doubt should consult a competent and disinterested attorney. (10) The fact of disqualification may be waived by the parties but if such waiver is to be relied upon it should be entered into openly, signed by all the parties at interest or their attorneys and should be made part of the record in the deposition proceedings, preferably a part of the caption.

EVIDENCE OF AUTHORITY 

Q. By what authority does a notary take depositions?

A. (1) By authority of a deposition commission duly and legally issued and delivered to him, or caused to be delivered to him, by a competent court or officer. Such commission may call for the taking of testimony of one witness, or of two or more witnesses.

REQUISITES OF COMMISSION 

(2) The commission should be properly signed and dated, and if from a court having a seal, the impress of the seal should be shown. It should name the notary to whom it is addressed, name the witness or witnesses, and show the style and number of the lawsuit or other proceeding in which the testimony is to be used. If it lacks either or any of these essentials it may be returned for correction. If the notary does not understand it, or any part of it, he may ask for further instructions, or consult a competent local attorney as to the procedure which he should folow. 

ORAL, OR ON INTERROGATORIES 

(3) If the deposition is to be on interrogatories which accompany it, the notary may himself set the time and place for taking the answers; if the deposition is to be on oral examination, the commission should state the time and place when and where such deposition will be taken. These two rules are not strictly followed in all the states. 

DEPOSITIONS OF DIFFERENT KINDS 

(4) Depositions are of different kinds, and the witnesses are of different kinds; for example there are depositions of ordinary witnesses, and then there are party depositions and ex parte depositions. The notary need not concern himself about these differences and distinctions, since his duties are fully charted for either kind.

GETTING THE ANSWERS 

Q. When a notary receives a commission to take answers to written interrogatories accompanying the commission, how should he proceed?

A. He should follow the successive steps laid out by the statutes, decisions and court rules of his own notarial jurisdiction. 

These are not the same in all the states, yet there is a surprising similarity in the requirements, and it is believed that the

DEPOSITIONS 

163 4 notary who intelligently follows the suggestions following will have little difficulty in properly performing his official duty. When in doubt he should consult a competent and disinterested attorney.

TIME AND PLACE 

Q. By whom, and under what laws or rules, are the time and place fixed for the deposition hearing?

A. (1) A previous statement to the effect that the notary may himself fix the time and place is subject of course to general law and to the orders of the court or other tribunal out of which the deposition commission is issued. In some instances the time and place are fixed by statute; in others the commission will name the time and place as ordered by the court. If the parties or their attorneys wish to agree upon a time and place, they may do so, but this is subject to concurrence of the notary, and the reasonable convenience of the deponent. ORAL (2) If the hearing is to be on oral questions, due notice must be given to every party. But it will be supposed that this notice has been given by the attorney applying for the commission, a step for which the notary is not responsible. If the commission states when and where such oral depositions are to be taken, the duty of the notary is to acquiesce in the arrangements so made. However, his (the notary's) convenience is also to be considered, and if there is undue hardship or inconvenience in the arrangements so made, he is at liberty to decline to act.

WRITTEN 

(3) If the deposition is one based upon written interrogatories, then it is not ordinarily supposed that any party to the proceeding, or his attorney, will be present, and the notary, in absence of law or court order to the contrary, may himself fix the time and the place. In doing this he should, if he can, defer to the reasonable convenience of the witness. 

PLACE SELECTED 

(4) Unless otherwise ordered by court, or fixed by law, the place may be wherever suits the notary, public or private. He may go to the home or place of business of the witness, if the witness so desires. or he may have the witness meet him at his (the notary's) home or place of business, or elsewhere. A

statute in Louisiana provides that a notary must perform such duties in his own office unless illness of a party, or other sufficient cause, necessitates resort to some other place. 

TIME RE-SET 

(5) If the commission states a definite time for the deposition hearing, that time should be observed. The parties have the legal right to waive and re-set the time, if they wish to do so, in event of oral questions and answers, but if such waiver and re-setting are agreed upon, the notary should see that the record shows such waiver and re-setting, otherwise his certificate and return will not fit the requirements and orders of the commission, and the deposition may for that reason be rejected and suppressed when offered in court. Since, in case of written interrogatorics, only the convenience of the notary and the deponent are to be considered, there is ordinarily no need that the record show waiver and re-setting. 

SUNDAY, LEGAL HOLIDAY 

(6) Unless in case of emergency, the deposition should not be taken on Sunday or a legal holiday. This, not only because of reverence for the Sabbath, and for the laws of the land in fixing holidays, but as well because if the hearing is held on such a day, some alert lawyer in court may rise and challenge the legality. His objection may be overruled, because in practically all the states, it is not illegal to take a deposition on such a day, but the notary may come in for criticism, and the effect of the witness' testimony may be thereby to some extent marred or weakened. 

DEPONENT'S CO-OPERATION 

Q. Suppose the witness is not co-operative — what then?

A. (1) Having been notified, either formally by means of notice in writing, if so directed in the commission, or informally, by verbal communication, if no written notice is commanded to be given, it is ordinarily expected that he will promptly appear at the time and place named, and there give his answers to the questions. (2) Strictly speaking, the notary may be under no legal obligation or duty to consult deponent (witness) about his, deponent's, convenience in the deposition proceeding or any part thereof, yet there would seem no reasonable inhibition against exhibition by the notary of a proper spirit of accommodation.

DEPOSITIONS 

165 

AUTHORITY TO COMPEL ANSWERS 

Q. What authority, if any, has a notary to compel a witness to appear and give answers in a deposition? 

A. (1) In some of the states, the notary is clothed with authority to commit to jail for contempt, as of a court, so that if the witness does not obey the subpoena and attend the hearing and give answer, he may be punished as for contempt, and an attachment may be issued to forcibly bring him before the notary. to give such answers. (2) In others of the states, any authority of a notary public to punish a witness is denied, as being an infringement by such notary upon the prerogative of a court, a power never intended to be given to a notary. The appellate courts in the different states simply do not agree about this. (3) In some of the states where the notary's right to compel a witness to testify is denied, the procedure to compel testimony is set out by statute providing that if a witness refuses to attend, or being in attendance refuses to testify, the notary may report the matter back to the judge of the court or tribunal from whence the deposition commission came, and thereupon the court or tribunal takes over, and either compels the witness to testify, confines him to jail for contempt and disobedience, or turns him loose. (4) Columes could be quoted from the opinion of appeal courts upon this subject of the right of the notary to compel a witness to attend and testify, but it is not perceived that exhaustive discussion and review of that subject would be particularly helpful here. In fewest words: In some of the states, a notary can compel a witness to appear and answer the questions, or punish him for his refusal to answer; in others of the states, he has the recourse of referring back to the court or other authority from whence the commission came, the whole matter of the witness' comtumacy, and abiding the action of the higher authority.

LEGAL QUESTION, NOT NOTARIAL 

(5) Actually this is a legal question, not notarial. If the notary is a lawyer, he will be, or should be, familiar with the law governing such situations, and can carve out the route for himself; if he is not a lawyer, probably his best course is to consult a competent local attorney, one of course who is in no way interested in the case. However, the lawyer may naturally feel like asking for a fee for his advice and/or services, and

the notary not being allowed to tax such a fee as part of his official charges may not feel like paying. He will not need an attorney's services merely to make his report back to the court or other source of the deposition commission, and quite often that is the wisest indeed the only step for him to take.

WRITING THE ANSWERS 

Q. How should deponent's answers be written or recorded? 

A. (1) It is the notary's duty and responsibility to see that the answers, whether to oral questions or to written interrogatories, are correctly written. He may attend to this himself, or he may have it done by any other disinterested person. Pen and ink, or typewriter, should be used. It should be done in the notary's presence and under his direction. Frequently the notary will be a shorthand expert, capable of taking the testimony himself as it is given, and this is a legal and acceptable way to proceed.

AGREEMENTS PERMITTED 

(2) Subject to the notary's sanction, the parties to the lawsuit may make practically such agreement as they wish with reference to writing the answers. Very often it will be agreed that the answers may be taken in shorthand and afterward transcribed and the transcript presented to the witness by the notary for his approval and signature. When any such agreement is made, written record thereof should be made by the notary, and shown in his return of the commission and answers; it should be in writing, signed by the parties or attorneys. Occasionally the parties or attorneys will even agree that the transcribed notes need not be signed by the witness but may be returned without his signature. Acquiesence by the notary in such a procedure is not recommended. He is commissioned to get the deponent's deposition answers to questions and should get them, with signature attached. 

NOT WRITTEN BY ATTORNEY 

(3) No attorney in the case should be permitted to write the answers or to assist in writing the answers. Occasionally this will be allowed, but the practice is improper and is widely condemned. One state supreme court recently stated: "The trial courts should, we believe, suppress all depositions wherein the parties to a suit, their agent or attorney, act in a capacity of clerk or typist in taking the depositions of a witness in a case."

DEPOSITIONS 

167 

ERASURES, CORRECTIONS, INTERLINEATIONS 

(4) The clerical work should be neatly and accurately done; lead pencil should not be used. Corrections, erasures and interlineations are to be avoided, as far as conveniently possible; that such things mar the good appearance of the document may or may not be important; that they may create doubts in the minds of court or jury in the hour of trial is unmistakably important. But the writing or typewriting should be made to reflect the very meaning of the witness, and if after the answers have been placed on paper, errors or omissions are discovered, these may be corrected or supplied by means of erasures and interlineations if necessary, provided it is done before signing and certifying, on request of the witness, and no fraud is attempted.

TWO OR MORE, TAKEN SEPARATELY 

(5) If the commission directs the taking of answers of two or more persons, these should be taken separately, not together. 

Ordinarily it is best that no one of such witnesses be permitted to hear the testimony of another, though there is no general rule to this effect, so far as this writer has observed. All the depositions taken under one commission should be certified and returned together under that commission. 

HEARING MAY BE RECESSED 

(6) If the taking of the answers is not completed at once the hearing may be recessed to another time, or place, as suits the convenience of the parties. If, or when, such recess or adjournment is taken, the notary should have the record of the deposition to show such action, the reason therefor, and that the parties or some of them, agreed, or did not agree thereto. 

GOOD ORDER MAINTAINED 

(7) It is the duty of the notary to see that good order is maintained. Actually it is not within contemplation of the law, when interrogatories are being answered, that anyone shall be present except the notary and the witness, perhaps a stenographer and interpreter. Answers to oral questions will of course necessitate presence of others, but only those who are duly authorized, should take any part whatever in the proceedings. Nearly always, in case of interrogatories there will be an interrogatory which requires of the witness a statement as to who are present, where the proceedings are being had, and the time.

WITNESS, INTERPRETER, SWORN 

Q. Who administers oath or affirmation to deponent, interpreter? 

A. (1) The notary. It is essential that the witness be sworn. 

The rule generally is that he must be sworn before he answers questions or interrogatories, and that he must be thereafter sworn when he attaches his signature. Unless the law of the state prescribes a different form of oath or affirmation, the following will be sufficient for administration before the answers are taken: "You (the witness) do solemnly swear (or affirm) that you will give true answers to such interrogatories as may be propounded to you (or such oral questions as may be asked you) in the case of (here state number and style of lawsuit or other proceeding, and state where pending) So help you, God," to which the deponent should give affirmative answer that he does so swear or affirm. (2) After the answers are given, if the deponent is to be again sworn, unless the law of the state prescribes a different form, the following may be used: "You do solemnly swear (or affirm) that the answers which you have given and signed in answer to interrogatories (or oral questions) propounded to you in the case of (here give number and style of lawsuit or other proceeding, and where pending) are true and correct, so help you God." To which the witness should give affirmative response. (3) If an interpreter is to be used, other than the notary himself, he may be sworn or affirmed in somewhat the following language: "You do solemnly swear (or affirm) that in the case of (here state style and number of case, and where pending) wherein you are acting as interpreter of the answers of the deponent (here name the deponent) to translate his answers from the (French, German, Italian, or other) language into the English language, you will faithfully, correctly and impartially perform your duties as such interpreter, and that you will true and accurate interpretation and translation give of all questions propounded by you to such witness and of all answers received by you from such witness, So help you, God." 

To which the interpreter should give affirmative response. 

The administration of this oath, and the form thereof should be included in the notary's transcript of the proceedings. 

INFORMATION and Belief (4) If it is desired to administer the oath or affirmation on "information and belief," rather than in direct and positive form, or if the deponent is to testify only as to his "knowledge

DEPOSITIONS 

169 and belief," the form of the oath or affirmation administered may be altered to fit that situation. may

NOTARY MAY TRANSLATE 

(5) If the witness is ignorant of English, or if he is mute and testifies only in sign language, an interpreter may be needed. If the witness does not speak English, the notary himself translate and write down the correct answers in English; or he may write them in the foreign language and transmit them to the court where they may be translated for use in evidence. If the notary acts as interpreter, there is no need to swear him for that duty; if he appoints someone else to interpret, but himself understands the foreign language, then the interpreter need not be sworn, since the notary will understand and can certify the correctness of the answers. The court will presume that correct interpretation has been made, but if proof is made of fraudulent or inaccurate interpretation the deposition will be rejected and suppressed. 

NUMERICAL ORDER, QUESTIONS 

(6) The direct interrogatories should be propounded to the witness in their numerical order, beginning with No. 1. When the first has been received, then No. 2 may be asked and when it has been answered, No. 3, and so on to the last and highest number. Cross-interrogatories should then be propounded in their numerical order, beginning with No. 1, then No. 2 and so on, down to the last and highest number, each answer being taken before another question is asked. It often happens that there are two or more sets of cross-interrogatories, in which event the notary may take up these sets of questions in the order in which the issuing authority has attached them to the commission. Care should be exercised that no question, direct or cross, be overlooked.

ANSWERS VERBATIM 

(7) Answers of the witnesses are to be taken verbatim. Of course they should be so written as to make sense, and a mere explanatory note or comment inserted by the notary to clear up an obscurity or ambiguity of meaning is not necessarily fatal to use of the deposition as evidence, but no liberties should be taken with the plain intent of the deponent's words. 

WITNESS READ ANSWERS 

(8) The purpose of the rule requiring that the interrogatories and answers be read over to the deponent before he signs the

deposition is that he may know what questions he has been asked, and whether the answers given are correct. If the answers are read to him singly as given, or if his entire testimony is read to him when it is all in and if, in either case, he is then permitted to read all of his answers himself, this is sufficient compliance with the rule. If he is blind, illiterate, unfamiliar with English or if, for any other reason, he cannot read the deposition for himself, that part of the proceedings must be dispensed with; if he is too deaf to hear the answers read, he must read them himself, and they need not be read to him. 

It might be added that there are persons whose depositions cannot be legally taken, for example one who is not only blind and mute, but also unable to write. 

DEPONENT'S SIGNATURE 

(9) In some of the states there is no deposition unless and until deponent's name is signed to the answers; in others, the signature is not essential, the deposition being acceptable if properly identified, and not otherwise under attack. In nearly all the states, the signing by deponent may be waived by the parties or their attorneys, though the practice of offering as evidence an unsigned deposition is quite often frowned upon. 

This is a question of statute in the different states. 

MAY SIGN BY MARK 

(10) If deponent is blind, illiterate, or for any other reason unable to write his name, and the deposition is taken in a jurisdiction where signature is required, then he may sign by mark. If he signs by mark, it is not improper that the record show why he so signs. 

SAME NAME (11) Due allowance is made everywhere for the custom of calling persons by other than their right names, but a deponent's signature should be signed as it is shown in the deposition commission, or if not so signed, then a note should be given indicating a reason for the difference. One signature may suffice for answers to both direct and cross-questions and interrogatories, though commonly, it is found better that a. signature be attached to each group of answers. EXHIBITS (12) A common use of the deposition is to prove up a paper or its contents. Such paper may be in the form of a blueprint, photograph, drawing, will, deed, promissory note, bill of sale

DEPOSITIONS 

171 contract or any of the other many forms in which business papers are afloat in the modern channels of trade. When it is sought to prove an instrument in writing by deposition the usual and recognized practice is for the witness or deponent to attach the instrument to his answer, marking and describing the same as an exhibit. Obviously, if a paper of any kind is attached to the answers, the answers must mention it and describe it for identification, and then such paper itself must also be marked in such way as to positively identify it as the instrument testified about. The witness should write on the paper, with ink, on margin or back side, his own name and the identifying word, letter or number. For example: William

Smith, Exhibit A. Then in his answers he should state that he has himself so marked it, such statement being in substantially these words: "I have signed the said paper William

Smith and have marked it Exhibit A." The notary taking the deposition may identify the instrument by writing thereon his certificate that it is the identical instrument presented to the witness and about which he testified, but it is better that this identification be made by the witness, in his own handwriting, or by his mark. No general statute upon the subject of exhibits on depositions has been noted. Nearly all the court decisions have in one way or another hinged on the question of identification. If a paper is too large or unwieldy to be included in the return envelope with the depositions, it may be returned separately. But its identification thereby becomes more difficult. In a case, for example, where X-Rav pictures were too large to be inclosed, they were marked Exhibits Nos. I and 2, and bore the signature of the notary, which was duly proved up in court, and the deposition and pictures were admitted in evidence. No matter how the exhibit may be gotten into court, if it is properly and positively identified as the paper testified about, it may be received in evidence as part of the deposition. It should be attached to the answers, marked as an exhibit and inclosed in the return envelope if its size and nature permit; otherwise, it may be transmitted to court separately. A witness may attach to his answers as an exhibit, even a paper which has not been requested. The interrogatory may ask the witness merely to state the contents of the involved paper; oftener it will ask that the original paper itself or a copy, be attached to the answers. Under the rules of evidence, ordinarily a witness is not allowed to state the contents of a paper if he is in possession of such paper or a copy, but must attach such paper or copy, as being the better evidence. The witness cannot be compelled to produce as ex-

hibits, his own private files or papers, yet if he is in possession of papers in which other parties have an interest, and he is asked to attach same to his answers, it is proper for him to comply with the request.

RULING ON TESTIMONY 

(13) If a deposition is being taken before a court or judge, and objections are offered by a party or attorney to a question or an answer, the court or judge may then and there overrule or sustain the objection. It is not, though, the duty of a notary public commissioned to take a deposition, to pass on the admissibility of evidence. It is proper to make note of an objection, so that it may be submitted to the court later when the deposition is offered on trial of the case, but the question should be allowed, and the answer should be written. This does not mean that the notary has entirely no control of the proceedings. He has the responsibility of going forward with the deposition, and of holding the proceedings within due bounds of propriety and relevancy, but he is not a judge or a court to pass upon the technical admissibility of question or answer.

WHAT LAW GOVERNS 

(14) A statement previously made, to the effect that a notary should follow the instructions outlined in the commission needs perhaps no repetition here, but it is not out of place to remind that a court or tribunal of one state has no jurisdiction beyond its own state lines. The notary is therefore bound to follow the laws and rules of his own state or jurisdiction, and subject to this, he should obey the instructions in the commission. If the commission is from a Federal authority, its orders are to be followed, ordinarily, without regard to state lines. 

ABSENCE OF PARTY OR WITNESS 

(15) A lawsuit or other judicial proceeding may often have more than one or two or three parties, and when a deposition is to be taken, it may be that all are entitled to be present. Or it may happen that the answers of several witnesses will be called for, to be taken on the same occasion, under the same commission, at the same time and place. Now in such a situation, of course all parties and all witnesses are entitled to be notified in due time and in lawful manner. But if it turns out that one or more of the witnesses, or one or more of the parties do not attend, the notary (or other officer) may nevertheless, if one witness, or one party, be present, go forward

DEPOSITIONS 

173 with the task in hand. The hearing may be postponed, of course, when circumstances warrant such postponement, or when all parties agree thereto; or recess may be taken, to another time and/or another place; but if all persons have been duly notified and some are there and waiting, but others are not, then it is up to the notary to use his own discretion to go forward for the convenience and interest of those who have heeded the notice.

FEES PAYABLE, WHEN 

(16) The notary's fees in deposition work are commonly paid by the party who asks to have the deposition taken. It will sometimes happen that the notary's fees are slow in being paid, indeed, sometimes never paid at all. This writer knows of no law compelling a notary to accept a commission to take a deposition unless he wishes to do so. If, therefore, a commission is tendered him, he may properly inquire in advance about the fee arrangements, and may ask for an advance or deposit to cover the charges for his services, before he performs them. In many places it is the custom that the notary be paid before or at time of making return of the commission, especially where a state line is crossed. 

NOTARY'S CHARGES 

(17) High authority exists for the statement that the laborer is worthy of his hire, and this maxim applies to the notary who takes a deposition. Attached to and as a part of his return of the commission should be shown an itemized statement of his charges, and an endorsement showing whether or not they have been paid; if paid, by whom; if not paid, then by whom owed. It is not practicable in a book of this kind to set out in detail the charges or fees that a notary may charge in any and every state or territory or district, for every type or phase of deposition-taking. Elsewhere schedules of fees are shown. These change from time to time. Some deposition forms show in full the charges which a notary may lawfully make in the given jurisdiction, and these may generally be relied upon as correct.

CAPTION 

(18) For the caption and the certificate the notary is responsible. They are his work. Elsewhere forms are shown. 

It is advisable to set up the caption before or at the time the hearing begins. Of course the certificate can not be framed until the hearing is completed.

CERTIFICATE 

(19) When the answers have been given and have been correctly written or typewritten; when they have been read by the witness, or have been read to him; when (in those states requiring the signature, and in those instances where the parties and attorneys have not waived the signature) he has signed his name in the necessary places and for the necessary number of times, once or more, and when the notary has administered to him the necessary oath (or affirmation) of that jurisdiction, then the notary may proceed to certify the deposition. 

Notwithstanding the requisites of the deposition certificate do not vary widely in the different states, there does not seem to have been adopted anything in the nature of a general country-wide certificate form. In those states where the statute sets out a form, it should be followed; if a statute fails to set out a form but prescribes certain essentials, those essentials should be observed. In any event, the certificate will not be sufficient unless it shows full compliance with the law governing such proceedings. It should show names of the parties; number and style of the lawsuit; court in which pending; date and place or places of the hearing or hearings; that each lawful step was taken in due order; that the answers were sworn to by the witness and (if signature required, and not waived) signed by him.
