SEAL OF OFFICE 

Chapter 10 

WHAT LAW Governs 

Q. What law governs the whole subject of the notary's seal of office?

A. The law of the state or of the Federal jurisdiction under which such notary holds his commission. 

Q. Are the laws of the different jurisdictions similar?

A. They are not identical. They vary widely. In nearly all the states a notary is required to have a seal of office and to authenticate therewith all of his official acts, but not all of the states require him to have a seal, and in some of the states where a seal is required omission thereof from certain of his certificates is not fatal to the validity of such certificates.

How ACQUIRED 

Q. How does a notary acquire his seal?

A. In nearly all jurisdictions he buys and pays for it out of his own pocket. In fact, this writer's study has brought to light only one instance of the seal being furnished the notary at public cost, that being in the State of Mississippi. 

IMPRINT, PRESCRIBED 

Q. How is the imprint or form of the notary's seal prescribed? 

A. It is prescribed by the law-making power in that jurisdiction. Here are shown two notary seal imprints: PUBLIC

VILON COUNTY O DALLAS EXAS 

CHAS E.NO

NOTARY 

OELL PUBLIC GEORGIA

ATLARGE 

STATE AT

LEXING 

INGT ON, GA

one in use in the State of Texas, the other in use in the 

State of Georgia. Under Texas law a notary seal must show a five-point star with the words "Notary Public, County of 

Texas." In Georgia the notary seal must show the name and address of the notary. In some of the states a notary is allowed considerable discretion in the content and imprint of his official seal, in others the precise words are specified by statute.

NECESSITY 

Q. Is the imprint of a notary's official seal an essential part of his official certificates?

A. In nearly all jurisdictions it is an essential part of his official certificates, and without it, in such jurisdictions, his certificates are incomplete and therefore void. In some other jurisdictions, the seal is not essential. The reader is referred to the state-by-state analysis in Chapter 1. 

PURPOSE 

Q. What is the purpose of a notary's official seal?

A. To stamp the certificate with the authority of a public officer; to authenticate the notary and in turn to authenticate the instrument certified. It is prima facie evidence of the notary's official character, and is generally regarded as self-proving. 

ONE JURISDICTION 

Q. If a notary in one jurisdiction uses, intentionally or by inadvertence, the seal of a notary in another jurisdiction, what is the effect?

A. There is no certificate; the attempt to certify fails. In an 

Iowa case (In Re Sale of Liquors in Valley Junction, Barber v. Ford, 150 N. W. Rep. 86) where it was shown that a notary's certificate given in Polk county, Iowa, was under seal of a notary in Jones county, Florida, the supreme court of Iowa held the certificate invalid. Without doubt this ruling would be followed anywhere the question arises.

DISPOSED OF, How 

Q. When a notary dies, removes, resigns, or otherwise vacates his office, what disposition is made of his official seal? 

A. In some of the states, Texas, for example, where all notary seals are alike, except for county name, he is permitted to sell the seal to another notary. In some other jurisdictions

SEAL OF OFFICE 

231 he is required to deposit his seal with some public authority. 

In others no provision is made for disposition of the seal when he ceases to exercise the duties of his office. 

WHERE SHOWN 

Q. Where should the imprint of the seal be shown on a notary's official certificate?

A. Obviously it should be shown imprinted on or immediately adjacent to the wording of his certificate and signature. 

This writer has found no instance of law or court decision requiring that the seal be stamped in any particular place. 

MENTION IN INSTRUMENT 

Q. Must an instrument which is being certified make mention of the notary's seal?

A. No rule has been found requiring that the notary's seal be mentioned in the written instrument itself. 

MENTION IN CERTIFICATE 

Q. Must the notary's seal be mentioned in the notary's official certificate?

A. In some jurisdictions it has been held that the notary's cerficate is faulty unless it mentions the seal and shows its imprint. In other jurisdictions there seems no requirement that the seal be mentioned at all, but only that it be shown. The usual certificate bears somewhere these words, "my hand and seal," and that mention of the seal is held sufficient. 

TWO KIND 

Q. How many kinds of seals are known to the law?

A. Two, namely, a public seal and a private seal. 

Q. Is a notary's official seal a public seal or a private seal?

A. The notary seal is the seal of a public officer, a public seal.

,

OFFICIAL RECORDS Chapter II NECESSITY 

Q. Why should a notary public keep records of his official acts? 

A. (1) For the same reason that any public officer keeps such records, namely, that his official acts deal with rights of property and of persons, and it is in the public interest that a written or printed record of such acts be preserved. (2) True, in many instances of the notary's official acts, such as taking and certifying acknowledgments to deeds, the instrument certified is forthwith filed for record with an official recorder, usually called county clerk, and it may, for that reason, be contended that the notary's record is superfluous and unnecessary. But let it be remembered that often such instruments are lost or destroyed before filing for permanent record, and thereupon the notary's record becomes the only official one of the instrument's execution. Obviously circumstances can arise, and they do arise, whereunder the notary's record of the execution of an instrument becomes very important. (3) Again, there are other instruments commonly entrusted to a notary for certification which are never recorded elsewhere than in his, the notary's own records. Records of protest, for example, are left to the notary alone for preservation. (At least, this writer has found no instance of law or custom providing any other than the notary for keeper of protest records.) If the notary keeps no official record of such transactions, there is none kept. Conceding that thousands of instances may pass where no record is needed or asked for, there yet remains the one where only by the notary's books can the truth be proved, and where if no notary record is available, injustice and wrong may be done and suffered. 

RECORD SHOULD SHOW, WHAT 

Q. What facts, or data, or information should the notary's official records contain? 

A. (1) This is governed by the statute of each state within its own boundaries. To set out in detail the various state laws applicable is not practicable here. In nearly all the states these points or facts are required to be shown:

a. The name, or names, of the party, or parties, if more than one. b. The date, the place, and the character or kind of instrument certified, that is to say, a deed, mortgage, sales contract, or whatever the paper certified may be. c. The kind of certificate given, that is to say, acknowledgment, affidavit or other. d. The kind of property dealt with, if any; as real estate or live stock or machinery, giving of course, a brief description. (2) In some jurisdictions only records of protests are required to be kept; in others, records of all official acts; in others, only those relating to real estate sales or incumbrances, in some one or more, but not all of the types of instruments which a notary may be called upon to certify. (3) Publishers and stationery concerns the country over carry in stock form books arranged for the notary's convenience and guidance in preserving the record of his official acts and certificates.

MUST FURNISH 

Q. How does a notary acquire official record books? 

A. By buying them and paying for them out of his own purse.

HOW DISPOSED OF 

Q. When a notary dies, resigns, moves or otherwise vacates his office, what is done with his official record books? 

A. (1) In some of the states there appears no statute governing the notary's final disposition of such records, and it would appear that in those jurisdictions he may preserve, destroy or otherwise dispose of them as he sees fit. But in others of the states there are strict regulations. Principal and most common of such regulations is that the notary upon vacating his office, or his heirs or legal representatives upon his death, shall forthwith deliver such records to the designated public custodian, usually the county clerk or recorder. The records are properly regarded as of public interest. (2) Requirement that the records be delivered to the public custodian, upon vacancy in the notary's office from death or otherwise, is supported in nearly all the states by provision for damages to any person injured by failure to make such delivery. Also by criminal penalties assessable upon conviction for such failure.

OFFICIAL RECORDS 

235 

CUSTODIAN'S CERTIFIED COPY 

Q. After the records have passed out of the notary's hands how may they be proved?

A. It is in some cases provided by statute that the custodian may give certified copies. Whether so provided or not it would seem that the custodian, being in all cases a public officer, may properly give a copy of any such record, certifying over his signature and seal, that it is a true and correct copy of a record on file in his office, describing such record, and the volume or record in which it is contained, naming the notary or other person from whom it was received, and stating the time when it was delivered into his or his predecessor's keeping.

PENALTY, FAILURE TO KEEP 

Q. For the notary's failure to keep official records, what penalty is provided?

A. (1) In those states-nearly all-where law requires the notary to keep official records, there are criminal punishments, as by fine, or imprisonment, or both, prescribed for his failure to do so. Cases are on record where the notary has been subjected to heavy monetary fine, and also to time in jail for his negligent, or wilful, disregard of these statutory provisions. (2) There are likewise in some states statutes expressly providing that any person injured by the notary's failure to keep official records may recover of the notary and/or his official bondsmen, damages in money as compensation for such injury. In a state or jurisdiction where the law requires the notary to keep such record, it would appear that the injured patron would have a right of damage recovery, whether the law so expressly provides or not. (3) Where no official bond is required, it would appear that a person injured by the notary's failure to keep official records, would have grounds for action against the notary personally, though there are court holdings which conflict with this idea. 

LAW OBSERVANCE 

Q. Is the law requiring a notary to keep such records well observed?

A. Extensive inquiry and observation lead to the belief that probably this part of the law is not very well observed.

NOTARY ADVISED TO KEEP 

Q. Is a notary advised to keep official records?

A. Definitely, yes; whether required by the laws of his jurisdiction or not, he should keep a fair record of all his official acts and certificates. (See Chapter 1)

REQUIREMENTS DIFFERENT 

Q. To what extent are the record requirements of the different jurisdictions identical?

A. In general they are quite similar, having as their object the same end. Yet, they vary widely in wording and in operative detail. Much might be written and many comparisons made, but it can not be undertaken here. Louisiana's set-up may be cited as the most comprehensive and perhaps one of the best observed. As typical of the requirements in Federal jurisdictions, here is an Alaska statute: "It shall be the duty of every notary public, on his resignation or removal from office or at the expiration of his term and, in case of his death, of his legal representative, to forthwith deposit all the records kept by him in the office of the clerk of the division of the district court in which he resides, and on failure to do so the person so offending is liable in damages to any person injured thereby. "It shall be the duty of each clerk metioned in section 34 of this title to receive and safely keep all records and papers of the notary in each case above named and to give attested copies of them under his seal, for which he may demand such fees as by law may be allowed to the notaries, and such copies shall have the same effect as if certified by the notary."

CRIMINAL OFFENSES 

Chapter 12 

PENALTIES PROVIDED 

Q. Are there offenses defined, and penalties provided, by criminal law, of which a notary may be found guilty, by reason of his holding the office of notary public?

A. There are statutes in practically all of the states regulating his conduct and prescribing punishment at law for his failure to obey such statutes, some of which apply to "acts of omission", and some to "acts of commission".

THREE SUBJECTS 

Q. To what notarial duties or subjects do such regulatory laws relate?

A. (1) Official certificates; official fees; official records. (2) In these pages it is more than once stated and explained that the giving of a false certificate may be an offense against the law. For example, if a notary certifies that a person "personally appeared" before him to swear or affirm, or to acknowledge, when such person did not so appear, then he may be found guilty of giving a false certificate and may be punished therefor by monetary fine, by confinement in jail or in penitentiary. For the wilful giving of any certificate which is false, the notary may become liable to punishment at law. 

Ignorance of facts, error, mistake, these may be offered in defense or in mitigation, and upon proper proof may clear the notary of blame.

FEES, EXTORTION 

(3) The law fixes the fees which he may lawfully charge, naming the service for which charge may be made, and setting the amount thereof. If in disregard or violation of such law he levies a charge which is not so authorized, or if he wilfully demands and receives one in larger amount than the law prescribes, he thereby becomes guilty of extortion and subject to punishment. An inadvertently excessive charge is not extortion; nor is it an offense for an officer to collect a fee to which he is not entitled if he does this in honest mistake.

Prompt restitution, though, upon discovery of error, is to be expected. (See Chapter 2.) 

FEE SCHEDULES, Reports (4) In nearly all the states he is required to keep posted con-

spicuously in his place of business a schedule or list of the official services for which he makes a charge, and the amount of each, and a penalty is assessed against him if he fails to keep such schedule or list so posted. In others it is required that he keep an accurate record of all fees collected and that such record be open to public inspection. In others, as in Maryland, periodic official fee reports are ordered. RECORDS (5) In some states no official records are required of the notary; in others records of one kind are required, in still others records of another kind. There is a lack of uniformity. 

But in those states where records are prescribed, the law would be a vain one if it did not also provide penalty for its disregard, and thus in nearly all jurisdictions where there are record requirements we find also penalty stipulations for their disregard.

PROTEST 

(6) Protest, which involves notarial certificates and notarial records and also provides for notarial fees, is one of the "usual duties" of a notary which is strongly supported by criminal penalties, differing in kind and amount but effective in nearly all jurisdictions.

WHAT PENALTIES 

Q. For such offenses, what penalties are provided?

A. (1) For official misconduct penalties applicable to all public officers, he has no exemption; that is to say, criminal laws applicable to public officers, apply to him as to others, with like measure of punishment. (2) Penalty in any instance depends upon the wording of the statute. There may be confinement in a penitentiary, or in a jail; or there may be a money fine. (3) Punishment by removal from office is sometimes provided. Also disqualification to hold office in future. (4) In any case where a notary may be punished at criminal law he may be likewise liable in damages to an injured party. Thus, for failure to do his full duty in protest, he may be liable to prosecution and to suit for damages. Or in giving a false certificate of acknowledgment to a deed, he may become subject to criminal indictment by the state, as well as liable to suit for damages by the party whose title to land has been abused by such false certificate.

BIRTH CERTIFICATES Chapter 13 IMPORTANCE 

Q. What persons should have a birth certificate?

A. Every person's birth certificate should be properly recorded, so that proof thereof may be made if need arises. The time has passed when everybody takes anybody's word for everything. Pension eligibility, property rights, family reputation, social standing, these and other things may depend upon proper proof of a person's time and place of birth. What is said here has reference to the individual need and benefit of a birth certificate. The necessity and importance to the public at large of birth-recording laws are apparent. General enactment of such laws throughout the nation in recent years constitutes but a belated recognition of a need long appreciated by many.

NOTARY'S CERTIFICATE NEEDED, WHEN 

Q. What duties has a notary in connection with preparation, execution or filing of a birth certificate?

A. A birth certificate, that is to say, the certificate of a person newly born, is commonly made out by the physician, or other attendant, does not need to be sworn to or acknowledged, and there is no need of a notary's services. In case of what is called a Delayed Birth Certificate, that is to say, the certificate of one not newly born, affidavit or acknowledgment is necessary and the services of a notary are required. 

FORMS Q. What about forms for Delayed Birth Certificates? 

A. The forms are furnished by the department in your state having charge of birth certificate matters. In some states the Department of Health is in charge, in other states some other department. If you do not know, write to the secretary of state at your state capitol for information, or ask your county clerk or county recorder.

NOT DIFFICULT SUBJECT 

Q. Is any special or particular knowledge or experience necessary to enable a notary to properly fill out a birth certificate?

A. No. If he has sufficient intelligence and education to serve as a notary public, and if he has studied and understands the chapters in this book on Certificates, Acknowledgments and Affidavits, he will certainly have no difficulty with birth certificates.

GENEALOGY 

Q. Within what general subject is the topic of birth certificates embraced?

A. (1) The general subject of genealogy, which in turn may be defined as a chronology or enumeration of descent; a charting or tracing of ancestry. Because legitimate births have from time immemorial come through families, genealogy is commonly mentioned as a matter of the family, rather than of the individual person. Actually, though, genealogical research is in most instances devoted to the tracing of the ancestry of a single individual, not of a family group. Obviously the birth certificate is an indispensible part of the structure of genealogy. (2) Peoples of the East, notably the Jews and the Arabs, carefully preserved and handed down from generation to generation their genealogical compilations; so did the ancient 

Greeks and some of the Celtic races of western Europe. The 

Bible more than once recites family chronology, which must inevitably have been based upon birth data. (3) Until modern times genealogy dealt with the ancestry of kings, nobles, persons of high and important station in life. 

The birth certificate requirement of today sets up a plan of genealogy for everyone, high and low alike.

PEDIGREE, THE FAMILY TREE 

Q. What relation does the birth certificate bear to one's pedigree, commonly called the family tree?

A. (1) The birth certificate is merely evidence of one's birth; it certifies the fact of the birth, the time and place. But it is the key to any pedigree; to any family tree. Pedigree, in law, covers births, marriages and deaths, but the birth is essential and first; its proof can not be dispensed with in charting a pedigree. Pedigree is a term often used to describe the charting of the ancestry of livestock, but in its origin it dealt with the ancestry of man. It is comparable to the ab stract of title to real estate. (2) In America in recent years and prior to the enactment

BIRTH CERTIFICATES 

241 of the birth certificate laws, interest in the family tree was increasing generally among the people. Many families who, in former times, professed to disdain any interest in ancestral stars had come to feel that after all a nice family tree is a good thing to have. Due perhaps to the growth of interest in the related subjects, nearly every one of the states has created a department of archives and history, under that name or some other, to facilitate the work of those who wish to assemble information of their forebears in this country. In 

Washington are many sources of family tree information for many families in America. First in importance as such a source is the accumulated record of the United States Census, taken every tenth year since 1790. In 1814 when the British burned Washington quite a lot, but not all, of the census records were destroyed, but those compiled since-tens of thousands of immense volumes, containing hundreds of millions of names and addresses-are intact. Those taken in 1870 or any earlier time, are accessible to anyone who wishes to inspect them. Information in those of later time is available upon application of persons entitled to it. Then there is that perfect wonder-land of information on countless subjects-the Library of Congress. And the National Archives and the records of the War Department, the Navy Department, the General Land Office. In short, an American wishing to chart his family tree back to Revolutionary days or beyond, is very often able to do so, notwithstanding in those earlier days there were no provisions for birth registration, such as we now have. Crystalization into statute law of the birth registration idea assures that in future generations one who is interested in his ancestry will have less difficulty and expense in procuring accurate data. (3) It may be added parenthetically that this writer has worked extensively in solving difficult and obscure genealogical-family tree-problems, where important property inheritances, and disputed heirships, were involved. There is no hesitancy in stating that engaging in such work is a fascinating and enjoyable way to pass the time; though it is not of the same nature in all the states. The Georgia and Virginia sources and data, for example, are widely different from those in Indiana and Pennsylvania. Second only to Washington in drama and variety, are the sources in Texas. In all of the states there are competent persons who follow genealogy as a livelihood. In this writer's numerous contacts with such persons he has heard related, and been privileged to examine, many interesting narratives of American life in the early

days-tales of frontier hardships, of strange disappearances, of startling discoveries. Where "missing heirs" are, or have been, sought, to claim their individual shares, the interest is vastly intensified. Especially is this true where-as sometimes happens-oil is involved.

AUTHENTICATION 

Chapter 14 

DEFINITIONS 

Q. What is meant by "authentication"?

A. (1) The word "authenticate" has been defined many times by high judicial authority. Two definitions picked at random are as follows: "A proper or legal attestation. Acts done with the view of causing an instrument to be known and identified." (In Re Fowler, 4th Fed. Rep. 303. New York.) "The authentication of a written instrument is such official attestation as will render it legally admissible in evidence." (Mayfield vs. Sears, 32 N. E. 816 Supreme 

Court of Indiana.) (2) Elsewhere in these pages we go into the matter of authenticating a written instrument, meaning the attachment thereto of the notary's official certificate. But the notary's official certificate which authenticates the written instrument must sometimes itself be authenticated. In other words, it is of common occurrence that double authentication is required; that is to say, an authentication of the written instrument, which identifies the signer of the written instrument and verifies his signature, then an authentication of the official certificate, identifying the personality of the notary and verifying his signature and official seal.

WHEN NECESSARY 

Q. When is authentication of a notary's official certificate necessary?

A. (1) As a general rule, it may be stated that in his own jurisdiction the notary's official certificate need not be authenticated by any other officer; that the written instrument which the notary has authenticated will be accepted for public record or as evidence in court. (2) If the written instrument which the notary has officially certified is to be used outside of the notary's jurisdiction, generally it will be necessary that the notary's official certificate be authenticated by some higher authority. This re-

quirement of further authentication, however, is not universal. 

There are some states where the law will admit to public record or in evidence a written instrument certified by a notary anywhere without any certification or authentication of the notary himself or of his official certificate. 

NECESSITY ARISES, WHEN 

(3) It will be found that the necessity for authentication of the notary's official certificate more frequently arises where a state line is crossed. However, in some of the states, if a notary's certificate is to be used outside the county of his own residence, then he must be vouched for; that is to say, his certificate must be authenticated by a clerk of a court of record having a seal or some other like authority of the county of his residence. Thus, an Illinois statute required that a deed acknowledged in Illinois before a justice of the peace should be supported by a certificate of the county clerk that such justice of the peace was an acting justice of the peace. That statute, however, further provided that no such certificate should be required if the acknowledgment was taken by a justice of the peace in the county where the land is situated. Attention is directed to the state-by-state analysis of authentication requirements, following in this chapter.

NOTARY EX OFFICIO 

Q. What difference, if any, exists in the requirements for authentication of an appointed notary and a notary ex officio? 

A. The authentication here discussed is that which verifies the official certificate of a notary public. The official certificate of a notary appointed will differ from that of a notary ex officio, at least in this, that the one who is notary by virtue of holding another office must recite that fact in his certificate; his certificate should state "ex officio notary public," and it must officially mention the other office. Obviously then, there will be a difference in the authentication requirements.

The authentication certificate, in those instances where it may be called for and given, should, like the notary's certificate, recite the ex officio status, where that status exists. Thus it should read somewhat as follows: "That is justice of the peace and notary public ex-officio, duly qualified and acting, in and for Precinct No. 1, Bricker county, State of Ohio," rather than: "That is a notary public, duly appointed, qualified, commissioned and acting in and for the county of Bricker, State of Ohio."

AUTHENTICATION 

245 

NOTARY DE FACTO; NOTARY DE JURE 

Q. How, if at all, may the official certificate of a notary de facto or a notary de jure be authenticated?

A. (1) A notary public is just a notary public; a notary public de facto is something else; a notary public de jure would be still another thing. A person who is a notary in a regular and legal way, properly commissioned and acting, need give himself or herself no concern about a "de facto" status or a "de jure" status. Since, however, there may be (and often has been) such an officer as a notary de facto, and since the appellate courts have many times considered and decided appealed lawsuits involving de facto status and de jure status, it seems not out of place to devote a little study to what the authorities have declared.

OREGON SUPREME Court 

(2) In the case of Hamlin v. Kassafer, 15 Pacific Reporter, page 778, the Supreme Court of Oregon considered and discussed the status of de facto and de jure officers. That case involved a justice of the peace, as a public officer, not a notary public, but the rules laid down are applicable alike to all public officers. In part the court said: "An office has been defined to be a right to exercise "a public function or employment, and to take the fees and emoluments belonging to it; and Chief Justice Marshall says: 'He who performs the duties of that office is an officer. From the inherent nature of an office, no less than from reasons of public policy, there cannot be two persons in the possession of an office at the same time. 

It becomes important, then, to observe the distinction between an officer de jure and an officer de facto. Lord 

Ellenborough said: "One who has the reputation of being the officer he assumed to be, and yet is not a good officer in point of law' is an officer de facto. To constitute a person an officer de facto, he must be in the actual possession of the office, and in the exercise of its functions, and in the discharge of its duties. When this is the fact necessarily there can be no other incumbent of the office. An officer de jure is one who has the lawful right to the office, but who has either been ousted from, or never actually taken possession of the office. When the officer de jure is also the officer de facto, the lawful title and possession is united; then no other person can be an officer de facto for that office.

An officer de facto is one who exercises the duties of an office under color of an appointment or election to that office. He differs, on the one hand, from a mere usurper of an office, who undertakes to act as an officer without any color of right; and on the other hand, from an officer de jure who is, in all respects, legally appointed and qualified to exercise the office. It is not, in all cases easy to determine what ought to be considered as constituting a colorable right to an office, so as to determine whether one is a mere usurper. The distinction, then, which the law recognizes, is that an officer de jure is one who has the lawful right of title, without the possession, of the office; while an officer de facto has the possession, and performs the duties under the color of right, without being actually qualified in law so to act, both being distinguished from the mere usurper, who has neither lawful title nor color of right.

The mere claim to be a public officer is not enough to constitute one an officer de facto. There must be some color to the claim of right to the office, or, without such color, a performance of official duties, with the acquiescence of the public, for such a length of time as 

LOUISIANA SUPREME COURT 

One who has been commissioned as notary, and has taken the oath of office, and has been acting as notary for many years, and has the reputation of being such in the community in which he lives, but has failed to file his oath of office in the offices of the Secretary of State, and of the Clerk of Court, and has also failed to renew his bond every five years, as required by law, is a notary "de facto"; and the acts passed before him have the same validity as the acts passed before a notary de jure. The position of a notary public is an "office," and, where a person holds a commission as a notary and acts as one and has the reputation of being a notary public, he is a "notary public, de facto". (Davenport v. Davenport, 41 

So. 240.) 

CORPUS JURIS (3) From that eminent authority, Corpus Juris, Vol. 46, pages 506-7, the following is quoted: "Generally a person acting as a notary under color of authority with public acquiescence is held to be a notary de facto, and as to the public and third persons his acts are valid and cannot be attacked collaterally.

AUTHENTICATION 

247 The principal that ineligibility to hold an office does. not prevent the ineligible incumbent, if in possession under color of right and authority, from being an officer de facto, with respect to his official acts, in so far as third persons are concerned, has been applied to one who is appointed and acts in good faith as notary, but who is ineligible or disqualified to act as such by reason of alienage, sex, or interest, or by acceptance of another office, even though his office as notary is thereby "vacated" under the statute, or by reason of being an officer or stockholder in a corporation in violation of a statute, or one whose commission is defective, or who is holding over after expiration of his term, or who has failed to file his bond, take the oath of office, or otherwise comply with directory provisions of the statute, but in general no one can be a notary de facto without color of authority. It is well settled that a mere usurper is not an officer de facto, and the position depends upon a continuing exercise of the office, as single official act not being enough."

AUTHENTICATION NOT EFFECTIVE, WHEN 

(4) An official certificate of an authenticating authority, vouching for the authenticity of an official certificate of a notary de facto (or a notary de jure, if there is such a person) would be inaccurate if it did not on its face disclose the exact status- -de facto or de jure—and if it made such disclosure, it would probably not be accepted. This writer, therefore gives it merely as his own opinion that the official certificate of a notary de facto or of a notary de jure, cannot be satisfactorily authenticated to any jurisdiction outside his own. 

WHAT LAW GOVERNS 

Q. What law governs in authentication of a notary's official certificate?

A. The law of the state (jurisdiction) where the certificate is to be used. In the case of Simpson v. Wicker, 47 S.E. Reporter, page 965, the Supreme Court of Georgia rendered an opinion dealing with this general subject, a part of that opinion being as follows:

GEORGIA SUPREME COURT 

"Under the requirements of Civ. Code No. 5614, the clerk of this court has raised the question as to whether an affidavit in forma pauperis made in a foreign state

before a notary public is sufficiently authenticated by that officer's seal, where there is nothing further to show that he has statutory authority to administer oaths. 

The matter is of practical importance, inasmuch as it involves the admissibility of such affidavits on applications for injunction and in other legal proceedings. Nor is it free from difficulty, for there is great conflict in the decisions as to whether or not a notary public has the inherent power to administer an oath, or to attest affidavits, in any matter not connected with the protest of commercial paper. The question has never been directly and authoritatively passed upon in this state. In 

Charles v. Foster, 56 Ga. 612, it was decided that there was no presumption that he had any authority to attest a claim affidavit and approve a claim bond; this being out of the sphere of commerce, and involving the power of a magistrate or justice of the peace, his seal was no proof that he had such jurisdiction. In Brunswick 

Hardware Co. v. Bingham, 107 Ga. 270, 33 S.E. 56, there was no authentication, other than his own signature, that the person attesting the affidavit was a notary, it appearing both from the published opinion and the original record that no seal was attached. Outside of 

Georgia the decisions are directly in conflict on the subject. The courts of Alabama, Illinois, Michigan,

Colorado and Indiana hold that affidavits before a foreign notary are inadmissible when authenticated solely by his seal of office. Some of these decisions are based, at least in part, on the language of the statute defining the terms upon which foreign affidavits may be received in legal proceedings. In England, Maryland, District of Columbia, New York and Minnesota, the courts receive such affidavits when authenticated by the notary's seal. The Supreme Court of Minnesota, in 

Wood v. St. Paul, etc. Co. (Minn.) 44 N.W. 308, delivered a vigorous opinion to the effect that such affidavits were admissible when attented by the seal, and that, whether the power of notaries to administer oaths and attest affidavits was of statutory or customary origin, it was universal, and that, for a time beyond living memory, affidavits made before foreign notaries, when attested under their seals, had been received in the courts of England. Walrond v. Van Moses 8 Mod. 

323, was decided in 1722, before our adopting statute.

There, on an application to change bail, the court held

AUTHENTICATION 

249 that "a plaintiff who was in Holland might make an affidavit there and get it attested by a public notary, and should be admitted in evidence to hold the defendant to special bail here. In Tucker v. Ladd, 4 Cow. 47, an affidavit taken before a notary in New Hampshire was allowed, over objections, to be read in a hearing in New York. 21 Am. & Eng. Enc. L.565. At one time there appears to have been doubt as to the power of notaries to attest affidavits in this state. It was removed by the act of 1863. This statute, however, was probably in part declaratory of a power already recognized. Prior to that date no act had been passed defining their duties, or authorizing notaries to administer any sort of an oath. When they were elected by the 

General Assembly, or by the courts, nothing was said as to their powers or duties. They were referred to as officers whose functions were well known, among which was the power to administer oaths. For the fee bill of 1792 allows them to charge for every protest and oath included, $2.00, and for administering an oath in any other case, 25c. "From the act of 1792, therefore, it seems evident that the legislature assumed that notaries had the power to administer oaths by virtue of their office. It was a power not then granted, but recognized as inherent in notaries the world over. That such was the practice appears from Solomon v. Lacey, Dudley, 82, where the bare certificate of a notary public of New Jersey to the oath was received here in 1831. And therefore, whatever the conflict elsewhere, there was fair room to contend that in this state there was a legislative recognition that they had the power by virtue of their appointment. 

If so, it was not necessary to produce a foreign statute to show that they could do what was here recognized as a power inherent in the office. Still the doubt continued, and in 1870 the legislature passed an act authorizing pleas, answers, and defenses to be sworn to before certain officers, including notaries public; and, as the greater includes the less, it was no doubt argued that if they had the right to make such answer, which could be used as evidence on hearings for injunction, parties could likewise use, in the same case, affidavits attested by the same notary. This view, however, was not adopted in Brunswick Hardware Co. v. Bingham, 107 

Ga. 273, 33 S.E. 56. Then came the act of 1899, by

which the General Assembly expressly declared that any affidavit made out of the state of Georgia before any notary public, justice, judge, chancellor, or commissioner of any court of equity of the state or county where the oath is made, or before any other officer of said state or county who is authorized by the laws thereof to administer oaths, shall have the same force and effect, and be recognized in like manner, as if it had been made before any officer of this state authorized to administer the same. The proviso thereto did not make the act self-destructive and repeal it at the moment of its approval. The proviso was to the effect that the act should not apply to affidavits required to be made within the state of Georgia, nor should it impair or render invalid any of the existing provisions of law for making affidavits out of this state. "From the legislation generally, from this supposed customary power, and from the language of the act of 1899, it is clear that foreign notaries are authorized to attest affidavits, and that the same may be received in legal proceedings here with the same effect and under the same circumstances as if made in Georgia. The act of 1899 is, however, silent on the subject of what shall be sufficient evidence of their official character and power to administer oaths. It does not say whether a statement to that effect in the jurat will be sufficient, nor whether there must be attached a certificate from the secretary of state, or of the court making the appointment, or a copy of the statute conferring the power. It is evident that, so far as notaries are concerned, the silence in the statute must mean that the usual authentication by his seal of office is sufficient. Otherwise, who can say what else is necessary, or how it is to appear that he is what he claims to be, or has the authority to do what he actually does. The decision in Shockley v. Turnell, 114 Ga. 378, 40 S.E.279, related to an affidavit before a clerk of a county court, with nothing to show that he was a clerk. He was not one of the officers named in the act of 1899, and there was no evidence that he had authority under the laws of Texas to administer oaths. We conclude that in the present case the affidavit in forma pauperis, attested as it was by a notary under his seal, is properly receivable, and that the case should not be dismissed for failure to pay the cost."

AUTHENTICATION 

251 PROCEDURE

Q. By what procedure is a notary's official certificate ordinarily authenticated for use in another state?

A. (1) In some of the states we find statutes specifically authorizing a certain officer or officers to authenticate a notary's certificate for use in a foreign jurisdiction. Where such provision is made, then the authenticating must be done by the officer named in the statute, and no other. (2) In some of the states certain requisites are prescribed for such authentication, and where such requisites are prescribed the certificate is insufficient unless they are complied with. Thus, in one state where a statute required that the authentication certificate must state that the certifying officer is familiar with the handwriting of the notary whose certificate is being authenticated, then the alleged authentication was insufficient where the authenticating officer failed to state that he was familiar with the notary's handwriting. 

A notary or notary's attorney having a problem of authentication should bear in mind the requisites imposed in the particular jurisdiction and should see that same are fully complied with, in harmony with the procedure outlined in paragraph (5) following. (3) Again, the certificate of authentication in some instances is required merely to state that the notary who certified the execution of the written instrument was at the time of such certification a qualified and acting notary public. In other instances the certificate of authentication must state that the acknowledgment was taken in conformity to the laws of the jurisdiction where taken. (4) In those states where there is no statute specifically stating who shall authenticate the notary's certificate and no form or requisites are prescribed therefor, then the notary who wishes to have his official certificate authenticated should, if convenient, apply to the appointing authority for a certificate of authentication, or apply to the judge or clerk of a court of record having a seal. (5) Procedure followed in authenticating a notary's official work is that the authenticating officer gives his own stateinent or declaration over his own official certificate and seal, to the effect that the notary is a notary public-duly appointed, or ex officio, qualified, commissioned and actingwithin the specified jurisdiction; that as such officer he, the said notary public, has authority within that jurisdiction to

take and certify such acknowledgment or administer and certify such affidavit; that the purported signature of the said notary is true and genuine; that the said acknowledgment has been taken and certified, or that the said affidavit has been administered and certified according to the laws of that jurisdiction, or county or district or state as the case may be, and is entitled to full faith and credit. The said authenticating officer's certificate is then attached to the notary's official certificate, which is of course by now a part of the original signed paper or document, and thus the original document is authenticated by the notary's certificate and the notary's certificate is authenticated by the higher officer's certificate.

THE STATES 

Alabama

Authentication of notarial certificate is not required if the acknowledgment is taken outside the state of Alabama. If authentication is desired for acknowledgment taken in Alabama, it may be given by clerk of the circuit court in the county where the acknowledgment is taken. Fee 50 cents. Alaska

A notarial certificate given outside the Territory for use inside the Territory, requires no authentication. If it is desired to authenticate for use outside the Territory, a certificate given within the Territory, such authentication may be given by the clerk of the district court of the judicial division, for which a fee of 50 cents may be charged. Arizona

Authentication not necessary to acknowledgment taken within the state.

If acknowledgment taken outside the state but within United States, its insular possessions or territories, by a notary public, must be authenticated by a certificate by a clerk of a court of record of the county, parish, or district where acknowledgment taken. 

If acknowledgment taken outside the United States by a notary public, the notary's certificate must be authenticated by a certificate, under the great seal of the state or country, affixed by the custodian thereof, or by a certificate of a diplomatic, consular or commercial officer of the United States accredited to that country certifying as to the official character of the acknowledging officer.

253 Authentication of notary's official character for use outside the state may be certified by clerk of superior court in county for which notary appointed. Usual fee 25 cents. Arkansas

Authentication not necessary to certificate if acknowledgment taken by notary within state.

If acknowledgment taken by a notary public outside the state but within the United States, its insular possessions or territories, must be authenticated by a clerk of a court of record of the county, parish or district in which the acknowledgment is taken. 

If acknowledgment is taken by a notary public outside the United States, or possessions, the notary's ceritficate must be authenticated by a certificate under the great seal of the state or country, affixed by the custodian of such seal, certifying to the official character of such notary public. 

Authentication of the notary's official character for use outside the state may be given by circuit clerk of county in which notary's commission is recorded. Usual fee is 50 cents. California

A California notary's official certificate, when intended for use in other states, may be authenticated by the county clerk of the county where such certificate is given. An acknowledgment taken outside of California, for use in California, is sufficient if taken in compliance with laws of the place where taken, but certificate of the fact of such compliance is necessary. Such certificate may be given by the clerk of a court of record, where the notary resides, which certificate must show that the notary was lawfully authorized to give such certificate; that the signature of the notary to the certificate is true and genuine and that the acknowledgment was taken in accordance with the laws of the place where taken. 

Fee 25 cents. 

Canal Zone

Section 1358, Title 48 of United State Code Annotated is as follows: "Deeds and other instruments affecting land situated in the District of Columbia or any Territory of the United States may be acknowledged in the Canal Zone before any notary public or judge, appointed therein by proper authority, or by any officer therein who has ex officio the powers of a notary public: Provided, That the certificate by such notary in the Canal Zone shall be accompanied by the certificate of the governor or acting governor of such place to the effect that the notary taking such acknowledg-

ment was in fact the officer he purported to be; and any deeds of other instruments affecting lands so situated, so acknowledged since the 1st day of January, 1905, and accompanied by such certificate shall have the same effect as such deeds or other instruments thereafter so acknowledged and certified. 

An instrument in writing officially certified by a notary within the Canal Zone for use outside the Zone should have attached a certificate of authentication by the Executive Secretary of the Zone. A notarial certificate given outside the Zone for use within the Zone should bear a certificate of authentication according to the jurisdiction within which it is given. This may be by the clerk of a court of record, anywhere within the United States, reciting that the notary is such officer as his official certificate purports him to be, that he is authorized by law to give such certificate, that his official signature is genuine and that his certificate is in compliance with laws of that jurisdiction. No fees are stipulated. Colorado

Notarial certificates for use within the state do not require authentication. Notarial certificates given in Colorado for use in other states may be authenticated by the clerk of the county court of the county where the notary is commissioned. Fee 25 cents. Connecticut

Authentication of notary's certificate made in state for use in state not necessary.

Authentication of a certificate made outside the state for use in the state is advisable but not mandatory. 

Notary's certificate may be authenticated by clerk of superior court in county where notary resides, when desired for use outside state. Fee, $1.00. Delaware

Authentication of certificate of officer taking acknowledgments outside state, for use in state not required.

Authentication of certificate of notary to acknowledgment taken within state for use outside state is by prothonotary. Fee, 50 cents. 

District of Columbia

No authentication necessary to notary's certificate taken within the United States, if under notary's official seal. If notary has

Authentication is necessary to any notarial certificate made outside the United States.

Certificates of acknowledgment taken in the District for use elsewhere may be authenticated by the Clerk of the District Court. 

Fee, 50 cents. Florida

No authentication necessary for certificates of acknowledgment taken outside the state or in foreign countries, for use within the state, provided the certificate is under the notary's official seal and signature.

Certificates for use outside the state may be authenticated by clerk of circuit court of county where notary's commission is recorded. Usual fee, 50 cents. Georgia

Notarial certificates to acknowledgments taken outside the state require no authentication for use within the state, if under notarial seal. If not under seal, must be authenticated by clerk of a court of record in county of residence of notary. 

If certificate is for use outside the state, the certificate of a county notary may be authenticated by the clerk of the superior court in the county of residence; certificate of a notary appointed for the state at large, authenticated by the State Librarian. Fee, $1.00. Guam

Section 1432, Title 48, U.S.C.A. is as follows:

Hawaii

A notary's certificate, given within Hawaii for use outside, may be authenticated by the certificate of the clerk of the circuit court of the judicial circuit within which he is duly commissioned and acting. Also apparently such certificate may be given by the 

Hawaiian attorney general. A notary's certificate given within the Territory for use therein, does not require authentication. 

A notary's certificate, given outside the Territory for use within, must have attached a certificate of the secretary of state of the State or Territory, or of a clerk of a court of record having a seal, within the district where the certificate was given, to the effect that such notary was then duly authorized to take and certify such acknowledgment, or do such other official notarial act as is being certified; that the authenticating officer knows the said notary's handwriting and believes that the signature affixed thereto is genuine. Attention is directed to the Federal statute quoted under Canal Zone. For the authentication certificate a fee of 25c is stipulated. Idaho

Notarial certificate for use within the state, whether taken in the state or not, requires no authentication. If taken within the state, for use outside, and authentication is desired, it may be given by district clerk, who in turn may, if required, be authenticated by the district judge. Fee 50 cents. Illinois

Acknowledgments certified by notary outside the state for use within the state, are acceptable if certified under notarial seal; such acknowledgments are also acceptable if taken in conformity with the laws of the place where taken and that fact certified by a clerk or court of record of such place, whose certificate shall be signed by him and be under seal of his court. 

Notarial certificates taken within the state for use outside the state may be authenticated either by the county clerk in county of notary's residence, under his hand and seal, or by the Secretary of State, under the great seal of the State. Fee, 25 cents. Indiana

Acknowledgments taken in state are acceptable in any county in state if under notary's seal. If not under seal and to be filed in county other than that of notary's residence, the acknowledgment must be certified by clerk of circuit court of county where such notary resides, and attested by the seal of said court.

Clerk of court authenticates notary's certificates given inside the state for use outside the state. Notarial certificates made outside state for use in state must be authenticated. New York 

Authentication requirements are quite specific and lengthy.

Only those provisions relating to notaries public are treated here. 

Notary's official certificates given within the state are acceptable and entitled to record, without authentication, only in the county for which the notary is appointed. Provided however that in any county where there is a register of deeds office, such register shall not record or accept for record any deed or other instrument proved or acknowledged in the county for which he is registered, before any notary public, unless such notary public shall have filed in such registers office his autograph signature and a certificate from the county clerk of the county in which such notary public was originally appointed or a certificate from the county clerk of such registers county showing the due qualification of such notary public in such county clerk's office; or unless such acknowledgment or proof shall have been duly authenticated by the county clerk of the county in which such register holds office.

Notarial certificates for use in other counties within the state require authentication by the county clerk of the county of notary's authority.

Notarial certificates made outside the state but within the United States, its territories, possessions, sovereignties, or protectorates are not entitled to record or admission within the state unless authenticated by: a. Certificate of clerk, or other certifying officer, of a court of record in the district in which such acknowledgment was taken, under seal of such court, or b. Certificate of the clerk, register, recorder or other recording officer in the district in which such acknowledgment was made, or c. The certificate of the officer having charge of the official records of the appointment of such notary, or having a record of the signature of such notary.

Notarial certificates made in a foreign country are not entitled to record or admission unless authenticated by an officer designated in (a), (b) or (c) above, or by a consular officer of the 

United States resident in such country.

Certificate of notary's authority may be obtained from county clerk in county of notary's residence. Fee, 25 cents.

A New York officer authenticating a notary's certificate of acknowledgment or proof must subjoin or attach to the original certificate, a certificate under his hand. Such certificate of authentication must specify: a. That at the time when such original certificate purports to have been made, the officer making it was in fact such officer as he purports to be; b. That the authenticating officer: 

1. Is acquainted with the handwriting of the officer making the original certificate, or 2. Has compared the signature of such officer upon the original certificate with a specimen of his signature filed or deposited in the office of such authenticating officer, or recorded, filed or deposited, pursuant to law in any other place. c. That he believes the signature of such officer upon the original certificate is genuine; and if the original certificate is required to be under seal, such authenticating officer must also certify; d. That he has compared the impression of the seal affixed thereto with a specimen impression thereof filed or deposited in his office, or recorded, filed or deposited according to law, in any other place, and e. That he believes the impression of the seal upon the original certificate is genuine.

Notarial certificates made outside state for use in state are sufficient if under notary's official seal. If not under seal must be authenticated by the clerk of court of record in the state or jurisdiction of notary's residence.

Chapter 15 Scope. In this chapter is intended to be shown a form for every type of official certificate which a notary in any American jurisdiction may expect to be called upon to give. For reference to text in preceding pages, attention is especially directed to Chapters 3 and 4.

Identity of Person.-Whether the certificate be for an acknowledgment, affidavit or other instrument, the notary should in every instance make certain of the identity of the person who asks for such certificate.

Personal Appearance.-If the certificate recites that the one giving the instrument made personal appearance before him, then he should satisfy himself that such person and not some other, is appearing before him.

Spelling of Name, Blanks Filled.-It is not inadvisable for the notary to glance at the instrument, to see that names are correctly spelled, dates correctly stated, blanks filled or checked. 

Print or Stamp Name.-The name of the notary should be printed or stamped along with his signature in his own handwriting. In many jurisdictions this is a requirement of law; in others, not. It is not perceived that printing or stamping the name even if not required, can possibly do any harm, and it tends to over the handicap of illegible handwritings.

Commission Expiration Date.-Date of expiration of the no tary's commission should be shown. In many of the states, this is required by law; in others, not. It is not perceived that the addition of this notation can possibly do any harm to any certificate. 

In the certificates shown in following pages, where the date of expiration of notary's commission is shown, it is required by the law of that jurisdiction.

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS 

Many of the States, Arizona, Michigan, New Mexico, Massachusetts, Iowa, Oregon, Tennessee, others, have adopted in whole or in part the Uniform Acknowledgment Act. It is conceived that some confusion may have resulted from optional adoption of this Act in some jurisdictions, and of certain of its parts only in others, while in yet others, the use of either the Uniform forms, or of the State's own forms, is permissible and correct. The Act

from the Arizona statutes, (Laws of 1943, Chap. 8, page 183;

Cumulative Pocket Supplement, Arizona Code, 1939, Vol. 5, page 

239) is as follows:

UNIFORM ACKNOWLEDGMENT ACT 

"71-501. This Act may be cited as the Uniform Acknowledgment Act. "71-502. Any instrument may be acknowledged, either in the manner and form otherwise provided by law, or as provided in this Act. "71-503. The acknowledgment of any instrument may be made in this state before: 1. a judge of a court of record; 2. a clerk or deputy clerk of a court having a seal; 3. a commissioner or recorder; 4. a notary public; 5. a justice of the peace, or, 6. a master in chancery. "71-504. The acknowledgment of any instrument may be made without the state but within the United States, a territory or insular possession of the United States, the District of Columbia or the Philippine Islands, and within the jurisdiction of the officer, before: 1. a clerk or deputy clerk of any federal court; 2. a clerk or deputy clerk of any court of record of any state or other jurisdiction; 3. a notary public, or, 4. a commissioner of deeds. "71-505. The acknowledgment of any instrument may be made without the United States before: 1. an ambassador, minister, charge-d-affaires, counselor to or secretary of legation, consulgeneral, consul, vice-consul, commercial attache, or consular agent of the United States accredited to the country where the acknowledgment is made; 2. a notary public of the country where the acknowledgment is made, or 3. a judge or clerk of a court of record of the country where the acknowledgment is made. "71-506. The officer taking the acknowledgment shall know or have satisfactory evidence that the person making the acknowledgment is the person described in and who executed the instrument. "71-507. An acknowledgment of a married woman may be made in the same form as though she were unmarried. "71-508. The officer taking the acknowledgment shall endorse thereon or attach thereto a certificate substantially in one of the following forms:

(NOTE: The foregoing copies of statutory forms do not show date of expiration of notary's commission. In use of the forms, this should be shown. See 71-509) "71-509. The certificate of acknowledging officer shall be completed by his signature, his official seal, if any, the title of his office and if he is a notary public, the date his commission expires. "71-510. (a). If the acknowledgment is taken within this

State, or is made without the United States by an officer of the 

United States no authentication is necessary. "(b) If the acknowledgment is taken without this State, but in the United States, a territory or insular possessoin of the United States, the District of Columbia or the Philippine Islands, the certificate shall be authenticated by a certificate as to the official character of such officer, executed, if the acknowledgment is taken by the clerk or deputy clerk of a court; by the presiding judge of the court, or, if the acknowledgment is taken by a notary public, by a clerk of a court of record of the county, parish or district in which the acknowledgment is taken. "(c) If the acknowledgment is made without the United States and by a notary public or a judge or clerk of a court of record of the county where the acknowledgment is made, the certificate shall be authenticated by a certificate under the great seal of the country, affixed by the custodian thereof, or by a certificate of a diplomatic, consular or commercial officer of the United States accredited to that country, certifying as to the official character of such officer."

271 "71. Notwithstanding any provision of this act, the acknowledgments of any instrument without this State in compliance with the manner and form prescribed by the laws of the place of its execution, if in a state, a territory, or insular possession of the 

United States, the District of Columbia, or the Philippine Islands, verified by the official seal of the officer before whom it is acknowledged and authenticated in the manner provided by subsection (b) of section 10, shall have the same effect as an acknowledgment in the manner and form prescribed by the laws of this state for instruments executed within the state." (Official Signature)  

Proof of execution of a written instrument may be made by a subscribing witness before a notary public (or any other duly authorized officer). The notary (or other officer) must have satisfactory proof of the identity of such witness, or must know him personally. The proof must state on the oath of the witness that he (witness) knew the person described in and who executed the instrument and must state the name and place of residence of the witness. No statutory proof form is prescribed for Alaska, and any form in use in other jurisdictions may be used, provided it embodies the foregoing essentials. The following form may be used: 

Territory of Alaska

On this................day of..., 19.------ .a notary public in and for known to

Given under my hand and seal of office at.. ...........this ..........day of………..-----------, 19…--------

NOTE-In event one subscribing witness does not appear, the notary's certificate should recite that only one of said witnesses so appeared, and should show by such witness proof of the execution and acknowledgment of the instrument by the grantor named therein, in the presence of both named witnesses, and its signing

by them as such witnesses at the grantors request. If both witnesses named on the instrument are dead, or one is dead and the other not in Kentucky, the notary's certificate should recite that two witnesses, giving names and addresses, on oath before him. proved these facts and also proved the signatures of the grantor or grantors and at least one of the subscribing witnesses.

Louisiana

The state has adopted the Uniform Acknowledgment Act. 

In Louisiana every acknowledgment certificate must have two witnesses, of whom the notary may not be one. There are no counties, but parishes. The signer of the written instrument, the acknowledger, also signs the acknowledgment certificate. 

INDIVIDUAL 

A form in common use is as follows: 

State of Louisiana 

Parish of the month of.. 

Be it known that on this.................day of ..., 19........, before me the undersigned, a

to me notary public, personally came and appeared...... personally known and known by me to be the person whose genuine signature is affixed to the foregoing document, who signed said document before me and in the presence of the two witnesses whose names are thereto subscribed as such, being competent witnesses, and who acknowledged in my presence and in the presence of said witnesses, that he signed the above and foregoing document as his own free act and deed and for the uses and purposes therein set forth and apparent.

In witness whereof, the said appearer has signed these presents and I have hereunto affixed by hand and seal, together with the said witnesses on the day and date first above written. (SEAL) Witnesses 

(Official Signature) 

(It is permissible to reform the above so as to fit corporation, partnership, agent or attorney or other acknowledgment needs.) 

For execution outside the state of Louisiana, the caption may read:

 Pennsylvania

NOTE-Pennsylvania has adopted the Uniform Acknowledgment Act. Provision is made for separate examination of married women where right of dower or conveyance of their real estate is involved, but their other acknowledgments may be taken as of single persons. Deeds or other instruments not acknowledged, may be proved for record by two eye witnesses before notary or other officer authorized to take acknowledgments. If eye witnesses not available, proof may be made by handwriting by two persons, competent witnesses. In addition to the Uniform forms, use may be made of the forms following.

Puerto Rico

NOTE-The acknowledger must either be known to the notary or by proven under oath, to the notary's satisfaction. If the certificate does not state that the acknowledger is personally known to the notary, then the signature should be witnessed by at least two competent witnesses. The form is as follows: 

Subscribed before me by (give the name, age, trade or profession or occupation, whether single or married, and place of residence) personally known to me (or who has been identified to me by the two witnesses known to me, whose statement to that effect is also signed by them) this….......………….day of……………………………….. 19......... 

If proper, precede the word "subscribed" by the words "sworn to and."

PROOF BY HANDWRITING 

Note The form just shown is statutory, being Article 6611, Vernon's Sayles Statutes. Articles 6612-18 provide for proof of a deed or other instrument by proof of the handwriting of the signer of such instrument. Such proof may be used: 1. When the grantor and all subscribing witnesses are dead. 2. When the grantor and all the subscribing witnesses are non-residents of the State. 3. When the place of their residence is unknown to the party desiring the proof, and can not be ascertained. 4. When the subscribing witnesses have been convicted of felony, or have become of unsound mind, or have otherwise become incompetent to testify. 5. When all the subscribing witnesses are dead, or are nonresidents of the State or when their residence is unknown, or when their residence is unknown, or when they are incompetent to testify, and the grantor in such instrument refuses to acknowledge

the execution of the same for record. The evidence at hand must satisfactorily prove to the notary or other officer: 1. The existence of one or more of the conditions just mentioned. 2. That the witness testifying knew the person whose name purports to be subscribed to the instrument as a party, and is well acquainted with his signature, and that it is genuine. 3. That the witness testifying personally knew the person who subscribed the instrument as a witness, and is well acquainted with his signature and that it is genuine. 4. The place of residence of the witness testifying. 

There are other provisions, such as the authority of the officer to subpoena the witness to testify etc. A certificate form for such proof may be as follows:

County of

AFFIDAVIT CERTIFICATE OR JURAT 

(1). An affidavit certificate form, or jurat, is one thing; an affidavit form is another thing. The jurat and the affidavit differ as completely in form and purpose as do a deed and the acknowledgment certificate. (2). In many of the states affidavit forms are prescribed by statute for purposes such as procurement of a marriage license, registration of a voter, recording of a lien, but a statutory form for a prescribed statutory purpose can not very well be used, or re-made, to fit any other purpose. This writer has not found a statute in any state which attempts to outline a form for every kind of affidavit. Use of statutory affidavits in court to obtain a continuance of a case, to obtain appointment or dismissal of a receiver or other like purposes is common and general, but no reason is seen for reproducing such forms here, since they are for the use of the lawyer, not the notary. (3). Shown here is what is called a general affidavit form, and such a form may be easily used for almost any conceivable affidavit purpose, but this is not a statutory form. There are numerous statutory affidavit certificate forms, and they differ but little in their outline, as will be observed by study of those following. 

State of.. 

County of...

GENERAL AFFIDAVIT 

Before me, the undersigned, a notary public in and for..... county, State of.............., on this day personally appeared. known to me and known by me to be the person whose name is signed to this affidavit, and who, being by me duly sworn (or affirmed), on his oath (or affirmation) deposed and said: 

My name is.. 

County of... 

I live at....... 

State of... post office address is........ 

City of and my 

I am... -years of age, etc. (Here set out in full and accurate detail all the facts to which the affiant wishes to make oath.) 

I swear to the foregoing statement on my own personal knowledge, this affidavit being made in the interest of truth and for purpose of... the (Affiant's Signature)

Subscribed by... .before me at...... state of this............day of…………………………………….., 19........, as witness my hand and seal of office (if seal necessary). 

 (Official Signature)

NOTE-AS is explained elsewhere in these pages, and to be kept constantly in mind by a notary when affixing his official certificate, a notary's official signature includes his name, the title of his office and his jurisdiction. In some of the states it is further required that the official signature shall include the postoffice address, and of course, always the seal, if he has a seal.

NOTE-In many jurisdictions the term "jurat" is used exclusively to designate an officer's official signature to an affidavit.

HEIRSHIP 

NOTE-An affidavit of heirship is seldom of any value unless it is full and comprehensive. In writing one, bear in mind and include these things: Names, dates, places; births, marriages, deaths. 

The State of Iowa 

County of Nelson 

Before me, the undersigned authority, on this day personally appeared John Doe, known to me to be a credible person, who being by me duly sworn, on his oath deposed and said: 

My name is John Doe. I live in Nelson County, Iowa, on a farm about six miles Southwest of the town of Nelsonville. I am 

62 years of age; was born and raised in Nelson County and have lived here all my life.

I knew Richard Roe, who formerly lived in Nelson County, Iowa. He is now dead. He died on the 5th day of November, 1930, and was buried in Glenwood Cemetery at the town of Nelsonville, his last resting place being marked by a stone monument which I have seen though I do not at this moment recall the words of the inscription on said monument. The said Richard 

Roe was my first cousin. His mother, Mary Roe, was a sister of my father Robert Doe, prior to her marriage. I first knew Richard Roe when he moved into Nelson County in 1887 from North Carolina. He had not been married prior to that time. In 1890 

Richard Roe married Sarah Jones at San Angelo, Texas, and brought his wife back with him to Nelson County to make their home. There was one child born of this marriage, namely: Alice

Jane Roe. The said Alice Jane Roe was born on the 4th day of

July, 1891. I am certain of this date. Sarah Jones Roe, wife of 

Richard Roe, departed this life in Nelson County, Iowa, in the Fall or Winter of 1891. The said child, Alice Jane, was her only child. In the summer of 1892, Richard Roe married Stella Robertson, who is still living. Of the marriage between Richard Roe and Stella Roe, his second wife, three children and only three were born, to-wit: John Henry, oldest boy, born, 1893; Rebecca, a daughter, born in 1895; Thomas, born in 1897. I do not at this moment recall the birth dates of these three persons, though I am certain of the years in which each birth occurred. Neither of the four children of Richard Roe ever married and all four of them now live in Nelson County, Iowa. 

Richard Roe and his second wife, Stella Robertson Roe, never had any other children than these three and never adopted a child. Stella Robertson Roe was never married to any person except Richard Roe and Richard Roe was never married to any person except the two women whom I have named. 

When the said Richard Roe died he left no will and there was no administration upon his estate and no necessity for any administration, as the estate owed no debts. When Sarah Jones Roe, his first wife, died in 1891, she left no will and there was no administration upon her estate. The homestead occupied by said 

Richard Roe was bought and paid for by him on or about the 

10th day of January, 1891, after he married his first wife, Sarah 

Jones, and before her death.

The said homestead consisted of 160 acres of land and so far as I know, the said Richard Roe never thereafter acquired any other lands.

The purpose of this affidavit is to furnish information respecting the title and ownership of the said Richard Roe's homestead, and I have no personal interest whatsoever in the matter. 

I swear to the foregoing statement. 

JOHN DOE Affiant (Add affidavit certificate and acknowledgment certificate.)

Herein fail not, but have you then and there before me at my said office this writ, with your return thereon, showing how you have executed the same. 

Witness my hand and official seal at Alamo, Texas, the...... day of................. A. D. 19......... (Seal) 

JOHN DOE 

Notary Public, Bexar County, Texas

FEE SIMPLE DEED

This deed, made this........... by me, ..of.. ..day of………………………………………..in the year.... 

Witnesseth, that in consideration of (here state the consideration), I the said....... do grant unto (state grantee's name), of.......----------9 all that (here describe the property sold.) 

Witness my hand and seal. (Acknowledgment) (Seal) 

Note: The District statutes do not prescribe a warranty deed form, but effect is given to the use of certain phrases such as: "that he will warrant generally the property hereby conveyed."

After the words of conveyance, the words "with general warranty" may be added, or if that is the purpose, "with special warranty." Signature of a witness is not required, but commonly the notary not only certifies the acknowledgment but also signs as a witness.
